On the Distribution of 2 2 -Selmer ranks of Quadratic Twists of Elliptic Curves over ℚ {\mathbb{Q}}
Jinzhao Pan, Ye Tian

Abstract
. We characterize the distribution of 2 2 -Selmer ranks of quadratic twists of elliptic curves over ℚ {\mathbb{Q}} with full rational 2 2 -torsion. We propose a new type of random alternating matrix model M ∗ , 𝐭 Alt ​ ( 𝔽 2 ) M_{*,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}) over 𝔽 2 {\mathbb{F}}_{2} with 0 0 , 1 1 or 2 2 “holes”, with associated Markov chains, described by parameter 𝐭 = ( t 1 , ⋯ , t s ) ∈ ℤ s \mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s} where s s is the number of “holes”. We proved that for each equivalence classes of quadratic twists of elliptic curves: (1) The distribution of 2 2 -Selmer ranks agrees with the distribution of coranks of matrices in M ∗ , 𝐭 Alt ​ ( 𝔽 2 ) M_{*,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}) ; (2) The moments of 2 2 -Selmer groups agree with that of M ∗ , 𝐭 Alt ​ ( 𝔽 2 ) M_{*,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}) , in particular, the average order of essential 2 2 -Selmer groups is 3 + ∑ i 2 t i 3+\sum_{i}2^{t_{i}} . Our work extends the works of Heath-Brown, Swinnerton-Dyer, Kane, and Klagsbrun-Mazur-Rubin where the matrix only has 0 0 “holes”, the matrix model is the usual random alternating matrix model, and the average order of essential 2 2 -Selmer groups is 3 3 . A new phenomenon is that different equivalence classes in the same quadratic twist family could have different parameters, hence have different distribution of 2 2 -Selmer ranks. The irreducible property of the Markov chain associated to M ∗ , 𝐭 Alt ​ ( 𝔽 2 ) M_{*,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}) gives the positive density results on the distribution of 2 2 -Selmer ranks.



1.
Introduction

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a quadratic twist family of elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
There are two fundamental conjectures on it:

•

Goldfeld (
[
Gol79
]
, Conjecture B)
conjectured that the density of
E\in{\mathcal{E}}
such that

\ord_{s=1}L(E,s)=r
is
1/2
if
r=0,1
.

•

Kolyvagin (
[
Kol90
]
, Conjecture F)
has a heuristic that for any prime
p
and
r=0,1
,
there is an elliptic curve
E
in
{\mathcal{E}}
such that

\ord_{s=1}L(E,s)=r
and
the analytic Sha of
E
is a
p
-adic unit.

To study the
L
-values and Mordell-Weil groups for elliptic curves,
one may start with their Selmer groups.
The first significant result on the distribution of Selmer groups
in quadratic twist families of elliptic curves is by
Swinnerton-Dyer
[
SD08
]
and Kane
[
Kane13
]
.
They studied the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups for
families
{\mathcal{E}}

with full rational
2
-torsion points and satisfying certain additional conditions.
For simplicity let’s call them of type (A):

(A)

families
{\mathcal{E}}

with full rational
2
-torsion points
such that
E\in{\mathcal{E}}
does not have a rational cyclic
4
-isogeny, for example, the congruent number elliptic curves
ny^{2}=x^{3}-x
.

Their works are inspired by the pioneering work of
Heath-Brown
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]

on the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups
for the family
ny^{2}=x^{3}-x
.

Meanwhile, Poonen and Rains
[
PR12
]
,
Bhargava, Kane, Lenstra, Poonen and Rains
[
BKLPR15
]

proposed that
the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups for
all elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
ordered by height
should follow the model of random alternating matrices over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.
More precisely, let
M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
be the set of alternating matrices over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

of size
(2k+r)\times(2k+r)
, endowed with counting measure,
then the model defines the probability

P_{r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d):=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B)=d\mid B\in M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\big)

for
d\geq 0
and
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The work of Heath-Brown, Swinnerton-Dyer and Kane
indicates that the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups
in type (A) quadratic twist families coincides with the general
2
-Selmer model.

A crucial feature of the matrix model
M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

is that there is a Markov chain attached to it,
which was a key ingredient in
[
SD08
]
.
More precisely, let
X_{k}
be the random variable
\corank(B)
as

B\in M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
,
then the sequence
(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}
form a Markov chain
with transition probability

{\mathbb{P}}\big(X_{k+1}=m_{\mathrm{new}}\mid X_{k}=m\big)=\begin{cases}2^{-1-2m},&\text{if }m_{\mathrm{new}}=m+2,\\
2^{-m}(3-5\cdot 2^{-1-m}),&\text{if }m_{\mathrm{new}}=m,\\
(1-2^{-m})(1-2^{1-m}),&\text{if }m_{\mathrm{new}}=m-2,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Swinnerton-Dyer
[
SD08
]
analyzed the behavior of
2
-Selmer groups of
E^{(n)}

for type (A) curves
when the number
k
of prime factors of
n
increases.
It is described by an “almost Markov” process whose transition probability
differs from the above one by a controllable error term as
k\to\infty
.
This ultimately deduces the distribution of
2
-Selmer for type (A)
families.

After the work on type (A) families,
Klagsbrun, Mazur and Rubin
[
KMR13
]
,
[
KMR14
]
found that
the same matrix model and Markov chain also applies to quadratic twists of elliptic curves

E
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
{\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{Q}}(E[2])/{\mathbb{Q}})\cong S_{3}
.
To summarize, we have the following result due to Heath-Brown, Swinnerton-Dyer, Kane,
and Klagsbrun-Mazur-Rubin.

Theorem 1.1

(Heath-Brown, Swinnerton-Dyer, Kane,
and Klagsbrun-Mazur-Rubin)
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a quadratic twist family, which of type (A),
or the curves
E
in
{\mathcal{E}}
satisfies
{\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{Q}}(E[2])/{\mathbb{Q}})\cong S_{3}
.
Then for any
d\geq 0
,

\Prob\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d\mid E\in{\mathcal{E}}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r=0,1}P_{r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d),

where
S(E):={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})/E({\mathbb{Q}})[2]
is the essential
2
-Selmer group of
E
.
In particular, the average order of
S(E)

as
E
runs over
{\mathcal{E}}
is
3
.

1.1.
Matrix model, Markov chain, and distribution of
2
-Selmer rank

For the distribution of
2
-Selmer rank for families
{\mathcal{E}}

with full rational
2
-torsion points
which are not of type (A), no one has even made a conjecture
on the complete description of them.
It turns out that the question is quite subtle:
the distribution has nicer behavior when restricted to equivalence classes,
and can vary drastically among different equivalence classes
even in the same quadratic twist family.
The main goal of this paper is
to study the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups for them (see Theorems
1.2

and
1.6
).

First we define
\Sigma
-equivalence classes.
Let

\Sigma_{0}=\Sigma_{0}({\mathcal{E}}):=\{\text{primes dividing conductors of all curves in }{\mathcal{E}}\}\cup\{2,\infty\}.

Note that there exists an elliptic curve
E\in{\mathcal{E}}
with conductor supported in
\Sigma_{0}({\mathcal{E}})
.
Let
\Sigma
be any finite set of places of
{\mathbb{Q}}
containing
\Sigma_{0}
.
Two elliptic curves in
{\mathcal{E}}
are called
\Sigma
-equivalent
if they are isomorphic over
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}
for all
v\in\Sigma
.
The root number
\epsilon(E)\in\{\pm 1\}
depends only on the

\Sigma
-equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}
of
E
, denoted by
\epsilon({\mathfrak{X}})
.
Let
r({\mathfrak{X}}):=(1-\epsilon({\mathfrak{X}}))/2\in\{0,1\}
.

Next we define the relevant model of random alternating matrices,
extending the general
2
-Selmer model
M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.
A matrix of size
(2k+r)\times(2k+r)
can be considered as
“a block matrix with
2\times 2
big blocks of size roughly
k\times k
”.
The general model
M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
, which we will call type (A) model,
can be considered as “all
2\times 2
big blocks are freely chosen, only subject to
alternating condition”.
We propose type (B) and type (C) model which will add condition that one and two
diagonal blocks are zero (“holes”), respectively.
More precisely, we define

type (A) model:

\displaystyle M_{2k+r,\varnothing}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}):=M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}),

type (B) model:

\displaystyle M_{2k+r,(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}):=\left\{B\in M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\text{ of form }\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&B_{12}\\
B_{21}&B_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\},

type (C) model:

\displaystyle M_{2k+r,(t_{1},t_{2})}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}):=\left\{B\in M_{2k+r}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\text{ of form }\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&B_{12}&B_{13}\\
B_{21}&0&B_{23}\\
B_{31}&B_{32}&B_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\},

with
0
-block of size
k+\frac{t_{1}+r}{2}
for type (B);

0
-blocks of sizes
k+\frac{t_{1}+r}{2}
and
k+\frac{t_{2}+r}{2}
for type (C).
We say
s=0
,
s=1
,
s=2
for model of type (A), (B), (C), respectively,
and call
r
,
s
, and
\mathbf{t}:=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})
the parameters of the model.
Clearly the parameters are subject to constraints

t_{i}\equiv r\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)
, moreover
t_{1}+t_{2}\leq 0
for type (C).

Similar to
[
SD08
]
,
the Markov chain associated to our model
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

plays a crucial role.
The new feature for types (B) and (C) is that, if let

X_{k}
be the random variable
\corank(B)
as

B\in M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
,
then the sequence
(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}
does not form a Markov chain anymore.
Instead, we need to consider the refinement of
X_{k}
:

•

for type (B), there is
B_{1}^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
B_{21}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
associated to
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&B_{12}\\
B_{21}&B_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{2k+r,(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
;

•

for type (C), there are
B_{1}^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
B_{21}\\
B_{31}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
B_{2}^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{12}\\
0\\
B_{32}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
associated to
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&B_{12}&B_{13}\\
B_{21}&0&B_{23}\\
B_{31}&B_{32}&B_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in M_{2k+r,(t_{1},t_{2})}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.

Let
Y_{k}
be the random variable
(X_{k},\corank(B_{1}^{\prime}),\cdots,\corank(B_{s}^{\prime}))

which is a refinement of
X_{k}
,
then
(Y_{k})_{k\geq 0}
forms a Markov chain
(see Appendix
A.2
),
moreover, such a Markov chain is irreducible
(see Appendix
A.1
),
which means that any two points in state space are connected by a chain
of positive transition probabilities.

Once we have the model
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
we can define the probability

P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d):=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B)=d\mid B\in M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\big).

Our main result is as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a quadratic twist family of elliptic curves with full rational
2
-torsion, and

{\mathfrak{X}}\subset{\mathcal{E}}
a
\Sigma
-equivalence class.
Let
r=r({\mathfrak{X}})
.
Then there exists
s\in\{0,1,2\}
depending only on
{\mathcal{E}}
,
a parameter
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathfrak{X}}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})
depending only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
such that for any
d\geq 0
, we have

\Prob\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)=P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d).

More precisely,
there exist absolute constants
C_{1}>0
and
c>0
, such that for any sufficiently large
N

(depending on
{\mathfrak{X}}
),

(1.1)

\sum_{d\geq 0}\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N\big)-P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)\right|<C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2}),

where
N(E)
is the conductor of
E
.

Moreover, the parameter
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}_{{\mathfrak{X}}}

is invariant under the refinement of equivalence classes:
if
\Sigma\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathfrak{X}}
is a
\Sigma^{\prime}
-equivalence class,
then the class
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
has the same parameter
\mathbf{t}
as that of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.

We will call
{\mathcal{E}}
is of type (A), (B), (C)
if the above
s
is
0,1,2
, respectively.
This type (A) coincides with the type that Swinnerton-Dyer and Kane studied
in Theorem
1.1
.
There are also characterizations of type (B) and (C):

(B)

E\in{\mathcal{E}}
has a rational cyclic
4
-isogeny,
and
E[4]\not\subset E({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1}))
for all
E\in{\mathcal{E}}
, for example,
twists
ny^{2}=x(x-1)(x+3)
of
X_{0}(24)
.

(C)

E\in{\mathcal{E}}
has a rational cyclic
4
-isogeny,
and
E[4]\subset E({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1}))
for some
E\in{\mathcal{E}}
, for example,
twists
ny^{2}=x(x-9)(x-25)
of
X_{0}(15)
.

Remark 1.3
.

Types (B) and (C) are not isogeny invariant, for example, the curve

X_{0}(15):y^{2}=x(x-9)(x-25)
which is of type (C) has a
2
-isogenous curve

y^{2}=x(x-1)(x+15)
which is of type (B).

1.2.
Irreducible property and
2
-Selmer rank

The Markov chain associated to the matrix model
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

satisfies the irreducible property, that is,
any two points in state space are connected by a chain
of positive transition probabilities.
This implies that for
d\geq 0
,

P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)>0
if and only if
d\equiv r\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)

and
d\geq\max_{i}(t_{i})
.
In this case, Theorem
1.2
tells us that the

E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d
is of positive density.
Conversely,
if
P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)=0
,
then Proposition
2.5
implies that
there are only finitely many
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}

(in fact, no
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
with bad prime outside
\Sigma_{0}
) such that

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d
.
As an application, we have the following result.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a family with full rational
2
-torsion, and

{\mathfrak{X}}\subset{\mathcal{E}}
a
\Sigma
-equivalence class.
Let
E_{0}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
be a curve which has at least one bad prime outside
\Sigma_{0}
.
Let
d
be any integer with
d\equiv\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E_{0})\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)
and
d\geq\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E_{0})
.
Then

\Prob\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)>0.

Proof.

We only need to prove for families of type (B) and (C).
Let
\mathbf{t}=(t_{i})
be the parameter of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Then by Proposition
2.5
,

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E_{0})\geq\max_{i}(t_{i})
.
Hence
\Prob\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)=P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)>0

by
d\geq\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E_{0})\geq\max_{i}(t_{i})
.
∎

The same question can be asked for the whole quadratic twist family
{\mathcal{E}}
.
Inspired by the
p=2
case of a conjecture by Kolyvagin
(
[
Kol90
]
, Conjecture F),
we have the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a family of type (B) or (C).
Then for
r=0
and
1
,
there exists a
\Sigma
-equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}

with
r({\mathfrak{X}})=r
and
whose parameter
\mathbf{t}_{\mathfrak{X}}=(t_{i})
satisfies
\max_{i}(t_{i})\leq r
.

As a consequence, let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a quadratic twist family of elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with full rational
2
-torsion points. Then for any integer
d\geq 0
,

\Prob\left(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d\ \big|\ E\in{\mathcal{E}}\right)>0.

In particular, there is a positive density of
E\in{\mathcal{E}}

whose
2^{\infty}
-part of Shafarevich-Tate group is trivial.

For families of type (A) this is known by Heath-Brown, Swinnerton-Dyer and Kane
(Theorem
1.1
).

1.3.
Moments of
2
-Selmer groups

We are also interested in the average order
and the “essential” average order of the essential

2
-Selmer group
S(E)
for
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Here we define the average order of
S(E)
to be

\Avg\big(\#S(E)\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big):=\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\big(\#S(E)\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N\big),

and the “essential” average order of
S(E)
to be

\Avg_{0}\big(\#S(E)\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big):=\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\big(\#S(E)\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N,\#\Sigma_{1}(E)\sim\log\log N\big),

where
\Sigma_{1}(E)
is the set of bad primes of
E
outside
\Sigma
,
and for positive real numbers
r
and
N
, the notation
r\sim\log\log N

indicates that
|r-\log\log N|\leq(\log\log N)^{2/3}
.

By Theorem
1.2
,

\Prob\big(\#S(E)=2^{d}\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)=P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)
,
where
\mathbf{t}
is the parameter of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Hence it is reasonable to expect that the above average order
and “essential” average order are also equal to
the average order given by
P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)
,
that is,

\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)2^{d}
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a family with full rational
2
-torsion, and

{\mathfrak{X}}\subset{\mathcal{E}}
a
\Sigma
-equivalence class with parameter
\mathbf{t}
.
Let
\xi\geq 1
be an integer.
Then the
\xi
-th moment of essential
2
-Selmer groups for
{\mathfrak{X}}
is
equal to the
\xi
-th moment of the corresponding matrix model:

(1.2)

\Avg\big(\#S(E)^{\xi}\ |\ E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)=\Avg_{0}\big(\#S(E)^{\xi}\ |\ E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)=\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{{\mathrm{Alt}}}(d)2^{\xi d}.

In particular, for average order

(1.3)

\Avg\big(\#S(E)\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)=\Avg_{0}\big(\#S(E)\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}}\big)=\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)2^{d}=3+\sum_{i}2^{t_{i}}.

Moreover, the
\xi
-th moment has error term
that there exists
C>0
depending only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
and
\xi
,
such that for any sufficiently large
N
(depending on
{\mathfrak{X}}
)

(1.4)

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\big(\#S(E)^{\xi}\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N\big)-\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{{\mathrm{Alt}}}(d)2^{\xi d}\right|<C\cdot\frac{(\log\log N)^{2^{2\xi+1}}}{(\log N)^{1/2^{2\xi}}}.

The “essential”
\xi
-th moment has similar error term

(1.5)

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\big(\#S(E)^{\xi}\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N,\#\Sigma_{1}(E)\sim\log\log N\big)-\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{{\mathrm{Alt}}}(d)2^{\xi d}\right|<C\cdot\frac{\log\log\log N}{(\log\log N)^{1/3}}.

In particular, for any equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}
of type (A), the average order of
S(E)
is always
3
, while for any equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}
of type (B) and (C), the average order of
S(E)
is strictly greater than
3
.

Remark 1.7
.

The case of non-type (A) curves was also considered by the paper of Yu in 2005

[
Yu05
]
,
whose method follows Heath-Brown’s,
claimed that as long as

{\mathcal{E}}
is a family with full rational
2
-torsion points,
there are equivalence classes
{\mathfrak{X}}\subset{\mathcal{E}}

such that the average order of
S(E)
in
{\mathfrak{X}}
is
3

(
[
Yu05
]
, Theorem 2), but which contradicts with our result.

1.4.
Relation with other arithmetic problems

1.4.1.
\theta
-congruent number problem

The so called
\theta
-congruent number families are
one-to-one correspondence to
all families of elliptic curves with full rational
2
-torsion points, and the classification of types can be easily formulated.
Therefore Theorems
1.2
and
1.6
apply
to
\theta
-congruent number problem:
for each
\theta
one can compute the parameter
\mathbf{t}

for each equivalence classes of
\theta
-congruent number families,
and according to
\mathbf{t}
, deduces results on the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups
(in particular, positive density of non-
\theta
-congruent numbers) for them.

For a fixed angle
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, a positive integer
n
is called a
\theta
-congruent number
[
Kan00
]

if
n\sin\theta
is the area of a triangle with rational side lengths and with an angle
\theta
(thus
\cos\theta
must be rational).
To determine whether
n
is
\theta
-congruent or not, it is essentially to determine if the elliptic curve (called a
\theta
-congruent number curve)

ny^{2}=(x-\cos\theta)(x^{2}-1)

has positive Mordell-Weil rank. The
\theta
-congruent number curve family is
of type

(A)

if
\sin(\theta/2)
,
\cos(\theta/2)
are non-rational;

(B)

if
\sin(\theta/2)
,
\cos(\theta/2)
have one rational and one non-rational;

(C)

if
\sin(\theta/2)
,
\cos(\theta/2)
are all rational.

The
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
case is the classical congruent number problem
(
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]
,
[
Tian14
]
,
[
TYZ17
]
,
[
Smi16
]
and so on),
corresponding to the family
ny^{2}=x^{3}-x
, which is of type (A).

1.4.2.
X_{0}(24)
and tiling number problem

A positive integer
n
is called a tiling number,
if an equilateral triangle can be dissected
into
nk^{2}
congruent triangles for some integer
k\geq 1
.
Let
E=X_{0}(24):y^{2}=x(x-1)(x+3)
.
Then a square-free
n\geq 5
is a tiling number if any only if one of
the two elliptic curves
E^{(\pm n)}
defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
has positive Mordell-Weil rank

[
Lac20
]
.
These are families of
\theta
-congruent number curves
of
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
\theta=\frac{2\pi}{3}
,
which are of type (B).
In
[
FLPT24
]
we studied the behaviors of
2
-Selmer groups of them being minimal,
as a consequence, we proved that non-tiling numbers have positive density.
We noticed the differences of them compared to congruent number problem,
which was the beginning of the work in this paper.

Remark 1.8
.

It’s clear that one can put two curves
E^{(\pm n)}
together, and the followings are equivalent:

(i)

one of
the two elliptic curves
E^{(\pm n)}
defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
has positive Mordell-Weil rank;

(ii)

the quadratic twist
A^{(n)}

of the abelian surface
A:=E\times E^{(-1)}
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
has positive Mordell-Weil rank;

(iii)

the elliptic curve
E
over the biquadratic field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1},\sqrt{n})

has positive Mordell-Weil rank.

1.4.3.
X_{0}(15)
and modularity over imaginary quadratic fields

Recent work of Caraiani and Newton
[
CN23
]
relates
the quadratic twists of
E=X_{0}(15):y^{2}=x(x-9)(x-25)

with modularity over imaginary quadratic fields.
The main theorem in
[
CN23
]
states that,
if
n\geq 1
is such that
E^{(-n)}

over
{\mathbb{Q}}
is of Mordell-Weil rank
0
,
then all elliptic curves over the imaginary quadratic field

{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-n})
is modular.
This family is of type (C), and from the computation of the parameter
\mathbf{t}_{\mathfrak{X}}
,
Theorem
1.2
implies that the set of such
n
is of positive density.

1.5.
Idea of proof and organization of this paper

1.5.1.
Moments

Theorem
1.6
is not a direct consequence of
Theorem
1.2
, as the error term in
Theorem
1.2
prevents the terms in
Theorem
1.6
from converging.
In Theorem
1.6
,
the estimation of average order extends Heath-Brown’s argument
in
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]
,
which was for the family
ny^{2}=x^{3}-x
;
the estimation of the “essential” average order extends Kane’s argument
in
[
Kane13
]
, which was for all type (A) families.
It turns out that in both of these arguments, the algebra and the analysis can be done separately. Moreover, these methods also work for an arbitrary high-rank Rédei matrix
B

(Theorem
4.2
).
There are following quantities associated to
B
:

•

a quantity (
3.19
) purely in terms of
linear algebra data of
B
,

•

the “
\omega
-average order”
which can be think of the limit as
k\to\infty
of the average order when restricted to
integers with exactly
k
prime factors,

•

natural average order
and “essential” average order
similar to that in Theorem
1.6
.

The algebra part
(Proposition
3.15
) asserts that,
the linear algebra quantity (
3.19
)
is equal to “
\omega
-average order”.
The analysis part in §
4.2
and §
4.3
,
which is based Heath-Brown’s and Kane’s work,
asserts that the average order,
as well as the “essential” average order,
are also equal to the linear algebra quantity (
3.19
),
so they are equal to “
\omega
-average order”.

Here we emphasize the importance of the existence of the matrix
B
,
which will guide us to the main term of moments computation
when employing the ideas of
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]
and
[
Kane13
]
.
In
[
Yu05
]
, the main term and error term are calculated incorrectly due to lack of matrix guidance.

In the case of the
2
-Selmer groups for elliptic curves,
to compare the
“
\omega
-average order”
with the theoretical average order
\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)2^{d}

predicted by the matrix model,
we compare the almost Markov chain
for
2
-Selmer groups and the Markov chain for
the matrix model
M_{2k+t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.
The estimation of differences of these two transitional probabilities
allows one to deduce that
these two average orders are equal,
similarly for moments
(Theorem
3.2

and (
3.16
)).

We remark that in
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]
and
[
Kane13
]

which deals with type (A) case,
the “
\omega
-average order” and the average order predicted by type (A) matrix model

M_{2k+t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
, as well as moments, are computed directly, and compared to be equal,
without using Markov chain.
On the other hand, in our arguments, these two are not computed directly
(i.e. without closed form expression),
but they are compared via Markov chain argument.

The reason to study the “essential” average order is that,
among positive square-free integers
<N
,
the ones with number of prime factors
r\sim\log\log N

are of density
1-O(\exp(-c(\log\log N)^{1/3}))=1-o(1)

as
N\to\infty
,
hence it is expected that the property of these “good” integers
reflects the property of all positive square-free integers.

1.5.2.
From moments to distribution, or from Markov chain to distribution

Conversely, one may ask if Theorem
1.2

(without error term estimation) is a consequence of Theorem
1.6
.
This is indeed the case for type (A) families.
For type (A),
in Heath-Brown’s
[
HB94
]
and Kane’s
[
Kane13
]
work,
the estimation of moments
give rise to the distribution of rank of
2
-Selmer groups
without error term estimation (Theorem
1.1
).
We expect that
these methods also work for type (B).
However, it’s unlikely for type (C) that
they still work without significant modification,
as the moments in Theorem
1.6
grows too fast for type (C).

As we will see in Appendix
A.2
,
for
\mathbf{t}=\varnothing=(-\infty,-\infty)
for type (A),
or
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1})=(t_{1},-\infty)
for type (B),
or
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},t_{2})
for type (C),
the generating function of
P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)
is

\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)X^{d}=F_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(X):=F_{\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(X)+(-1)^{r}F_{\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(-X)\in{\mathbb{R}}[[X]],

where the formal power series

F_{\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(X):=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+2^{-j}X)}{\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+2^{-j})}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0}2^{i_{1}t_{1}+i_{2}t_{2}}\cdot\frac{2^{2i_{1}i_{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{i_{1}+i_{2}}(2^{1-j}X-1)}{\prod_{j=1}^{i_{1}}(2^{2j}-1)\prod_{j=1}^{i_{2}}(2^{2j}-1)}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[X]].

Here if
t_{1}=-\infty
or
t_{2}=-\infty
, then the corresponding
i_{1}

or
i_{2}
in the sum only takes
0
.
The generating function converges absolutely on the whole complex plane.
Hence it gives formula for the
\xi
-th moment
\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d)2^{\xi d}
for all
\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
by taking
X=2^{\xi}
;
in this case, the
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
only takes

i_{1},i_{2}\geq 0
,
i_{1}+i_{2}\leq\xi
.
This also gives the upper bound and lower bound of moments:
they are
2^{\xi^{2}/2+O(\xi)}
,
2^{2\xi^{2}/3+O(\xi)}

and
2^{\xi^{2}+O(\xi)}
for types (A), (B) and (C), respectively.

For type (C) the moments grow too fast (that is,
2^{\xi^{2}+O(\xi)}
),
the moment-to-distribution methods of Heath-Brown and Kane
do not apply.
We use the following approach to prove the distribution result Theorem
1.2

instead, for all types (A), (B) and (C), without using moments.

•

The
2
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})

depends only on the
\Sigma
-equivalence classes and Legendre symbols of prime factors of
n
.
This is well-known in
[
HB94
]
and
[
SD08
]
.
More importantly, the
2
-Selmer group is in fact the kernel of some matrix over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

whose coefficients are Legendre symbols of prime factors of
n

arranged in a particularly uniform way (we will call them Rédei matrices).
This is suggested in the proofs in
[
HB94
]
and
[
SD08
]
.
This is discussed in §
2
.

•

Thanks to the existence of such matrix whose kernel is
2
-Selmer,
we can analyze the behavior of
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
as the number
k
of prime factors
of
n
increases.
Swinnerton-Dyer’s work
[
SD08
]
also did this (without explicit matrix) to some extent.
Fully writing down the matrix allows us to discover the “holes”
in the matrix, similarly to the matrix model
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.
For type (A), the corank of such matrix already form an “almost Markov chain”,
as in
[
SD08
]
.
For types (B) and (C) they are not: since the existence of “holes”,
to determine the transition probability one must know the corank of submatrices
involving “holes”. This is also reflected in the Markov chain corresponding
to the relevant matrix model
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.
By the same refinement method in the matrix model, we consider the joint distribution
of corank of the matrix representing
2
-Selmer, as well as coranks of its submatrices,
then they form an “almost Markov chain” whose limit is the Markov chain
corresponding to the relevant matrix model.
The analysis of this process is discussed in §
3
,
and the Markov chain
corresponding to the relevant matrix model is discussed in Appendix §
A.2
.

•

Now we know that, if assuming the equidistribution of Legendre symbols,
then the limit as
k\to\infty
of the distribution of
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
with the number
k
of prime factors of
n
fixed, coincides with the prediction of
the relevant matrix model
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.
To compare this with the natural density in Theorem
1.2
,
the equidistribution of Legendre symbols need to be established.
This is mainly done in Smith’s earlier work
[
Smi17
]
.
In fact, one may deduce that, if for each
k
,
a permutation invariant subset of all possible Legendre symbols of
k
primes is given,
such that as
k\to\infty
the limit density of these subsets exists,
then it is equal to the natural density of the subset of positive square-free integers
associated to these subsets of all possible Legendre symbols,
and with error term estimations.
This is discussed in §
4.1
.

Finally, one may find details of proof of main results in §
4.4
.

1.6.
Joint distribution with
\phi
-Selmer groups

We also study the joint distribution
of
2
-Selmer group with
\phi
-Selmer groups in quadratic twist families.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a family with full rational
2
-torsion points.
Let
\phi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\phi}
be a compatible family of rational
2
-isogenies.
There are three different choices of
\phi
.
For each
\phi:E\to E_{\phi}
in
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\phi}
, there is

\phi
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{\phi}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})

which injects into
2
-Selmer group:

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{\phi}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
We define essential
2
-Selmer group
S(E)

and essential
\phi
-Selmer group
S_{\phi}(E)\hookrightarrow S(E)
as

(1.6)

S(E):={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})/E[2],\qquad S_{\phi}(E):={\mathrm{Sel}}_{\phi}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})/E[\phi].

On the other hand, the
\phi

induces the map

\pi:E[2]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}\phi(E[2])\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,
and it is easy to see that the
\phi
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{\phi}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})

is equal to the following
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer group:

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E/{\mathbb{Q}}):=\ker({\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})\hookrightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},E[2])\xrightarrow{\pi}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{F}}_{2}))\subset{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}}).

In §
2
we will see the key point that,
the
2
-Selmer is kernel of some matrix
B
,
while the
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer is the kernel of certain submatrix of
B
.

We define relevant model of random rectangular matrices,
describing the distribution of
\phi
-Selmer groups.
The model is similar to the random matrix models for class groups in
[
Ger84
]
.
For
t\in{\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{-\infty\}
and
d\geq 0
,
define

P_{t}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(d):=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B)=d\mid B\in M_{(k-t)\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\big)\text{ for }t\in{\mathbb{Z}},\quad\text{and}\quad P_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(d):=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }d=0,\\
0,&\text{if }d\geq 1.\end{cases}

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a family with full rational
2
-torsion, and

{\mathfrak{X}}\subset{\mathcal{E}}
a
\Sigma
-equivalence class.
Then for each of the three
\phi
as above,
there exists a
t_{\phi}=t_{\phi,{\mathfrak{X}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{-\infty\}

depending only on
\phi
and
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
and absolute constants
C_{1}>0
and
c>0
,
such that for any sufficiently large
N

(depending on
{\mathfrak{X}}
),

\sum_{d\geq 0}\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\phi}(E)=d\mid E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N\big)-P_{t_{\phi}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(d)\right|<C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2}).

The number of
\phi
such that
t_{\phi}\neq-\infty
is equal to the
s

in Theorem
1.2
,
and the
t_{\phi}
’s not equal to
-\infty

is equal to
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})

in Theorem
1.2
.

Now we recall the matrix model
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.
Recall that for type (B), there is a submatrix
B_{1}^{\prime}
associated to
B
,
and for type (C), there are submatrices
B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime}
associated to
B
.
Recall that
Y_{k}=(X_{k},\corank(B_{1}^{\prime}),\cdots,\corank(B_{s}^{\prime}))

is a refinement of
X_{k}=\corank(B)
.
The
Y_{k}
give rise to a joint probability:

P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d,d_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,d_{s}^{\prime}):=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\corank(B)=d\text{ and}\\
\corank(B_{i}^{\prime})=d_{i}^{\prime}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq s\end{array}\middle|~B\in M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\right).

It’s clear that for
i=1,\cdots,s
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{i}^{\prime})=d_{i}^{\prime}\mid B\in M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\big)=P_{t_{i}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(d_{i}^{\prime}),

hence
M_{2k+r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
also extends the model
M_{(k-t_{i})\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a family with full rational
2
-torsion, and

{\mathfrak{X}}\subset{\mathcal{E}}
a
\Sigma
-equivalence class.
Let
r=r({\mathfrak{X}})
and
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathfrak{X}}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})
be the parameter,
depending only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Then there exist absolute constants
C_{1}>0
and
c>0
, such that
the joint distribution of
2
-Selmer groups with
\phi
-Selmer groups
satisfies that for any sufficiently large
N

(depending on
{\mathfrak{X}}
),

(1.7)

\sum_{d,d_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,d_{s}^{\prime}\geq 0}\left|{\mathbb{P}}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=d\text{ and}\\
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\phi_{i}}(E)=d_{i}^{\prime}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq s\end{array}\middle|~E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N\right)-P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d,d_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,d_{s}^{\prime})\right|\\
<C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2}).

Theorem
1.6
also holds
for essential
\phi
-Selmer groups
S_{\phi}(E)
:
in (
1.2
) it is
\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{t_{\phi}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(d)2^{\xi d}
,
in (
1.3
) it is
1+2^{t_{\phi}}
,
and the error term in (
1.4
)
is
C\cdot(\log\log N)^{2^{\xi+1}}/(\log N)^{1/2^{2\xi}}
.

1.7.
Related and future work

1.7.1.
Chronology

Some results of this paper were announced in the 2022 ICM talk of the second-named author
[
Tian23
]
and also in
[
BT22
]
.

Upon completion of this paper, we became aware of the recent preprint
[
Smi25
]
of Smith. The latter proves striking results on the distribution of coranks of
2^{\infty}
-Selmer groups in quadratic twist families. However, it does not pursue the Markov chain model for the distribution of ranks of
2
-Selmer groups. In contrast the Markov chain aspect of the distribution is the focus of this paper, which is bound to lead to a refinement of the results of
[
Smi25
]
.

1.7.2.
Propsects

The results and ideas of this paper lead to Markov chain property for the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups in the remaining case that
{\mathbb{Q}}(E[2])
is quadratic, and will appear in a sequel. As alluded to above, they are also relevant to the Selmer counterpart of the Goldfeld conjecture, pertaining to distribution of coranks of
2^{\infty}
-Selmer groups.

2.
2
-Selmer groups and Rédei matrix

Let
\Sigma
be a finite set containing
-1,2
and some primes of
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
For each
k\geq 0

let
S_{k}(\Sigma)
be the set of positive square-free integers coprime to
\Sigma

with exactly
k
prime factors,
and let
S(\Sigma):=\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}S_{k}(\Sigma)

be the set of positive square-free integers coprime to
\Sigma
.
Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}

with full rational
2
-torsion points.
Then any curve in
{\mathfrak{X}}
can be written as
E^{(n)}

for some
n\in S(\Sigma)
and some elliptic curve
E/{\mathbb{Q}}

depending only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
with full rational
2
-torsion points
and has good reduction outside
\Sigma
.
Recall that the
2
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
is

(2.1)

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}}):=\ker\left(H^{1}(G_{S,{\mathbb{Q}}},E^{(n)}[2])\to\bigoplus_{p\in S}\frac{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},E^{(n)}[2])}{\Im(\kappa_{p})}\right),

here
S
is any finite set of places of

{\mathbb{Q}}
containing
2
,
\infty
and bad places for
E^{(n)}
,
and
\kappa_{p}:E^{(n)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})/2E^{(n)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\hookrightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},E^{(n)}[2])

is the local Kummer map.
For
\pi:E^{(n)}[2]\cong E[2]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

we define the
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer group

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}}):=\ker({\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\pi}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{F}}_{2}))\subset{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}}).

If
\phi:E\to E_{\phi}
is a rational
2
-isogeny,
which also gives
\phi:E^{(n)}\to E_{\phi}^{(n)}
, then it induces the map

\pi:E^{(n)}[2]\cong E[2]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}\phi(E[2])\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,
and it’s easy to see that the usual
\phi
-Selmer group

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{\phi}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
is equal to the
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer group

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
for such specifically chosen
\pi
.
So our main results can be rephrased by using
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups.
As in (
1.6
),
we define the essential
2
-Selmer group

S(E^{(n)}):={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})/E^{(n)}[2]

and essential
\phi
-Selmer group
S_{\phi}(E^{(n)})
,
similarly we can define the
\pi
-strict essential
2
-Selmer group

S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)}):={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})/\ker(\pi)\subset S(E^{(n)})

which is equal to the essential
\phi
-Selmer group
S_{\phi}(E^{(n)})
.
Later we will see that the following modified

\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})

is also useful:

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})\subset{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}}):=\ker\left({\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\pi}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\xrightarrow{{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma_{1}}}\bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma_{1}}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\right),

where
\Sigma_{1}
is the set of bad places of
E^{(n)}
outside
\Sigma
.
Namely, it only requires that the localization at
\Sigma_{1}
is contained in
the kernel of
\pi
.
Similarly define
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n)})
.

We will see by
2
-descent method (Theorem
2.2
,

2.8
,
2.9
and
Corollary
2.3
) that

Theorem 2.1
.

If
n=\ell_{1}\cdots\ell_{k}
and
n^{\prime}=\ell_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\ell_{k}^{\prime}
are two elements
of
S_{k}(\Sigma)
such that
\ell_{i}/\ell_{i}^{\prime}\in({\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times})^{2}

and
\left(\frac{\ell_{j}}{\ell_{i}}\right)=\left(\frac{\ell_{j}^{\prime}}{\ell_{i}^{\prime}}\right)

for all
i,j
and
v
,
then under the isomorphism
H^{1}(G_{S,{\mathbb{Q}}},E^{(n)}[2])\cong{\mathbb{Q}}(S,2)^{\oplus 2}\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(G_{S^{\prime},{\mathbb{Q}}},E^{(n^{\prime})}[2])\cong{\mathbb{Q}}(S^{\prime},2)^{\oplus 2}
,
\ell_{i}\mapsto\ell_{i}^{\prime}
,
p\mapsto p
for
p\in\Sigma
,
here
S:=\Sigma\cup\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{k}\}
and

S^{\prime}:=\Sigma\cup\{\ell_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,\ell_{k}^{\prime}\}
,
we have

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n^{\prime})}/{\mathbb{Q}})
,

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n^{\prime})}/{\mathbb{Q}})
,
and
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n^{\prime})}/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Same as essential versions.

For
2
-Selmer groups this result is well-known
(see Monsky’s Appendix
in
[
HB94
]
, or Swinnerton-Dyer’s work
[
SD08
]
).
The argument is similar for
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups.
In this paper we want to emphasize the crucial point
that this dependence is “uniform” as
k

varies when restricted to
\Sigma
-equivalence classes

{\mathfrak{X}}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
.

For each
k\geq 0
and each element
n\in S_{k}(\Sigma)
, we fix an order
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{k}

of its prime factors,
and define the following matrices and vectors
of coefficients in
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
associated to
n
.

•

Define
A=A(n):=(a_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
1\leq j\leq k\end{subarray}}\in M_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

where
a_{ij}:=\left[\frac{\ell_{j}}{\ell_{i}}\right]
if
i\neq j
,
and
a_{ii}:=\left[\frac{n/\ell_{i}}{\ell_{i}}\right]
.
Here
\left[\frac{\ell_{j}}{\ell_{i}}\right]:=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{\ell_{j}}{\ell_{i}}\right)\right)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

is the additive Legendre symbol.

•

For
d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
define
z_{d}=z_{d}(n):=(z_{1}^{(d)},\cdots,z_{k}^{(d)})^{\mathrm{T}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k}
,
where
z_{i}^{(d)}:=\left[\frac{d}{\ell_{i}}\right]
.
The
z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
is the transpose of
z_{d}
.
Define
D_{d}:=\diag(z_{d})\in M_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

and
A_{d}:=D_{d}+z_{d}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}\in M_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.

Clearly
\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{ij}=0
for all
i
,
also by quadratic reciprocity law,

a_{ji}=a_{ij}+z_{i}^{(-1)}z_{j}^{(-1)}
for all
i\neq j
;
equivalently,
A+A^{\mathrm{T}}=A_{-1}
.
This motivates us to define the probability space of Legendre symbols

(2.2)

\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}:=\left\{\big((a_{ij}),(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)\in M_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\times({\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k})^{\#\Sigma}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}a_{ji}=a_{ij}+z_{i}^{(-1)}z_{j}^{(-1)}\text{ for all }i\neq j,\\
\sum_{j}a_{ij}=0\text{ for all }i\end{array}\right\},

and a map
S_{k}(\Sigma)\to\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}
,

n\mapsto\omega(n)=\big(A(n),(z_{p}(n))_{p\in\Sigma}\big)
.
The above
A
,
D_{d}
and
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
for
c,d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
are viewed as maps
\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}\to M_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

for each
k
;
z_{d}
are maps
\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k}

for each
k
.
The Theorem
2.1

that
2
-Selmer groups depend only on Legendre symbols
is equivalent to say
that the
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
as well as
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)})

depends only on
\omega=\omega(n)\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}
.
By abuse of notation, we denote these
2
-Selmer groups by

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(\omega)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
,
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(\omega)})
, and so on.

To describe the “uniform”, we define the concept of
a
Rédei matrix
, which
is a block matrix whose entries are linear combinations
of
z_{d}
,
z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
,
A
,
D_{d}
and
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
,
where
c,d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.
A Rédei matrix
B
is viewed as a “uniform” rule,
which maps an element
\omega
of
\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}
for each
k
,
to a matrix
B(\omega)
;
it also maps an element
n
of
S(\Sigma)

to a matrix
B(n)
.
The set of Rédei matrices of size
(ak+b)\times(ck+d)

is denoted by
{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}
.

For a Rédei matrix
B
of size
k\times k

(namely, linear combinations of
A
,
D_{d}
and
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
),
write it uniquely as
B=B_{H}+B_{M}+B_{L}

“the decomposition by ranks”,
where

•

B_{H}
is the “high-rank part” which is linear combination of
A
,

•

B_{M}
is the “medium-rank part” which is linear combination of
D_{d}
,

•

B_{L}
is the “low-rank part” which is linear combination of
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
.

The
B
is called
of “high-rank” if
B_{H}\neq 0
;
of “medium-rank” if
B_{H}=0
and
B_{M}\neq 0
;
of “low-rank” if
B_{H}=B_{M}=0
.
Here we give a remark but without proof that,
these
3
types of
k\times k
Rédei matrices are classified
according to their behavior of ranks
when viewed as a random variable
\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}\to M_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
:

•

a “high-rank Rédei matrix” is that,
its rank is mostly
k+o(k)
as
k\to\infty
;

•

a “medium-rank Rédei matrix” is that,
its rank is mostly
\frac{1}{2}k+o(k)
as
k\to\infty
;

•

a “low-rank Rédei matrix” is that,
its rank is mostly
o(k)
as
k\to\infty
.

The main result of this section is as follows,
which is a reinterpretation of Swinnerton-Dyer’s work
[
SD08
]
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a
\Sigma
-equivalence class
of quadratic twist family of elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}

with full rational
2
-torsion points,
as in the beginning of the introduction.
Then there exists an integer
t_{\mathfrak{X}}\equiv r({\mathfrak{X}})\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)
depends only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
and a Rédei matrix

B=\begin{pmatrix}B_{11}&B_{12}&B_{13}\\
B_{21}&B_{22}&B_{23}\\
B_{31}&B_{32}&B_{33}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(2k+t_{\mathfrak{X}})\times(2k+t_{\mathfrak{X}})},

depends only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
, where

•

B
is alternating, namely
B_{ji}=B_{ij}^{\mathrm{T}}

and the diagonal of
B_{ii}
is
0
,

•

B_{11},B_{12},B_{21},B_{22}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{k\times k}
,

B_{13},B_{23}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{k\times t_{\mathfrak{X}}}
,
and
B_{33}=0\in M_{t_{\mathfrak{X}}\times t_{\mathfrak{X}}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
,

•

the entries of
B_{13}
and
B_{23}
are
z_{d}
,
d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{13}\\
B_{23}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{2k\times t_{\mathfrak{X}}}

is of generically full rank,
which means that, there exists
k\geq 1
and
\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}

such that

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{13}(\omega)\\
B_{23}(\omega)\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is of rank
t_{\mathfrak{X}}
,

•

B_{12}
and
B_{21}
are high-rank Rédei matrices;
the three matrices
B_{11}
,
B_{22}
and
B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}

are all medium-rank or low-rank Rédei matrices,
and the number of low-rank Rédei matrices among them
is
0
,
1
or
2
if
{\mathfrak{X}}
is of type (A), (B) or (C), respectively,

such that for any
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
define
B(E)
to be
B(n)

where
n\in S(\Sigma)
is the product of bad primes of
E

outside
\Sigma
,
then

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})=\corank(B(E))
,
and the three modified
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
are of dimensions

\corank(B_{j}^{\prime}(E))
,
j=1,2,3
, where

B_{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(2k+t_{\mathfrak{X}})\times(k+t_{\mathfrak{X}})}
are

B_{j}^{\prime}:=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1j}&B_{13}\\
B_{2j}&B_{23}\\
B_{3j}&B_{33}\end{pmatrix}\text{ for }j=1,2,\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{3}^{\prime}:=\begin{pmatrix}B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}&B_{13}+B_{23}\\
B_{21}+B_{22}&B_{23}\\
B_{31}+B_{32}&B_{33}\end{pmatrix},

in which exactly one
k\times k
block is high-rank Rédei matrix
(
B_{21}
,
B_{12}
and
B_{21}+B_{22}
, respectively).

The entries
B_{ij}
of
B
has more precise description
if we fix equations of
E
in
{\mathfrak{X}}
, see Theorem

2.8
.

Later in §
2.1
we will see that for
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
the
B(E)
is the Gram matrix of certain alternating form
\theta
whose kernel is isomorphic to
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Furthermore, when
E
has a bad prime outside
\Sigma
, then the
2
-torsion points

E[2]
injects into
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
, which
implies that the sum of columns of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1i}\\
B_{2i}\\
B_{3i}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
i=1,2
,
are linear combinations of columns of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{13}\\
B_{23}\\
B_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
For the modified
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer group it’s similar.
Hence we have the following corollary.

Corollary 2.3
.

For
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
with a bad prime outside
\Sigma
,
the dimension of its essential
2
-Selmer group
(
1.6
) is

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=\corank(B(E))-2=\corank(\widetilde{B}(E))
with

\widetilde{B}(E)=\widetilde{B}(n)=(\widetilde{B}_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq 3\\
1\leq j\leq 3\end{subarray}}
,
where
n\in S_{\geq 1}(\Sigma):=\bigsqcup_{k\geq 1}S_{k}(\Sigma)

is the product of bad primes of
E
outside
\Sigma
,

•

\widetilde{B}_{11},\widetilde{B}_{12},\widetilde{B}_{21},\widetilde{B}_{22}

are the corresponding
B_{ij}
with last row and column removed,

•

\widetilde{B}_{13},\widetilde{B}_{23}

are the corresponding
B_{ij}
with last row removed,

•

\widetilde{B}_{31},\widetilde{B}_{32}

are the corresponding
B_{ij}
with last column removed,

•

\widetilde{B}_{33}=B_{33}
.

Similarly, there are
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}

such that the three modified
\pi
-strict essential
2
-Selmer group
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E)

are of dimensions
\corank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}(E))
,
j=1,2,3
.

We introduce crucial invariants for an equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}
of type (B) and (C).

Definition 2.4
.

Let
s
be the number of low-rank Rédei matrices among

B_{11}
,
B_{22}
and
B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}
,
namely,
0
,
1
or
2
if
{\mathfrak{X}}
is of type (A), (B) or (C), respectively.
If
{\mathfrak{X}}
is of type (B), we may assume that

B_{11}
is low-rank Rédei matrix;
if
{\mathfrak{X}}
is of type (C), we may assume that

B_{11}
and
B_{22}
are low-rank Rédei matrices.
Define

B_{j}^{\prime\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{jj}&B_{j3}\\
B_{3j}&B_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

for
1\leq j\leq s
, which is a submatrix of
B_{j}^{\prime}
;
similarly define
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
which is a submatrix of
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}
.
Note that
\rank(B_{j}^{\prime\prime}(E))=\rank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(E))
for
1\leq j\leq s

and
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
which has a bad prime outside
\Sigma
.
Define the parameter
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathfrak{X}}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})
of
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
which is an unordered tuple of
s
integers, to be

t_{j}:=t_{\mathfrak{X}}-\max_{E\in{\mathfrak{X}}}\rank(B_{j}^{\prime\prime}(E))=t_{\mathfrak{X}}-\max_{E\in{\mathfrak{X}}}\rank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(E)).

Proposition
3.11

gives us a way to find
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that

\rank(B_{j}^{\prime\prime}(E))=\rank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(E))
is maximal,
hence the parameter
t_{j}
is computable.
In §
3.4
we will see that
if
\Sigma\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathfrak{X}}
is a
\Sigma^{\prime}
-equivalence class,
then the class
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
has the same parameter
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathfrak{X}}

as that of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.

There is an obvious lower bound of Selmer rank.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of type (B) or (C)
with parameter
\mathbf{t}=(t_{i})
.
Then for any
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
with a bad prime outside
\Sigma
,
for any
1\leq i\leq s
, the
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{i}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E)

and
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)
satisfy

0\leq\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{i}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E)\leq\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)\leq 2\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{i}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E)-t_{i}
.
In particular, we have
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{i}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E)\geq t_{i}

and
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)\geq\max_{i}(t_{i})
.

Proof.

By Corollary
2.3

we have
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{j}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E)=\corank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}(E))

and
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=\corank(\widetilde{B}(E))
.
By linear algebra,

0\leq\corank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}(E))\leq\corank(\widetilde{B}(E)),

and

2\rank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}(E))\leq\rank(\widetilde{B}(E))+\rank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(E))\leq\rank(\widetilde{B}(E))+t_{\mathfrak{X}}-t_{j},

so

\corank(\widetilde{B}(E))\leq 2\corank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}(E))-t_{j}.

Hence
\corank(\widetilde{B}(E))\geq\corank(\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}(E))\geq\max\{t_{j},0\}
.
∎

Example 2.6
.

Let
E:y^{2}=x(x-1)(x+3)
of type (B).
Then for any
{\mathfrak{X}}
contained in

\{E^{(-n)}\mid n\equiv 1\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,12\right)\text{ positive square-free}\}
,
its parameter
\mathbf{t}_{\mathfrak{X}}=(2)
.
This can be seen from the matrix calculation in
[
FLPT24
]
.

2.1.
The alternating matrix associated to
2
-Selmer groups

2.1.1.
2
-Selmer groups and linear algebra

Let
E/{\mathbb{Q}}
be an elliptic curve with full rational
2
-torsion points,

S
be a finite set of places of
{\mathbb{Q}}
containing
2
,
\infty
and bad places for
E
.
Besides (
2.1
), there is another point of view of
the
2
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
:

•

let
V=\bigoplus_{p\in S}V_{p}=\bigoplus_{p\in S}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},E[2])

be the space of local Galois cohomology,

•

let
U:=\Im\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{S}:H^{1}(G_{S,{\mathbb{Q}}},E[2])\hookrightarrow V\big)\subset V

be the image of global Galois cohomology,

•

let
W=\bigoplus_{p\in S}W_{p}=\bigoplus_{p\in S}\Im(\kappa_{p})\subset V

be the image of local Kummer maps,

then
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\Im\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{S}:{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})\hookrightarrow V\big)=U\cap W\subset V
.
The space
V
is endowed with a non-degenerating alternating pairing

e:V\times V\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
which is induced by Tate local duality and the Weil pairing

e_{E}:E[2]\times E[2]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.
The
U
and
W
are two Lagrangian subspaces of the symplectic space

(V,e)
.

Note the following linear algebra fact:
if
(V,e)
is a symplectic space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, and
W,U,K
are three Lagrangian subspaces
satisfying
V=U\oplus K
, then

\theta:W/(W\cap K)\times W/(W\cap K)\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2},\quad(x,y)\mapsto e(x_{U},y_{K})

is a symmetric pairing, and the natural projection
W\twoheadrightarrow W/(W\cap K)

gives
W\cap U\cong\ker(\theta)
;
furthermore, if there exists a “quadratic form”
\phi:V\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
associated to
e

in the sense that
e(x,y)=\phi(x+y)+\phi(x)+\phi(y)
for all
x,y\in V
,
and such that
\phi(U)=\phi(W)=\phi(K)=0
,
then the
\theta
is alternating.
Based on this observation, Swinnerton-Dyer
[
SD08
]

constructs systematic
K
when
E
varies in a quadratic twist family,
such that
\theta
is further alternating.
In the following we recall this construction.

2.1.2.
2
-Selmer groups in quadratic twist family

Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a
\Sigma
-equivalence class with full rational
2
-torsion points,
and
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
. Let
\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma_{1}(E)
be the set of bad places of
E
outside
\Sigma
,
and take
S=\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma_{1}
.
Then the image
W_{p}:=\Im(\kappa_{p})
of the local Kummer map has the following properties:

•

for
p\in\Sigma
,
W_{p}
depends only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
;

•

for
p\in\Sigma_{1}
,
W_{p}=\kappa_{p}(E[2])
.

The “quadratic form”
\phi:V\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
is the sum of local
“quadratic forms”
\phi_{p}:V_{p}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
for
p\in S
,
where each
\phi_{p}
is associated to
e_{p}:V_{p}\times V_{p}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
and satisfies

\phi_{p}(W_{p})=0
.
The definition of
\phi_{p}
is invariant under quadratic twists:
suppose
{\mathcal{E}}
is the quadratic twists of
y^{2}=x(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})

and
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
is
y^{2}=x(x-e_{1}m)(x-e_{2}m)
,
then
\phi_{p}
can be written down explicitly as

\phi_{p}\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}\\
b_{2}\end{pmatrix}:=[e_{1}e_{2}b_{1},e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})b_{2}]_{p},

independent of
m
.
Here
[\ ,\ ]_{p}
is additive Hilbert symbol,
and we used
V_{p}\cong\big({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times})^{2}\big)^{\oplus 2}

according to the basis
\{(e_{1}m,0),(0,0)\}
of
E[2]
.

The subspace
K
of
V
is constructed as
K:=K_{\Sigma}\oplus K_{\Sigma_{1}}
,
where
K_{\Sigma_{1}}=\bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma_{1}}H_{\mathrm{unr}}^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},E[2])

is the unramified Galois cohomology.
The
K_{\Sigma}
is a subspace of
V_{\Sigma}
, depending only on
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
satisfying the following properties:

•

V_{\Sigma}=U_{\Sigma}\oplus K_{\Sigma}
.

•

K_{\Sigma}
is a Lagrangian subspace of
(V_{\Sigma},e_{\Sigma})
.

•

\phi_{\Sigma}(K_{\Sigma})=0
.

•

W_{\Sigma}=(U_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma})\oplus(K_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma})
.

The existence of
K_{\Sigma}
is guaranteed by the following linear algebra result.

Lemma 2.7

(
[
SD08
]
, Lemma 2)
.

Let
(V,e)
be a finite dimensional symplectic space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.
Let
U
and
W
be two Lagrangian subspaces.
Let
\phi:V\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
be a map which is a “quadratic form” associated to
e
,
and such that
\phi(U)=\phi(W)=0
.
Then there exists a Lagrangian subspace
K
of
V

such that
V=U\oplus K
,
\phi(K)=0

and
W=(U\cap W)\oplus(K\cap W)
.

2.1.3.
Determining the entries of the alternating matrix

The choice of
K
makes
W/(W\cap K)=W_{\Sigma_{1}}\oplus\overline{W}_{\Sigma}

where
\overline{W}_{\Sigma}:=W_{\Sigma}/(W_{\Sigma}\cap K_{\Sigma})\cong U_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma}

which only depends on
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
The
t_{\mathfrak{X}}
in Theorem

2.2

is just the dimension of
\overline{W}_{\Sigma}
.
Fix basis
\{P_{1},P_{2}\}
of
E[2]
uniformly for
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
then

W/(W\cap K)=W_{\Sigma_{1}}\oplus\overline{W}_{\Sigma}=W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(1)}\oplus W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(2)}\oplus\overline{W}_{\Sigma}

where
W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(i)}:=\bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma_{1}}\kappa_{p}(\langle P_{i}\rangle)
.
The matrix
B=(B_{ij})
in Theorem
2.2

is just the Gram matrix
B
of
\theta
under this decomposition;
note that the dimensions of
W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(1)}
,

W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(2)}
and
\overline{W}_{\Sigma}
are
k
,
k
and
t_{\mathfrak{X}}
,
respectively, where
k=\#\Sigma_{1}(E)
.

It’s easy to write down the correspondence of elements of
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})

and elements of
\ker(B)
explicitly.
Suppose
x=(x^{(1)};x^{(2)})\in{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})\subset H^{1}(G_{S,{\mathbb{Q}}},E[2])\cong{\mathbb{Q}}(S,2)^{\oplus 2}
under our choice of basis of
E[2]
.
Then the corresponding
y=\overline{{\mathrm{loc}}_{S}(x)}\in\ker(B)\subset W/(W\cap K)=W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(1)}\oplus W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(2)}\oplus\overline{W}_{\Sigma}

is
(y_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(1)};y_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(2)};y_{\Sigma})
,
where
y_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(i)}={\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma_{1}}(x^{(i)})=(\ord_{\ell}(x^{(i)}))_{\ell\in\Sigma_{1}}\in W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(i)}\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k}

is obvious to describe, while

y_{\Sigma}=\overline{{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(x)}\in\overline{W}_{\Sigma}
is
the image of
x
under the map

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}}W_{\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{W}_{\Sigma}
, which is not so obvious to describe,
since it depends on the choice of
\overline{W}_{\Sigma}
.

From this it’s easy to give a description of modified
\pi
-strict

2
-Selmer groups in terms of the kernel of matrix.
For example, if
\pi:E[2]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
is
P_{1}\mapsto 0
,
P_{2}\mapsto 1
,
then an element
x=(x^{(1)};x^{(2)})\in{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
is contained in

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
if and only if

y_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(2)}={\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma_{1}}(x^{(2)})=0
.
Hence the corresponding
(y_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(1)};y_{\Sigma})

is contained in
\ker(B_{1}^{\prime})
. For other
\pi
it’s similar.

To prove Theorem
2.2
as well as getting more
precise description of
B_{ij}
, it’s better to fix equations of
E
in
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
and we have the following result.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
E:y^{2}=x(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})
be an elliptic curve, where
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
\Sigma
be containing
-1
all primes dividing
2e_{1}e_{2}(e_{1}-e_{2})
.
Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of square-free integers,
and
q\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
be the
\Sigma
-part of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Then Theorem
2.2

holds for
\{E^{(m)}\mid m\in{\mathfrak{X}}\}
, namely,
for any
m=qn\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, where

n\in S(\Sigma)
the prime-to-
\Sigma
part of
m
,
we have

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})=\corank(B(n))
.
Moreover, the
B_{11},B_{21},B_{22}
have form

\displaystyle B_{11}

\displaystyle=A_{e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})}+B_{11,L},

\displaystyle B_{21}

\displaystyle=A+D_{e_{1}q}+B_{21,L},

\displaystyle B_{22}

\displaystyle=A_{e_{1}e_{2}}+B_{22,L},

where

B_{11,L},B_{21,L},B_{22,L}
are low-rank Rédei matrices,
namely, linear combinations of
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
.

In the above theorem,
the medium-rank part of

B_{11}
,
B_{22}
and
B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}

are
D_{e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})}
,
D_{e_{1}e_{2}}

and
D_{-e_{2}(e_{1}-e_{2})}
, respectively.
It turns out that the quadratic twist family
containing
E:y^{2}=x(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})

is of type (A), (B) or (C)
if and only if there are
0
,
1
or
2
perfect squares
among
e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})
,
e_{1}e_{2}

and
-e_{2}(e_{1}-e_{2})
.
This justifies the assertion on the type in Theorem
2.2
.

In the following we prove Theorem
2.8
,
fixing a choice
P_{1}=(e_{1}m,0)
and
P_{2}=(0,0)

of the basis of
E[2]
where
E:y^{2}=x(x-e_{1}m)(x-e_{2}m)

(which is the
E^{(m)}
in Theorem
2.8
,
and is the
E
in Theorem
2.2
).
We use the following notation: if
T\subset S
,
let
H_{T}^{1}:=H^{1}(G_{T,{\mathbb{Q}}},E[2])

and let
U_{T}
be the image of
{\mathrm{loc}}_{T}:H_{T}^{1}\hookrightarrow V_{T}
.

The
\theta|_{\overline{W}_{\Sigma}\times\overline{W}_{\Sigma}}

We have
\theta|_{\overline{W}_{\Sigma}\times\overline{W}_{\Sigma}}=0
.
In fact, if
x\in W_{\Sigma}\subset W
,
then there are unique
x_{1}\in U
,
x_{2}\in K
,

x_{1}^{\prime}\in U_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma}
and
x_{2}^{\prime}\in K_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma}

such that
x=x_{1}+x_{2}=x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime}
.
Let
x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}\in H_{\Sigma}^{1}\subset H_{S}^{1}
be the unique element
such that
x_{1}^{\prime}={\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})
.
Then it’s easy to see that
x_{1}={\mathrm{loc}}_{S}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})=x_{1}^{\prime}+{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma_{1}}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})

and
x_{2}=x_{2}^{\prime}+{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma_{1}}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})
,
both of them are contained in
W_{\Sigma}\oplus K_{\Sigma_{1}}
,
which is a Lagrangian subspace of
V
,
since
W_{\Sigma}
and
K_{\Sigma_{1}}

are Lagrangian subspaces of
V_{\Sigma}
and
V_{\Sigma_{1}}
, respectively.
In particular,
if
x,y\in W_{\Sigma}
, then

\theta(x,y)=e(x_{1},y_{2})=0
.

The
\theta|_{\overline{W}_{\Sigma}\times W_{\Sigma_{1}}}

For
\ell\in\Sigma_{1}
, the local Kummer map
\kappa_{\ell}
under our chosen basis

\kappa_{\ell}:{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\cdot\{P_{1},P_{2}\}=E[2]\hookrightarrow V_{\ell}\cong\big({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})^{2}\big)^{\oplus 2}

is represented by the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{11}m&e_{12}\\
e_{21}&e_{22}m\end{smallmatrix}\right)

where

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{11}&e_{12}\\
e_{21}&e_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right):=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{1}e_{2}\\
e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})&-e_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

The Weil pairing
e_{E}:E[2]\times E[2]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
induces a bilinear map

d:H_{\Sigma}^{1}\times E[2]\to{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
independent of
m
,
such that for any
m\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, any
\ell\in\Sigma_{1}
,
any
x\in H_{\Sigma}^{1}
and any
P\in E[2]
, we have

e_{\ell}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell}(x),\kappa_{\ell}(P)\big)=\left[\frac{d(x,P)}{\ell}\right].

In fact, let
H=(h_{ij})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
be the matrix representing
e_{E}

under the basis of
E[2]
,
if
x=(b_{1},b_{2})^{\mathrm{T}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)^{\oplus 2}\cong H_{\Sigma}^{1}

and
P=P_{t}
,
t=1,2
,
then by the formula of the cup product
e_{\ell}
and the local Kummer map

\kappa_{\ell}
,
it’s easy to see that

d(x,P)=\sum_{i}h_{it}b_{i}
, here the group operation on
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)

is written additively.
Namely, the
d
is represented by the same matrix as the Weil pairing
e_{E}
.

Suppose
0\neq x\in U_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma}

and
0\neq P\in E[2]
.
Consider
\theta|_{\langle x\rangle\times W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(t)}}
.
Say
\ell\in\Sigma_{1}
and
y=\kappa_{\ell}(P)
.
We have
x=x_{1}^{\prime}={\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})
,
hence
x_{1}={\mathrm{loc}}_{S}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})

and

\theta(x,y)=e(x_{1},y)=e\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{S}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}),\kappa_{\ell}(P)\big)=e_{\ell}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}),\kappa_{\ell}(P)\big)=\left[\frac{d(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}},P)}{\ell}\right].

That is to say,

\theta|_{\langle x\rangle\times W_{\Sigma_{1}}^{(t)}}

is represented by the Rédei matrix

z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{1\times k}

with
d=d(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}},P)\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.
When
x
runs over a basis of
U_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma}

and
P
runs over a basis of
E[2]
, we obtain that

\theta|_{\overline{W}_{\Sigma}\times W_{\Sigma_{1}}}

is represented by a Rédei matrix
in
{\mathrm{RM}}_{\dim\overline{W}_{\Sigma}\times 2k}
,
which is the submatrix
(B_{31},B_{32})

of
B
in Theorem
2.2
.

We claim that this Rédei matrix
is generically full rank.
Equivalently, when
x
runs over a basis of
U_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma}
,
the
\big(d(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}},P_{t})\big)_{t=1,2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)^{\oplus 2}

are linearly independent.
By the formula of
d
and that
H=(h_{ij})
is invertible,
this is equivalent to that, when
x
runs over a basis of
U_{\Sigma}\cap W_{\Sigma}
,
the
x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}\in H_{\Sigma}^{1}\cong{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)^{\oplus 2}

are linearly independent.
But we know that such
x
satisfies
x=x_{1}^{\prime}={\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})
,
so
x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}
must be linearly independent.

The
\theta|_{W_{\Sigma_{1}}\times W_{\Sigma_{1}}}

For
\ell\in\Sigma_{1}
,
define
Z_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}^{1}

to be the subspace
\langle\ell\rangle^{\oplus 2}
of
({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})^{2})^{\oplus 2}\cong V_{\ell}
.
Then clearly
V_{\ell}=K_{\ell}\oplus Z_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}^{1}
.
Define
\kappa_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}
to be

E[2]\xrightarrow{\kappa_{\ell}}V_{\ell}\to Z_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}^{1}

and define
{\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}
to be

H_{\ell}^{1}\xrightarrow{{\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell}}V_{\ell}\to Z_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}^{1}
.
Then it’s easy to see that these two maps are isomorphisms.
So we obtain an isomorphism
\lambda_{\ell}:={\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}^{-1}\circ\kappa_{\ell,{\mathrm{tr}}}:E[2]\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\ell}^{1}
,
in fact,
under our chosen basis it is just represented by the identity matrix.
For
x=\kappa_{\ell}(P)\in W_{\ell}
write

x=x_{1}+x_{2}

according to
V=U\oplus K
.
Let
x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}\in H_{S}^{1}
be such that

x_{1}={\mathrm{loc}}_{S}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}})
.
Write
x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}=x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}}+x_{1,\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathrm{glob}}

according to
H_{S}^{1}=H_{\Sigma}^{1}\oplus H_{\Sigma_{1}}^{1}
.
Write
x_{2}=x_{2,\Sigma}+x_{2,\Sigma_{1}}

according to
K=K_{\Sigma}\oplus K_{\Sigma_{1}}
.
Then it’s easy to see that

x_{1,\Sigma_{1}}^{\mathrm{glob}}=\lambda_{\ell}(P)\qquad\text{and}\qquad x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}}=({\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}|_{H_{\Sigma}^{1}})^{-1}({\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))_{1}^{\prime}),

here
{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))={\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))_{1}^{\prime}+{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))_{2}^{\prime}
according to
V_{\Sigma}=U_{\Sigma}\oplus K_{\Sigma}
.

Suppose
x=\kappa_{\ell}(P)
and
y=\kappa_{\ell^{\prime}}(Q)

with
P,Q\in E[2]
and
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{1}
.
Then

\theta(x,y)=e(x_{1},y)=e_{\ell^{\prime}}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(x_{1}^{\mathrm{glob}}),y\big)=e_{\ell^{\prime}}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}}),y\big)+e_{\ell^{\prime}}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\lambda_{\ell}(P)),y\big).

We have
{\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}})\in K_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
while
{\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))\in K_{\ell^{\prime}}

if
\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell
,
and
{\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))\in Z_{\ell^{\prime},{\mathrm{tr}}}^{1}

if
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
.
Therefore, if we suppose
P=P_{s}
and
Q=P_{t}
for
s,t\in\{1,2\}
,
then we have

(2.3)

e_{\ell^{\prime}}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}}),y\big)=\sum_{i}h_{it}\left[\frac{\pi_{i}(x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}})}{\ell^{\prime}}\right],

where
\pi_{i}:H_{\Sigma}^{1}\to{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
is the projection to
i
-th component
according to our chosen basis; similarly,

(2.4)

e_{\ell^{\prime}}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\lambda_{\ell}(P)),y\big)=\sum_{j}h_{sj}[\ell,e_{jt}]_{\ell^{\prime}}+h_{st}[\ell,m]_{\ell^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}h_{st}\left[\frac{\ell}{\ell^{\prime}}\right],&\text{if }\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell,\\
\sum_{j}h_{sj}\left[\frac{e_{jt}}{\ell}\right]+h_{st}\left[\frac{-m/\ell}{\ell}\right],&\text{if }\ell^{\prime}=\ell,\end{cases}

recall that
e_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
describes the local Kummer map
\kappa_{\ell}
.

We fix
s,t
, let
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{1}
varies,
and study the behavior of (
2.3
)
and (
2.4
).
Clearly, the (
2.4
) is represented by the
Rédei matrix
D_{d}+h_{st}(A^{\mathrm{T}}+D_{-q})\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{k\times k}

with
d=\sum_{j}h_{sj}e_{jt}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
here the group operation on
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
is written additively.

In the following we prove that
the (
2.3
) is represented by a Rédei matrix
whose entries are linear combinations of
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
independent of
n
,
where
c,d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.
First we consider the map
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma_{1},2)\to H_{\Sigma}^{1}

given by
\ell\mapsto x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}}=({\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}|_{H_{\Sigma}^{1}})^{-1}({\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))_{1}^{\prime})
for
\ell\in\Sigma_{1}
.
It is the composition of the map

{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma_{1},2)\to V_{\Sigma}
,

\ell\mapsto{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}(\lambda_{\ell}(P))={\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}((1,\cdots,1,\ell,1,\cdots,1)^{\mathrm{T}})

where the
\ell
is at the
s
-th row,
and the map
V_{\Sigma}=U_{\Sigma}\oplus K_{\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow U_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\Sigma}^{1}
,

z\mapsto({\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma}|_{H_{\Sigma}^{1}})^{-1}(z_{1}^{\prime})
.
Clearly the first map is represented by a Rédei matrix
in
{\mathrm{RM}}_{4\#\Sigma\times k}
,
and the second map is represented by a matrix in
M_{2\#\Sigma\times 4\#\Sigma}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

independent of
\Sigma_{1}
.
Hence the map
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma_{1},2)\to H_{\Sigma}^{1}
,

\ell\mapsto x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}}
is given by a Rédei matrix
in
{\mathrm{RM}}_{2\#\Sigma\times k}
, and for
i=1,2
,
the map
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma_{1},2)\to{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,

\ell\mapsto\pi_{i}(x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}})
is given by a Rédei matrix

B_{i}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{\#\Sigma\times k}
.
From this it’s easy to that the bilinear map

{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma_{1},2)\times{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma_{1},2)\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,

(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\mapsto e_{\ell^{\prime}}\big({\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(x_{1,\Sigma}^{\mathrm{glob}}),y\big)
given by
(
2.3
)
is represented by the Rédei matrix

\sum_{i}h_{it}B_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}Z_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{T}}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{k\times k}

where
Z_{\Sigma}:=(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{k\times\#\Sigma}
.
It is clearly a linear combination of
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
.

2.2.
The
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups

Swinnerton-Dyer’s alternating matrix approach in
[
SD08
]

is not so good to handle
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups,
but only modified
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups.
In this section we compare them.
To do this, we use the interpretation (
2.1
) directly.
The result is similar to Theorem
2.8
,
with a Rédei matrix which is not alternating,
but with simpler low-rank parts.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
E:y^{2}=x(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})
be an elliptic curve, where
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Fix a choice
P_{1}=(e_{1},0)
and
P_{2}=(0,0)

of the basis of
E[2]
.
Let
\Sigma
be containing
-1
all primes dividing
2e_{1}e_{2}(e_{1}-e_{2})
.
Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of square-free integers,
and
q\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
be the
\Sigma
-part of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Then there exists an integer
a
and a Rédei matrix

B\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(2k+a)\times(2k+2\cdot\#\Sigma)}
depending only on
E
and
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
of form

B=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}P&Q\\
\hline\cr R&S\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}A+D_{e_{1}q}&D_{e_{1}e_{2}}&Z_{\Sigma}\\
D_{e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})}&A+D_{-e_{1}q}&&Z_{\Sigma}\\
\hline\cr R_{1}&R_{2}&S_{1}&S_{2}\end{array}\right),

where
Z_{\Sigma}:=(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{k\times\#\Sigma}
,
such that
for any
m=qn\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, where

n=\ell_{1}\cdots\ell_{k}\in S(\Sigma)
the prime-to-
\Sigma
part of
m
,
under the map
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})\to({\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k})^{\oplus 2}\oplus({\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Sigma})^{\oplus 2}

which maps

x=(x^{(1)};x^{(2)})
to
((\ord_{\ell_{i}}(x^{(1)}))_{i=1}^{k};(\ord_{\ell_{i}}(x^{(2)}))_{i=1}^{k};(\ord_{p}(x^{(1)}))_{p\in\Sigma};(\ord_{p}(x^{(2)}))_{p\in\Sigma})
,
we have

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})=\ker(B(n))
.

If
\pi:E[2]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

is given by
P_{1}=(e_{1},0)\mapsto 0
and
P_{2}=(0,0)\mapsto 1
,
then there are
B_{1}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(2k+a)\times(k+\#\Sigma)}

and
B_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(2k+a)\times(k+2\cdot\#\Sigma)}

such that for any above
m=qn
,

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})=\ker(B_{1}(n))

and
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})=\ker(B_{1}^{\prime}(n))
,
where
B_{1}
and
B_{1}^{\prime}
are of form

B_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}A+D_{e_{1}q}&Z_{\Sigma}\\
D_{e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})}\\
\hline\cr R_{1}&S_{1}\end{array}\right),\qquad B_{1}^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cc}A+D_{e_{1}q}&Z_{\Sigma}\\
D_{e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})}&&Z_{\Sigma}\\
\hline\cr R_{1}&S_{1}&S_{2}\end{array}\right).

For other
\pi
the results are similar.

Proof.

Write
\Sigma_{1}=\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{k}\}

and
S=\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma_{1}
,
then
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
consists of elements

x=(x^{(1)};x^{(2)})
of
{\mathbb{Q}}(S,2)^{\oplus 2}
,
such that
{\mathrm{loc}}_{p}(x)\in\Im(\kappa_{p})
for all
p\in S
.

When
p\in\Sigma
,
it’s easy to see that the map
{\mathrm{loc}}_{p}:{\mathbb{Q}}(S,2)\to{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times})^{2}
,
under our choice of basis, is represented by a Rédei matrix
whose entries are
0,1
or
z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
,
d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.
The map
\big({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times})^{2}\big)^{\oplus 2}=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},E^{(m)}[2])\to H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},E^{(m)}[2])/\Im(\kappa_{p})

depends only on
E
and
{\mathfrak{X}}
, hence is represented by a fixed matrix.
Therefore, the condition
that
{\mathrm{loc}}_{p}(x)\in\Im(\kappa_{p})
for all
p\in\Sigma

can be represented by some
R,S
in
B
.

For
\ell=\ell_{i}\in\Sigma_{1}
, recall that

\kappa_{\ell}:{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\cdot\{P_{1},P_{2}\}=E[2]\cong E^{(m)}[2]\hookrightarrow\big({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})^{2}\big)^{\oplus 2}

is represented by the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{11}m&e_{12}\\
e_{21}&e_{22}m\end{smallmatrix}\right)

where

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{11}&e_{12}\\
e_{21}&e_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right):=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{1}e_{2}\\
e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})&-e_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
If we let
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
be any quadratic non-residue modulo
\ell
,
let
\{(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
0\end{smallmatrix}),(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
a\end{smallmatrix}),(\begin{smallmatrix}\ell\\
0\end{smallmatrix}),(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\ell\end{smallmatrix})\}

be a basis of
\big({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})^{2}\big)^{\oplus 2}
,
then it’s easy to see that

\Im(\kappa_{\ell})=\ker\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\left[\frac{e_{11}m/\ell}{\ell}\right]&\left[\frac{e_{12}}{\ell}\right]\\
0&1&\left[\frac{e_{21}}{\ell}\right]&\left[\frac{e_{22}m/\ell}{\ell}\right]\end{pmatrix}.

On the other hand, the map

{\mathrm{loc}}_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Q}}(S,2)\to{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times})^{2}={\mathbb{F}}_{2}\cdot\{a,\ell\}
is represented by

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc|c}\left[\frac{\ell_{1}}{\ell_{i}}\right]&\cdots&\left[\frac{\ell_{i-1}}{\ell_{i}}\right]&0&\left[\frac{\ell_{i+1}}{\ell_{i}}\right]&\cdots&\left[\frac{\ell_{k}}{\ell_{i}}\right]&\left(\left[\frac{p}{\ell_{i}}\right]\right)_{p\in\Sigma}\\
0&\cdots&0&1&0&\cdots&0&0\end{array}\right).

Now it’s easy to see that we can put these matrices
together for all
\ell\in\Sigma_{1}
, and
after reordering rows, we get Rédei matrices of the form
we want for
P,Q
in
B
.

For the
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups
for the above
\pi
,
recall that an element
x=(x^{(1)};x^{(2)})\in{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})

is contained in
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
if and only if

x^{(2)}=0
,
and is contained in

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
if and only if

{\mathrm{loc}}_{\Sigma_{1}}(x^{(2)})=0
.
Hence the form of
B_{1}
and
B_{1}^{\prime}
is clear.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

By the same method one can prove a similar result for a
general
2
-adic Galois representation
with full rational
2
-torsion points.
Let
W=({\mathbb{Q}}_{2}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})^{a}
be endowed with a continuous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-linear

G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-action, unramified outside
\Sigma
,
such that the
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-action on
W[2]
is trivial.
For each
v\in\Sigma
, fix a local condition at
v

for the family
\{W^{(m)}\mid m\in{\mathfrak{X}}\}
(note that they are isomorphic
as
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}}
-modules), take unramified local condition at
v
for
v\notin\Sigma
,
define the
2
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(W^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
for
m\in{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Then there exist
b,d\geq 0
,
a Rédei matrix
B=(B_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq a+1\\
1\leq j\leq a+1\end{subarray}}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ak+d)}
, where

B_{ij}=D_{e_{ij}}
for
i,j\leq a
,
i\neq j
,

B_{ii}=A+D_{e_{ii}q}
for
i\leq a
, such that

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(W^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})=\corank(B(n))
for all
m\in{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Here
e_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
is such that
the
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
action
\rho:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to{\mathrm{Aut}}(W[4])={\mathrm{GL}}_{a}({\mathbb{Z}}/4{\mathbb{Z}})
on
W[4]

is given by
\rho=1+(\chi_{e_{ij}})

where
\chi_{e_{ij}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to 2{\mathbb{Z}}/4{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\{\pm 1\}

is the quadratic character associated to
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{e_{ij}})/{\mathbb{Q}}
.
The above theorem can be viewed as a special case:

a=2
,
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{11}&e_{12}\\
e_{21}&e_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{1}e_{2}\\
e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})&-e_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Now it’s clear from linear algebra that we have the following comparison
of these two types of
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups.

Corollary 2.11
.

Under the setup of Theorem
2.9
,
suppose
\pi:E[2]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

is
P_{1}=(e_{1},0)\mapsto 0
and
P_{2}=(0,0)\mapsto 1
,
then if
e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})
is a perfect square and
if
m=qn
is such that
(z_{p}(n)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k})_{p\in\Sigma}

are linearly independent, then

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})={\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(m)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
For other
\pi
there are similar results.

By Proposition
3.11

it’s expected that for most
n
, the

(z_{p}(n)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k})_{p\in\Sigma}

are linearly independent,
hence if
e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})
is a perfect square,
then it’s expected that for most
n
, these two types of
\pi
-strict

2
-Selmer groups are equal, so Theorem
1.9

should comes from the similar result
for modified
\pi
-strict

2
-Selmer groups, which can be studied by Theorem

2.2
