using alternating Rédei matrices.
We can’t say much for now when

e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})
is not a perfect square; later in §
3.3

we will see that
in this case, these two types of
\pi
-strict

2
-Selmer groups are expected to be trivial,
corresponding to
t_{\pi}=-\infty
case in Theorem
1.9
.

3.
Markov analysis

Recall that (Theorem
2.2
,

2.8
,
2.9
and
Corollary
2.3
)
for a
\Sigma
-equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}
of quadratic twist family of elliptic curves
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with full rational
2
-torsion points,
the dimension of
2
-Selmer group of
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}

is equal to the corank of
B(E)=B(n)
where
n\in S(\Sigma)

is the product of bad primes of
E
outside
\Sigma
;
similarly for
\pi
-strict
2
-Selmer groups

{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
and
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E/{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Later (in Theorem
3.2

and Corollary
3.3
)
we will see that the matrix
\widetilde{B}
and
\widetilde{B}_{i}^{\prime}
in
Corollary
2.3

for essential
2
-Selmer groups

S(E)

and modified
\pi
-strict essential
2
-Selmer groups

S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E)

have better Markov behavior
as the number
k\geq 1
of the prime factors of
n
varies.
In this section we will do the Markov analysis on such matrices.

For each
k\geq 1
and each element
\omega=\big((a_{ij}),(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}

as in (
2.2
),
we define the following matrices and vectors of coefficients in
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

associated to
\omega
.

•

Define
A^{\prime}=A^{\prime}(\omega):=(a_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k-1\\
1\leq j\leq k-1\end{subarray}}\in M_{k-1}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.

•

For
d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
, define
z_{d}^{\prime}=z_{d}^{\prime}(\omega):=(z_{1}^{(d)},\cdots,z_{k-1}^{(d)})^{\mathrm{T}}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}
.
The
(z_{d}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}}
is the transpose of
z_{d}^{\prime}
.
Define
D_{d}^{\prime}:=\diag(z_{d}^{\prime})\in M_{k-1}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

and
A_{d}^{\prime}:=A^{\prime}+z_{d}^{\prime}(z_{d}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}}\in M_{k-1}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.

In other words,
A^{\prime}
,
A_{d}^{\prime}
and
z_{c}^{\prime}(z_{d}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}}

are the corresponding
A
,
A_{d}
and
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}
with
last row and column removed;
z_{d}^{\prime}
is the corresponding
z_{d}^{\prime}
with
last element removed.
Unlike
A
, the restriction

\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{ij}=0
does not affect
A^{\prime}
, hence the
A
,
z_{d}
, etc. are
also called
restricted
Rédei matrices;
the
A^{\prime}
,
z_{d}^{\prime}
, etc. are
called
unrestricted
Rédei matrices.
The set of unrestricted Rédei matrices of size
(a(k-1)+b)\times(c(k-1)+d)

is denoted by
{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(a(k-1)+b)\times(c(k-1)+d)}
.
Similar to restricted Rédei matrices of size
k\times k
,
the unrestricted Rédei matrices of size
(k-1)\times(k-1)

are also divided into
3
types according to their behavior of ranks,
and also have decompositions by ranks.

When
E
varies in a
\Sigma
-equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
the product
n\in S(\Sigma)
of bad primes of
E
outside
\Sigma

also varies in a
\Sigma
-equivalence class,
as in Theorem
2.8

and Theorem
2.9
.
Here
n_{1},n_{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}/({\mathbb{Q}}^{\times})^{2}
are called
\Sigma
-equivalent to each
other, if
n_{1}/n_{2}\in({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times})^{2}
for all
p\in\Sigma
.
It’s clear that to give a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of
S(\Sigma)

is equivalent to give
\mathbf{s}=(s_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Sigma}
,
by

(3.1)

S^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma):=\left\{n\in S(\Sigma)~\middle|~\left[\frac{p}{n}\right]=s_{p}\text{ for all }p\in\Sigma\right\}\subset S(\Sigma).

Correspondingly, define

\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}:=\left\{\omega=\big((a_{ij}),(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}~\middle|~\sum_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}^{(p)}=s_{p}\text{ for all }p\in\Sigma\right\}\subset\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}.

The map
S_{k}(\Sigma)\to\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}
,
n\mapsto\omega(n)

restricts to
S_{k}^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma)\to\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
.

In this section we study the distribution of
\corank(B(\omega))

as
k\geq 1
,
\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
varies,
for the following unrestricted Rédei matrix
B
,
which is the abstraction of the matrix
\widetilde{B}
in Corollary

2.3
.

Definition 3.1
.

A “high-rank alternating Rédei matrix of level
2
”
is a Rédei matrix
B=(B_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq 3\\
1\leq j\leq 3\end{subarray}}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+t)\times(2(k-1)+t)}
where

•

B
is alternating, namely
B_{ji}=B_{ij}^{\mathrm{T}}

and the diagonal of
B_{ii}
is
0
,

•

B_{11},B_{12},B_{21},B_{22}
are of size
(k-1)\times(k-1)
,

B_{13},B_{23}
are of size
(k-1)\times t
for some fixed integer
t
independent of
k
,
and
B_{33}
is of size
t\times t
,

•

the entries of
B_{13}
and
B_{23}
are
z_{d}^{\prime}
,
d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{13}\\
B_{23}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is of generically full rank,
namely, there exists
k\geq 1
and
\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}

such that

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{13}(\omega)\\
B_{23}(\omega)\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is of rank
t
,

•

B_{12}
and
B_{21}
are high-rank Rédei matrices;
the three matrices
B_{11}
,
B_{22}
and
B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}

are all medium-rank or low-rank Rédei matrices.

Suppose the medium-rank part of
B_{11}
and
B_{22}
are
D_{d_{1}}^{\prime}

and
D_{d_{2}}^{\prime}
, respectively,
d_{1},d_{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.
Then the medium-rank part of
B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}

is
D_{-d_{1}d_{2}}^{\prime}
.
Such
B
is classified, roughly, according to the number
s

of the low-rank Rédei matrices among
B_{11}
,
B_{22}
and
B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}
,
namely, number
s
of
1
among
d_{1},d_{2},-d_{1}d_{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
as the following types:

(A)

s=0
,
(d_{1},d_{2})\neq(-1,-1)
;

(A’)

s=0
,
(d_{1},d_{2})=(-1,-1)
;

(B)

s=1
;

(C)

s=2
.

Note that the matrix coming from elliptic curves over
{\mathbb{Q}}
in Corollary

2.3

cannot be of type (A’), since from Theorem
2.8
,

d_{1}=e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})
and
d_{2}=e_{1}e_{2}
, so
d_{1}+d_{2}=e_{1}^{2}>0
.
In this paper we only study such
B
of types (A), (B) or (C),
but let’s give a remark on type (A’)
compared to type (A): the
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}&B_{11}+B_{22}\\
B_{12}+B_{21}+B_{22}&B_{11}+B_{12}+B_{21}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is a low-rank Rédei matrix for type (A’),
while it’s a medium-rank Rédei matrix for type (A),
which causes extra difficulty similar to types (B) or (C)
compared to type (A).

We introduce crucial invariants for
B
of type (B) and (C).
Similar to Definition
2.4
,
if
B
is of type (B), we may assume that
B_{11}
is low-rank Rédei matrix
(namely
d_{1}=1
and
d_{2}\neq 1
);
if
B
is of type (C), we may assume that
B_{11}
and
B_{12}

are low-rank Rédei matrices
(namely
d_{1}=d_{2}=1
).
Define

B_{j}^{\prime}:=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1j}&B_{13}\\
B_{2j}&B_{23}\\
B_{3j}&B_{33}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{j}^{\prime\prime}:=\begin{pmatrix}B_{jj}&B_{j3}\\
B_{3j}&B_{33}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{for }1\leq j\leq s,

corresponding to
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
in
Corollary
2.3
and
Definition
2.4

(we may also define them for
j=3
case, similar to that
in Theorem
2.2
),
then we define the parameter
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})
of
B
,
which is an unordered tuple of
s
integers, to be

t_{j}:=t-\max_{k\geq 1,\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}\rank(B_{j}^{\prime\prime}(\omega))\qquad\text{for }1\leq j\leq s.

The
B_{j}^{\prime\prime}
is a low-rank Rédei matrix,
whose properties are summarized in §
3.2
,
most importantly, the number
t_{j}
is easily computable
by Proposition
3.11
.

The main result in this section is the following,
which is related to Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 3.2
.

The random variables

Y_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to I:={\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{s+1},\qquad\omega\mapsto\big(\corank(B(\omega)),\corank(B_{1}^{\prime}(\omega)),\cdots,\corank(B_{s}^{\prime}(\omega))\big)

satisfy that for all
k\geq 1
,

(3.2)

\sum_{\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}}\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k+1}=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}})-\sum_{\mathbf{m}}P_{\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k},

where
0<\alpha<1
and
C>0
are constants independent of

\mathbf{m}
,
\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}
and
k
,
namely, they
form an almost Markov chain, whose limit transition matrix
is
P_{\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}=P_{\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}},\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}

for the model
M_{2k+t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

in Appendix
A.2
.
In particular, the random variables
Y_{k}
and

X_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,

\omega\mapsto\corank(B(\omega))
satisfy

(3.3)

\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})=P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(\mathbf{m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)=P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m),

and moreover, for any
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}

there exists
C>0
and
0<\alpha<1
such that for all
k\geq 1
,

(3.4)

\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)-P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)\right|\leq\sum_{\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})\in I}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})-P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(\mathbf{m})\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k}.

Proof.

The (
3.2
) comes from Theorem

3.4
,
the fact that the range of
\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})
is

0\leq m_{j}^{\prime}\leq m\leq 2m_{j}^{\prime}-t_{j}

for
1\leq j\leq s
(same as that in Proposition
2.5
),
and the fact that there are at most
3\cdot 4^{s}
choices of

\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}
when
\mathbf{m}
is fixed
(vice versa).
Now Theorem
A.3
(ii) implies the (
3.3
).
As for (
3.4
), we only need to prove
\xi\geq 1
case, which
comes from Theorem
A.5
(ii),
taking
\nu:I\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
to be

\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})\mapsto 2^{\xi\cdot m}
,
utilizing an upper bound of
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)
in Corollary
3.5
.
∎

The above result for type (A) is proved in
[
SD08
]

in terms of linear algebra and vector spaces
(i.e. without choosing a basis and written as matrix form),
also proved in
[
Smi16
]
in the matrix form.
The advantage of matrix form instead of pure linear algebra
becomes clearer for types (B) and (C):
in these types it’s easy to spot the low-rank submatrices
B_{j}^{\prime\prime}

of
B
(which cause difficulty in the analysis)
when written as matrix form.

Summing up the relevant terms in (
3.2
),
the same argument allows us to deduce the following result,
which is related to the
t_{\pi}\neq-\infty
case of Theorem
1.9
.

Corollary 3.3
.

For
1\leq j\leq s
, the random variables

Z_{j,k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,

\omega\mapsto\corank(B_{j}^{\prime}(\omega))
satisfy the similar results:
for all
k\geq 1
,

(3.5)

\sum_{m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{new}}}\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{j,k+1}=m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{new}})-\sum_{m^{\prime}}P_{m^{\prime}\to m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{j,k}=m^{\prime})\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k},

where
0<\alpha<1
and
C>0
are constants independent of

m^{\prime}
,
m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{new}}
and
k
,
namely, they
form an almost Markov chain, whose limit transition matrix
is
P_{m^{\prime}\to m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}=P_{m^{\prime}\to m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{new}},t_{j}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}

for the model
M_{(k-t_{j})\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

in Appendix.
In particular, they satisfy

(3.6)

\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{j,k}=m^{\prime})=P_{t_{j}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(m^{\prime}),

and moreover, for any
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}

there exists
C>0
and
0<\alpha<1
such that for all
k\geq 1
,

(3.7)

\sum_{m^{\prime}\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m^{\prime}}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{j,k}=m^{\prime})-P_{t_{j}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(m^{\prime})\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k}.

To prove Theorem
3.2
,
we use an isomorphism

\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k(k-1)/2}\times({\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})^{\#\Sigma},\qquad\omega\mapsto\big((a_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq k-1},(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}\big).

This induces a natural projection
\Omega_{k+1}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\twoheadrightarrow\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
.
Under this projection, the behavior of
A^{\prime}
,
z_{d}^{\prime}
and
D_{d}^{\prime}
are better than
A
,
z_{d}
and
D_{d}
.
Define
\Omega_{\infty}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}:=\varprojlim\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

and view
\{Y_{k}\}
as a sequence of random variables on
\Omega_{\infty}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
.
The computation of the transition probability

{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k+1}=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})
is very
similar to that in Theorem
A.6
,
but more ingredients are needed:

•

Analysis involving low-rank Rédei matrix.

•

How to compute the probability (Lemma
3.6
),
which is also used in
[
Smi16
]
.

3.1.
Change of corank from
k
to
k+1

The projection
\Omega_{k+1}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\twoheadrightarrow\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
has a splitting of probability spaces:

\Omega_{k+1}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}=\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\times\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\quad\text{where}\quad\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}:=\left\{\big(u_{1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1},a_{kk}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2},(z_{k}^{(p)}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)\right\}={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Sigma},

here
u_{1}:=(a_{ik})_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}
.
Denote
u_{2}:=(a_{ki})_{1\leq i\leq k-1}=u_{1}+z_{k}^{(-1)}z_{-1}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}
.
If
\omega_{\mathrm{new}}
is an element of
\Omega_{k+1}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
, we may write

\omega_{\mathrm{new}}=(\omega,\omega_{\Delta})
according to the above decomposition,
and we say that “
\omega_{\mathrm{new}}
comes from
\omega
”.

Let
B=(B_{ij})
be a
“high-rank alternating Rédei matrix of level
2
”
in Definition
3.1
.
Let
\omega
and
\omega_{\mathrm{new}}
be as above, with
\omega
fixed.
By abuse of notation, write
B=B(\omega)
and
B_{\mathrm{new}}=B(\omega_{\mathrm{new}})
,
similarly for
B_{ij}
and
B_{ij,{\mathrm{new}}}
.
Then

B_{ii,{\mathrm{new}}}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{ii}&v_{ii}\\
v_{ii}^{\mathrm{T}}&0\end{pmatrix}~(i=1,2),\quad B_{12,{\mathrm{new}}}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{12}&v_{12}\\
v_{21}^{\mathrm{T}}&v_{7}\end{pmatrix},\quad B_{i3,{\mathrm{new}}}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{i3}\\
v_{3i}^{\mathrm{T}}\end{pmatrix}~(i=1,2),

and

B_{\mathrm{new}}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc|cc}B_{11}&B_{12}&B_{13}&v_{11}&v_{12}\\
B_{21}&B_{22}&B_{23}&v_{21}&v_{22}\\
B_{31}&B_{32}&B_{33}&v_{31}&v_{32}\\
\hline\cr v_{11}^{\mathrm{T}}&v_{21}^{\mathrm{T}}&v_{31}^{\mathrm{T}}&0&v_{7}\\
v_{12}^{\mathrm{T}}&v_{22}^{\mathrm{T}}&v_{32}^{\mathrm{T}}&v_{7}&0\\
\end{array}\right),

where
v_{11},v_{22}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}
,

v_{31},v_{32}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{t}

denend only on
(z_{k}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}
;

v_{12},v_{21}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}

depend only on
u_{1}
and
(z_{k}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}
;
and
v_{7}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

depends only on
u_{1}
,
a_{kk}
and
(z_{k}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}
.
If the precise forms of
B_{ij}
are given (for example in
Theorem
2.8
),
it’s easy to write down the explicit formula of the map

\big(u_{1},a_{kk},(z_{k}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)\mapsto(v_{11},v_{12},v_{21},v_{22},v_{31},v_{32},v_{7})
.
But this is not necessary in our argument.
We only need the following.

•

The map

(3.8)

{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Sigma}\to({\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})^{4}\times({\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{t})^{2},\qquad\big(u_{1},(z_{k}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)\mapsto(v_{11},v_{12},v_{21},v_{22},v_{31},v_{32})

is a linear map.
It maps
(u_{1},0)
to
(0,u_{1},u_{1},0,0,0)
.

•

If
u_{1}
and
(z_{k}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}
are fixed, then the map
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,

a_{kk}\mapsto v_{7}
is bijective.

This is the “Markov chain nature” of
B\to B_{\mathrm{new}}

(compare with corresponding part in Appendix).

To compute the transition probability

{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k+1}=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})
, an
important simplification comes from low-rank Rédei matrix.
It turns out that the following event
(i.e. subset of
\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
) is important:

(3.9)

F:=\{\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\mid(z_{p}^{\prime}(\omega)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})_{p\in\Sigma}\text{ are linearly independent}\}.

For example, the event
F
implies that

(z_{p}(\omega)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k})_{p\in\Sigma}
are also linearly independent,
which we already seen as a condition of Corollary
2.11
.
It’s clear that

{\mathbb{P}}(F)=\prod_{i=1}^{\#\Sigma}(1-2^{i-k})\geq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{\#\Sigma}2^{i-k}=1-2\cdot(2^{\#\Sigma}-1)\cdot 2^{-k}=1-C\cdot 2^{-k}

with constant
C=2\cdot(2^{\#\Sigma}-1)>0

depending only on
\#\Sigma
.
That is to say, for almost all
\omega
, the

(z_{p}^{\prime}(\omega)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})_{p\in\Sigma}
are linearly independent.
The relation of the event
F
and low-rank Rédei matrices
is via Proposition
3.11
.
Recall that we have defined
B_{j}^{\prime\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{jj}&B_{j3}\\
B_{3j}&B_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for
1\leq j\leq s
,
which are of low-rank.
Let
\rho_{j}:=\rho(B_{j}^{\prime\prime})
be the maximal possible rank of
B_{j}^{\prime\prime}
.
We assumed that
(B_{31},B_{32})
is generically full rank,
which means that its maximal possible rank is just
t
.
So by Proposition
3.11
, the event
F
implies that

\rank(B_{31},B_{32})=t\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rank(B_{j}^{\prime\prime})=\rho_{j}\text{ for }1\leq j\leq s.

Theorem
3.2

is proved via proving the following result.

Theorem 3.4
.

For any elements
\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})

and
\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}=(m_{\mathrm{new}},m_{1,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime})
of

I:={\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{s+1}
,
and any
k\geq 1
, the transition probability

{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k+1}=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})
is zero unless

m_{\mathrm{new}}-m\in\{0,\pm 2\}
and
m_{i,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime}-m_{i}^{\prime}\in\{-2,-1,0,1\}
for all
i
;
for such
\mathbf{m}
and
\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}
we have

\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k+1}=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})-P_{\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}\right|\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})<C\cdot\big(\alpha^{m+k}+{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\text{ and not }F)\big),

where
0<\alpha<1
and
C>0
are constants independent of

\mathbf{m}
,
\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}
and
k
.
Note that
P_{\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}=0

if
m_{i,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime}-m_{i}^{\prime}\notin\{0,\pm 1\}
for some
i
.

Corollary 3.5
.

For any
k\geq 1
,
m\geq 0
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}\geq m)<C\cdot 2^{-\alpha m^{2}}
,
where
C>0
and
\alpha>0
are constants independent of
m
and
k
.

To see why the event
F
(
3.9
) makes simplifications,
note that a priori
\rank(B^{\prime}_{i,{\mathrm{new}}})-\rank(B_{i}^{\prime})\in\{0,1,2,3\}
,
but under the event
F
, for type (B) and (C),
we must have
(v_{11};v_{31})\in\operatorname{span}(B_{1}^{\prime\prime})
,
in particular,
\rank(B^{\prime}_{1,{\mathrm{new}}})-\rank(B_{1}^{\prime})\in\{0,1,2\}
;
and for type (C),
(v_{22};v_{32})\in\operatorname{span}(B_{2}^{\prime\prime})
,
in particular,
\rank(B^{\prime}_{2,{\mathrm{new}}})-\rank(B_{2}^{\prime})\in\{0,1,2\}
.
Therefore, to compute transition probabilities up to suitable error term,
we only need to compute

{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k+1}\in J\times\prod_{i=1}^{s}J_{i}^{\prime}\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})
for all
J,J_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,J_{s}^{\prime}
,
where
J={\mathbb{Z}},\{m+2\}
or
\{m-2\}
,
and for
i=1,\cdots,s
,
J_{i}^{\prime}={\mathbb{Z}},\{m_{i}^{\prime}+1\}
or
\{m_{i}^{\prime}-1\}
;
we may also assume the event
F
happens,
which makes the linear algebra criteria simpler.

Let’s look at an example

{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{\mathrm{new}})=m+2\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)
,
corresponding to
J=\{m+2\}
and
J_{i}^{\prime}={\mathbb{Z}}
for all
i
.
Let
\Omega=\{\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\mid Y_{k}(\omega)=\mathbf{m}\}
.
For each
\omega_{\mathrm{new}}=(\omega,\omega_{\Delta})
with
\omega\in\Omega
,
let
w_{i}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}v_{1i}\\
v_{2i}\\
v_{3i}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for
i=1,2
.
Then

{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{\mathrm{new}})=m+2\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{1}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\text{ and }w_{2}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big).

Suppose
\omega\in\Omega
is fixed.
Let
W=W_{\omega}
consists of all possible
w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
as
\omega_{\Delta}
varies,
namely, the image of the map (
3.8
).
Let
V=V_{\omega}
be
\operatorname{span}(B)\oplus\operatorname{span}(B)
.
Let
\widetilde{V}={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{2(2(k-1)+t)}
which contains
V_{\omega}
and
W_{\omega}

for all
\omega
.
Then

{\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{1}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\text{ and }w_{2}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)={\mathbb{P}}\big(w\in V_{\omega}\mid\omega\in\Omega,w\in W_{\omega}\big).

In fact, the computation of other transition
probabilities also involves the computation of
the probabilities of forms
{\mathbb{P}}\big(w\in V_{\omega}\mid\omega\in\Omega,w\in W_{\omega}\big)
.
As another example,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{\mathrm{new}})=m-2\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{1}\notin\operatorname{span}(B)\text{ and }w_{2}\notin\operatorname{span}(B)\text{ and }w_{1}+w_{2}\notin\operatorname{span}(B)\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)

\displaystyle=1-{\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{1}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)-{\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{2}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)-{\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{1}+w_{2}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}+2\cdot{\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{1}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\text{ and }w_{2}\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big).

Error terms of the form
O(1)\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(\text{not }F\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})
may be introduced if we utilize the event
F
. For example, in type (B),
under the event
F
, the
(v_{11};v_{31})\in\operatorname{span}((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})
is always true,
hence

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{1,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime})=m_{1}^{\prime}+1\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}\big((v_{11};v_{31})\in\operatorname{span}((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})\text{ and }w_{1}\in\operatorname{span}(B_{1}^{\prime})\text{ and }(v_{12};v_{32})\in\operatorname{span}((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}\big(w_{1}\in\operatorname{span}(B_{1}^{\prime})\text{ and }(v_{12};v_{32})\in\operatorname{span}((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)+O(1)\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(\text{not }F\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}),

where
O(1)
is bounded independent of
k
and
\mathbf{m}
.

To compute
{\mathbb{P}}\big(w\in V_{\omega}\mid\omega\in\Omega,w\in W_{\omega}\big)
,
we have the following simple linear algebra lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\widetilde{V}
be a fixed
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
-vector space of finite dimensional.
Let
(\Omega,{\mathbb{P}})
be a discrete probability space,
such that each element
\omega
of
\Omega
produces
subspaces
V_{\omega}
and
W_{\omega}

of
\widetilde{V}
.
Then

{\mathbb{P}}\big(w\in V_{\omega}\mid\omega\in\Omega,w\in W_{\omega}\big)=\sum_{h\in\Hom(\widetilde{V},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\\
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0\\
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}.

Proof.

Denote by
P
the left hand side.
Clearly

P=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)\cdot{\mathbb{P}}\big(w\in V_{\omega}\mid w\in W_{\omega}\big)=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)\cdot\frac{\#(V_{\omega}\cap W_{\omega})}{\#W_{\omega}}.

On the other hand,
for
\omega\in\Omega
,
we have

\frac{\#(V_{\omega}\cap W_{\omega})}{\#W_{\omega}}=\frac{\#V_{\omega}}{\#(V_{\omega}+W_{\omega})}=\frac{\#(\widetilde{V}/(V_{\omega}+W_{\omega}))}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}=\frac{\#\Hom(\widetilde{V}/(V_{\omega}+W_{\omega}),{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}.

This yields the desired result.
∎

This is the key formula which we will use later.
The conditions
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0

and
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0
may be rewritten as
h
lies in the left
kernel of a matrix whose columns generate
V_{\omega}+W_{\omega}
.
The key point is that,
when we take
\Omega=\{\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\mid Y_{k}(\omega)=\mathbf{m}\}
,
we may decompose
\Hom(\widetilde{V},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})

into disjoint subsets, such that one of them produces
the “main term” of
P
, which has a simple form
independent of
k
, and the remaining
part of them produce the “error terms” of
P
, which is

O(\alpha^{k})\cdot\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}
as
k\to\infty
for some
0<\alpha<1
.
To give an upper bound of “error terms”,
we may replace
W_{\omega}
by its suitable subspace,
which makes the computation simpler.

As an example, let’s back to

{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{\mathrm{new}})=m+2\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{h\in\Hom(\widetilde{V},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\\
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0\\
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}

with
\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})=2^{2m}
.
Since elements of
\widetilde{V}
is written as column vectors

w=(v_{11};v_{21};v_{31};v_{12};v_{22};v_{32})
,
we may write
h\in\Hom(\widetilde{V},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})

as a row vector
h=(h_{11},h_{21},h_{31},h_{12},h_{22},h_{32})
,
and the
h(w)
is just the product of a row vector with a column vector.
Recall the property of the map (
3.8
),
which implies that

\{(0;u_{1};0;u_{1};0;0)\mid u_{1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}\}
is a subspace of
W_{\omega}

for all
\omega
, so a necessary condition for
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0

is that
h_{12}=h_{21}
.

Consider type (B) as an example,
which is
d_{1}=1
and
d_{2}\neq 1
.
It turns out that
the
\{(h_{12},h_{22},h_{32})=0\}
gives the main term,
in this case the
h|_{V_{\omega}+W_{\omega}}=0
is equivalent to

(h_{11},h_{31})((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}},B_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})=0,

where
B_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a matrix whose columns
are
(v_{11};v_{31})
as
(z_{k}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}

runs over a basis of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Sigma}
,
similar to the map (
3.8
).
Note that
(B_{1}^{\prime\prime},B_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})
is a submatrix of

B_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{\omega})
for certain

\widetilde{\omega}\in\Omega_{k+\#\Sigma}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
,
so under the event
F
(
3.9
),
we have
\rank(B_{1}^{\prime\prime},B_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})=\rho_{1}
,
and similarly,
since
B_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is a submatrix of
B_{1}^{\prime}
,
we have
\rank((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}},B_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})=\rank(B_{1}^{\prime})
.
On the other hand, if the event
F
does not hold, then

\rank((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}},B_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})\geq\rank(B_{1}^{\prime})
.
Therefore the main term for type (B) is

(3.10)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\Hom(\widetilde{V},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\\
(h_{12},h_{22},h_{32})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\\
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0\\
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}\cdot 2^{k-1+t-\rank((B_{1}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}},B_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=2^{-1-2m}\left({\mathbb{P}}(F\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})\cdot 2^{m_{1}^{\prime}}+{\mathbb{P}}(\text{not }F\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})\cdot O(1)\cdot 2^{m_{1}^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{m_{1}^{\prime}-1-2m}+O(1)\cdot\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot 2^{m_{1}^{\prime}-2m}\cdot 2^{-k},

where
O(1)
is bounded independent of
k
and
\mathbf{m}
.

The
\{(h_{12},h_{22},h_{32})\neq 0\}
gives the error term; for the estimation of upper bound,
we replace
W_{\omega}
by its subspace
\{(0;u_{1};0;u_{1};0;0)\mid u_{1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}\}
,
and we are going to prove that

(3.11)

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\Hom(\widetilde{V},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\\
(h_{12},h_{22},h_{32})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\\
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0\\
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}\leq C\cdot\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot 2^{-2m}\cdot(3/4)^{k}

for some constant
C>0
independent of
\mathbf{m}
and
k
.

To prove (
3.11
),
recall that we must have
h_{12}=h_{21}
, and in this case
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0

is equivalent to that

\begin{pmatrix}h_{11}&h_{12}&h_{31}\\
h_{12}&h_{22}&h_{32}\\
\end{pmatrix}B=0.

The cases are divided according to the dimension of

\operatorname{span}\{h_{11},h_{12},h_{22}\}
.

For example, consider the case
\dim(\operatorname{span}\{h_{11},h_{12},h_{22}\})=1
with

h_{22}\neq 0
,
h_{11}=h_{12}=0
.
Then the above condition becomes

\begin{pmatrix}0&0&h_{31}\\
0&h_{22}&h_{32}\\
\end{pmatrix}B=0,

which implies that

(3.12)

\displaystyle h_{22}B_{22}+h_{32}B_{32}

\displaystyle=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad h_{22}(D_{d_{2}}^{\prime}+B_{22,L})=h_{32}B_{32},

(3.13)

\displaystyle h_{22}B_{21}+h_{32}B_{31}

\displaystyle=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad h_{22}A^{\prime}=h_{22}(B_{21,M}+B_{21,L})+h_{32}B_{31},

(3.14)

\displaystyle h_{31}(B_{31},B_{32})

\displaystyle=0.

These three equations are three typical types of restrictions.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
1\neq d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(k-1)\times(k-1)}

be a low-rank Rédei matrix.
Let
(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}
varies.
Then the average order of
\ker(D_{d}^{\prime}+B)
is

\frac{1}{\#\{(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}\}}\sum_{(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}}\#\ker(D_{d}^{\prime}+B)\leq 2^{\rho(B)}(3/2)^{k-1}.

Proof.

We have
\#\{(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}\}=2^{(k-1)\cdot\#\Sigma}
,
and since
d\neq 1
, for each
0\leq m\leq k-1
,
there are
\binom{k-1}{m}2^{(k-1)\cdot(\#\Sigma-1)}
elements
in
\{(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}\}
such that
z_{d}^{\prime}
has exactly
m

non-zero entries.
For such elements,
\rank(D_{d}^{\prime})=m
and

\rank(D_{d}^{\prime}+B)\geq\rank(D_{d}^{\prime})-\rank(B)\geq m-\rho(B)
.
The desired inequality follows easily.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer,
X,Y\in M_{m\times(k-1)}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
be two fixed matrices, with

\rank(X)=r
.
Let
\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
with
(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}
fixed
and
(a_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq k-1}
varies.
Then

{\mathbb{P}}(XA^{\prime}=Y)\leq 2^{-rk+r(r+1)/2}.

Proof.

Write
X=(x_{ij})
and
Y=(y_{ij})
.
For each
i
let
S_{i}
be the set of
j
such that
x_{ij}\neq 0
.
By column operations on
X
and
Y
,
we may assume that there exists
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}

such that for each
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,r\}
,

\ell_{i}\in S_{j}
if and only if
i=j
.
Let
a_{ij}
(
1\leq i\leq j\leq k-1
)
be fixed except for those with
i\in\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\}

or
j\in\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\}
;
there are
rk-r(r+1)/2
of them.
They can be solved in at most one way as follows:

•

a_{\ell_{i}j}=y_{ij}+\sum_{k\in S_{i}\setminus\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\}}a_{kj}
for

i\in\{1,\cdots,r\}
and

j\in\{1,\cdots,k-1\}\setminus\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\}
,

•

a_{\ell_{i}\ell_{j}}=y_{i\ell_{j}}+\sum_{k\in S_{i}\setminus\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\}}a_{k\ell_{j}}
for

i,j\in\{1,\cdots,r\}
.

Now the result is clear.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{t\times u(k-1)}
be of generically full rank,

0\neq x\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{t}
be a row vector.
Then there exists
1\neq d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
depends only on
B
and
x
,
such that for any
\omega=(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}

satisfying
x\cdot B(\omega)=0
, we have
z_{d}^{\prime}(\omega)=0
.

Proof.

Write
B=((z_{d_{ij}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})_{1\leq i\leq t,1\leq j\leq u}

with
d_{ij}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
for
1\leq i\leq t,1\leq j\leq u
.
The
B
is of generically full rank means that
the
t
elements
(d_{11},\cdots,d_{1u}),\cdots,(d_{t1},\cdots,d_{tu})

in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)^{\oplus u}
are linearly independent.
Hence the element
x\cdot(d_{ij})
in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)^{\oplus u}

is non-trivial, say its
j
-th component is
d\neq 1
.
Now the result is clear since the
j
-th component of

x\cdot B(\omega)
is
z_{d}^{\prime}(\omega)
.
∎

To estimate the upper bound,

•

the first step is to estimate the number
of possible
(h,(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma})
;

•

the second step is that for each
(h,(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma})
, estimate the number
of possible
(a_{ij})
.

For the first step,

•

if
h_{31}=0
, apply Lemma
3.7

on (
3.12
)
(note that type (B) implies
d_{2}\neq 1
), obtain that
there is a constant
C>0
such that
the number of possible
(h,(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma})
is

\leq C\cdot 2^{(k-1)\cdot\#\Sigma}(3/2)^{k}
;

•

if
h_{31}\neq 0
, apply Lemma
3.9

on (
3.14
), obtain that
there is a constant
C>0
such that
the number of possible
(h,(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma})
is

\leq C\cdot 2^{k}\cdot 2^{(k-1)\cdot(\#\Sigma-1)}
.

The sum of these two is

\leq C\cdot 2^{(k-1)\cdot\#\Sigma}(3/2)^{k}
.

For the second step, apply Lemma
3.8

on (
3.13
), obtain that
there is a constant
C>0
such that
the number of possible
(a_{ij})
is

\leq C\cdot 2^{k(k-1)/2}\cdot 2^{-k}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\Hom(\widetilde{V},{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\\
h_{22}\neq 0,h_{11}=h_{12}=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\\
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0\\
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\frac{2^{-2m}}{\#\Omega}\cdot(2^{(k-1)\cdot\#\Sigma}(3/2)^{k})\cdot(2^{k(k-1)/2}\cdot 2^{-k})

\displaystyle=C\cdot\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot 2^{-2m}(3/4)^{k}.

The other cases of
h
are similar, also
using the above three types of restrictions.
Combine them we obtain the error term estimate (
3.11
).

Now we know that the transition probability

{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{\mathrm{new}})=m+2\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)
for type (B)
is the sum of (
3.10
) and (
3.11
),
on the other hand, from Appendix we know that for type (B),

P_{(m,m_{1}^{\prime})\to(m+2,*)}^{\mathrm{Alt}}=2^{m_{1}^{\prime}-1-2m}
,
hence from
m\leq 2m_{1}^{\prime}-t_{1}

it’s easy to obtain the inequality in Theorem
3.4
.
The same transition probability for types (A)
and (C) are similar.
For type (A), the
\{h=0\}
gives the main term, which is

2^{-1-2m}=P_{m\to m+2}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
;
for type (C), the
\{h_{12}=0\}
gives the main term, which is

2^{m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}^{\prime}-1-2m}+O(1)\cdot\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot 2^{m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}^{\prime}-2m}\cdot 2^{-k}

(note that
P_{(m,m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})\to(m+2,*)}^{\mathrm{Alt}}=2^{m_{1}^{\prime}+m_{2}^{\prime}-1-2m}
from Appendix).
The error term is the same as type (B), namely the right hand side of
(
3.11
).

To prove Theorem
3.4
,
there are
3^{s+1}
transition probabilities
need to be computed (there will be less when considering symmetry
and some obvious linear algebra restrictions),
including
{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{\mathrm{new}})=m+2\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)
we have just
computed.
They can be computed by the same method,
which are straightforward but lengthy.
We omit the details here.

3.2.
Low-rank Rédei matrix

Recall that we have already defined
a low-rank Rédei matrix of size
(k-1)\times(k-1)
.
For general sizes, we have the similar definition.

Definition 3.10
.

An unrestricted Rédei matrix
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(a(k-1)+b)\times(c(k-1)+d)}

is called a low-rank Rédei matrix, if its entries are
linear combinations of
0
,
1
,
z_{d}^{\prime}
,

(z_{d}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}}
and
z_{c}^{\prime}(z_{d}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}}
,

c,d\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.

It’s clear that if
B
is of low-rank,
then the maximal possible rank
\rho
of
B

\rho=\rho(B):=\max_{k\geq 1,\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}\rank(B(\omega))

is a finite number.

A main result for a low-rank Rédei matrix is that the probability
of its rank equal to its maximal possible rank tends to
1
as
k\to\infty
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
B
be a low-rank Rédei matrix.
Let
\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
be such that

(z_{p}^{\prime}(\omega)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})_{p\in\Sigma}
are linearly independent.
Then
\rank(B(\omega))=\rho
.
In particular, for all
k
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(\rank(B(\omega))=\rho\mid\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\big((z_{p}^{\prime}(\omega)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})_{p\in\Sigma}\text{ are linearly independent}\mid\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\#\Sigma}(1-2^{i-k})\geq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{\#\Sigma}2^{i-k}=1-2\cdot(2^{\#\Sigma}-1)\cdot 2^{-k}.

Proof.

We may write
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{smallmatrix}\right)

with
P=(P_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq s_{1},1\leq j\leq s_{2}}

where each
P_{ij}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(k-1)\times(k-1)}

is a fixed linear combination of
z_{c}^{\prime}(z_{d}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}}
,

Q=(z_{q_{ij}}^{\prime})_{1\leq i\leq s_{1},1\leq j\leq t_{2}}

and
R=((z_{r_{ij}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})_{1\leq i\leq t_{1},1\leq j\leq s_{2}}

where each
q_{ij}
and
r_{ij}
are fixed elements of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
,
and
S=(s_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq t_{1},1\leq j\leq t_{2}}\in M_{t_{1}\times t_{2}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})

is a fixed matrix.
We may impose the following assumptions.

•

We may assume that
P=(P_{ij})=0
.
In fact, we can add some new rows and columns to
B

and obtain the following matrix

\widetilde{B}=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q&\begin{pmatrix}Z_{\Sigma}^{\prime}\\
&\ddots\\
&&Z_{\Sigma}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\\
R&S\\
&&I\end{pmatrix},

here
Z_{\Sigma}^{\prime}:=(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(k-1)\times\#\Sigma}
,
and
I\in M_{(s_{1}\cdot\#\Sigma)\times(s_{1}\cdot\#\Sigma)}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
is the identity matrix.
It satisfies

\rank(\widetilde{B})=\rank(B)+s_{1}\cdot\#\Sigma
.
Utilizing newly added columns, by column operation it’s easy to see that
we can eliminate all the
P_{ij}
’s in
\widetilde{B}
.

•

By row and column operations, we may assume that

Q
and
R
are of “full rank” in the sense that

(q_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq s_{1}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)^{\oplus s_{1}}
,
j=1,\cdots,t_{2}

are linearly independent, as well as

(r_{ij})_{1\leq j\leq s_{2}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)^{\oplus s_{2}}
,
i=1,\cdots,t_{1}

are linearly independent.

By column operation
we may assume that the matrix
(q_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq s_{1},1\leq j\leq t_{2}}

is “block lower triangular” in the sense that we have a
decomposition
[t_{2}]=\bigsqcup_{\ell=1}^{s_{1}}J_{\ell}
,
such that the submatrix
(q_{ij})_{j\in J_{\ell}}
is zero for
i<\ell
,
and the elements in the submatrix
(q_{ij})_{j\in J_{i}}
are linearly independent
in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.
Similarly, by row operation we may assume that the matrix

(r_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq t_{1},1\leq j\leq s_{2}}

is “block upper triangular” in the sense that we have a
decomposition
[t_{1}]=\bigsqcup_{\ell=1}^{s_{2}}I_{\ell}
,
such that the submatrix
(r_{ij})_{i\in I_{\ell}}
is zero for
\ell>j
,
and the elements in the submatrix
(r_{ij})_{i\in I_{j}}
are linearly independent
in
{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.

Now it’s clear that

\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\rank\big((z_{q_{ij}}^{\prime})_{j\in J_{i}}\big)+\sum_{j=1}^{s_{2}}\rank\big(((z_{r_{ij}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})_{i\in I_{j}}\big)\leq\rank(B)\leq t_{1}+t_{2}.

On the other hand, if

(z_{p}^{\prime}(\omega)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})_{p\in\Sigma}
are linearly independent,
then

\rank\big((z_{q_{ij}}^{\prime})_{j\in J_{i}}\big)=\#J_{i}
for all
i=1,\cdots,s_{1}
,
and
\rank\big(((z_{r_{ij}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}})_{i\in I_{j}}\big)=\#I_{j}
for all
j=1,\cdots,s_{2}
,
hence the left hand side of the above inequality is also equal to

t_{1}+t_{2}
.
This means that the maximal possible rank of
B

is
\rho=\rho(B)=t_{1}+t_{2}
, and the desired result holds.
∎

From the proof one can see that the
\rho

can be effectively computed, either by Gaussian elimination,
or by choosing any element
\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
such that

(z_{p}^{\prime}(\omega)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1})_{p\in\Sigma}
are linearly independent,
and compute the rank of
B(\omega)
.

3.3.
Rédei matrix whose corank is expected to be zero

In this section we study
j>s
cases, namely, the cases
that the
B_{j}^{\prime}
is not of low-rank.
In this case the
B_{j}^{\prime}
, as a submatrix of
B
of form in
Definition
3.1
,
produced by Swinnerton-Dyer’s argument
[
SD08
]

in §
2
,
is not so easy to analyze, since it contains non-zero low-rank parts
which are not so easy to control, as in Theorem
2.8
.
In this case we use Rédei matrices
in Theorem
2.9
to describe the
essential
\pi
-strict Selmer groups
instead.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let

B=(P,Q)=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}+D_{d_{1}}^{\prime}&Q_{1}\\
D_{d_{2}}^{\prime}&Q_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
D_{d_{a}}^{\prime}&Q_{a}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{a(k-1)\times((k-1)+d)}

with
a\geq 2
,
such that at least one of

d_{1},\cdots,d_{a}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
is
\neq 1
,
and
Q=(Q_{1};\cdots;Q_{a})\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{a(k-1)\times d}
is of generically full rank,
namely, there exists
k\geq 1
and
\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

such that
\rank(Q(\omega))=d
.
Then there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\#\Sigma
and
d
, such that
for any
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
,
the random variable
X_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,

\omega\mapsto\corank(B(\omega))
satisfies

P_{\geq m}^{(k)}:={\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}\geq m)\leq C\cdot k\cdot 2^{-m}(3/4)^{k}.

In particular,
P_{m}^{(\infty)}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)

is
1
if
m=0
, is
0
otherwise.

Proof.

We may assume that
d_{2}\neq 1
.
If write
Q=(z_{q_{ij}}^{\prime})_{1\leq i\leq a,1\leq j\leq d}
, then
we have

B_{\mathrm{new}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}P&Q&(u_{1};0;\cdots;0)\\
\hline\cr(u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}};0;\cdots;0)&(z_{k}^{(q_{ij})})&(a_{kk}+z_{k}^{(d_{1})};z_{k}^{(d_{2})};\cdots;z_{k}^{(d_{a})})\end{array}\right),

0\leq\rank(B_{\mathrm{new}})-\rank(B)\leq a+1

and correspondingly,
-a\leq\corank(B_{\mathrm{new}})-\corank(B)\leq 1
.
We may assume
Q=(Q_{11},0;Q_{21},Q_{22})

with
Q_{11}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(k-1)\times d_{1}}
and

Q_{22}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(a-1)(k-1)\times(d-d_{1})}
, such that

Q_{11}
and
Q_{22}
are both of generically full rank.
This implies that
q_{11},\cdots,q_{1d_{1}}
are linearly independent,
and
q_{1,d_{1}+1}=\cdots=q_{1d}=0
.

For simplicity of notation, let
\cdots
be the condition
\corank(B)=m
,
and let
\Omega:=\{\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\mid\corank(B(\omega))=m\}
.
We start with two conditional probabilities.
Firstly, for each
\omega\in\Omega
, let
W_{\omega}:=(*;0;\cdots;0)={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}
,
V_{\omega}:=\operatorname{span}(B)
,

\widetilde{V}:=(*;*;\cdots;*)={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{a(k-1)}
, then by Lemma

3.6

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big((u_{1};0;\cdots;0)\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h:\widetilde{V}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\\
h|_{V_{\omega}}=0\\
h|_{W_{\omega}}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)}{\#(\widetilde{V}/V_{\omega})}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\\
h:{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{(a-1)(k-1)}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\\
h\cdot(D_{d_{2}}^{\prime};\cdots;D_{d_{a}}^{\prime})=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\#\Omega\cdot 2^{(a-1)(k-1)-d+m}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\\
h:{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\\
h\cdot D_{d_{2}}^{\prime}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\#\Omega\cdot 2^{k-1-d+m}}=\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot(3/4)^{k-1}\cdot 2^{d-m}.

Secondly, for each
\omega
, let
W_{\omega}=(*;*;0)={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k-1+d_{1}}
,

V_{\omega}=\operatorname{span}(B^{\mathrm{T}})
,
then by Lemma
3.6

and Proposition
3.11
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big((u_{2};(z_{k}^{(q_{1j})})_{1\leq j\leq d}^{\mathrm{T}})\in\operatorname{span}(B^{\mathrm{T}})\mid\cdots\big)=\frac{1}{\#\Omega}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}\frac{\#V_{\omega}}{\#(V_{\omega}+W_{\omega})}=\frac{1}{\#\Omega}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}\frac{2^{k-1+d-m}}{2^{k-1+d_{1}+\rank(Q_{22})}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\#\Omega}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega\text{ such that}\\
Z_{\Sigma}^{\prime}\text{ is of full rank}\end{subarray}}2^{-m}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\text{ such that}\\
Z_{\Sigma}^{\prime}\text{ is not of full rank}\end{subarray}}2^{d-m}\right)\leq 2^{-m}+\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot(2^{\#\Sigma}-1)\cdot 2^{1-k+d-m}.

From these we can obtain that

\displaystyle P_{m\to m+1}^{(k)}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}(u_{1};0;\cdots;0)\in\operatorname{span}(B)\\
\text{and }(u_{2};(z_{k}^{(q_{1j})})_{1\leq j\leq d}^{\mathrm{T}};a_{kk}+z_{k}^{(d_{1})})\in\operatorname{span}(B^{\mathrm{T}};(u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}},0,\cdots,0))\\
\text{and }(0;(z_{k}^{(q_{ij})})_{1\leq j\leq d}^{\mathrm{T}};z_{k}^{(d_{i})})\in\operatorname{span}(B^{\mathrm{T}};(u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}},0,\cdots,0))\text{ for }2\leq i\leq a\end{array}\middle|~\cdots\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\big((u_{1};0;\cdots;0)\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid\cdots\big)\leq\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot(3/4)^{k-1}\cdot 2^{d-m},

\displaystyle P_{m\to m}^{(k)}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}(u_{1};0;\cdots;0)\notin\operatorname{span}(B)\\
\text{and }(u_{2};(z_{k}^{(q_{1j})})_{1\leq j\leq d}^{\mathrm{T}};a_{kk}+z_{k}^{(d_{1})})\in\operatorname{span}(B^{\mathrm{T}};(u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}},0,\cdots,0))\\
\text{and }(0;(z_{k}^{(q_{ij})})_{1\leq j\leq d}^{\mathrm{T}};z_{k}^{(d_{i})})\in\operatorname{span}(B^{\mathrm{T}};(u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}},0,\cdots,0))\text{ for }2\leq i\leq a\end{array}\middle|~\cdots\right)

\displaystyle\qquad{}+{\mathbb{P}}\big((u_{1};0;\cdots;0)\in\operatorname{span}(B)\text{ and }\rank(B_{\mathrm{new}})=\rank(B)+1\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\big((u_{2};(z_{k}^{(q_{1j})})_{1\leq j\leq d}^{\mathrm{T}})\in\operatorname{span}(B^{\mathrm{T}})\mid\cdots\big)+{\mathbb{P}}\big((u_{1};0;\cdots;0)\in\operatorname{span}(B)\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-m}+\frac{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}{\#\Omega}\cdot(3/4)^{k-1}\cdot 2^{\#\Sigma+d-m}.

Hence for any
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle P_{\geq m}^{(k+1)}=\sum_{i\geq m}P_{i}^{(k+1)}=\sum_{i\geq m-1}P_{i\to i+1}^{(k)}P_{i}^{(k)}+\sum_{i\geq m}P_{i\to i}^{(k)}P_{i}^{(k)}+\sum_{j\geq 1}\sum_{i\geq m+j}P_{i\to i-j}^{(k)}P_{i}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\leq(3/4)^{k-1}\sum_{i\geq m-1}2^{d-i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq m}2^{-i}P_{i}^{(k)}+(3/4)^{k-1}\sum_{i\geq m}2^{\#\Sigma+d-i}\right)+\sum_{i\geq m+1}P_{i}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\leq(3/4)^{k-1}\cdot(2^{\#\Sigma}+2)\cdot 2^{d+1-m}+2^{-m}P_{\geq m}^{(k)}+P_{\geq m+1}^{(k)}.

Note that
P_{m}^{(1)}=1
if
m=d
, and
P_{m}^{(1)}=0
otherwise.
Denote
C_{0}:=(3/4)^{-1}\cdot(2^{\#\Sigma}+2)\cdot 2^{d+1}
.
Then by induction on
k
, it’s easy to see that, for any
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
,

P_{\geq m}^{(k)}\leq C_{0}\cdot k\cdot 2^{-m}(3/4)^{k}\cdot\begin{cases}4/3,&\text{if }m\geq 2,\\
8/3,&\text{if }m=1.\end{cases}

This completes the proof.
∎

Now we look at the example described in Theorem
2.9
.
Let
E:y^{2}=x(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})
,

P_{1}=(e_{1},0)
,
P_{2}=(0,0)
,

\pi:E[2]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
is given by
P_{1}=(e_{1},0)\mapsto 0
and
P_{2}=(0,0)\mapsto 1
.
Let
\Sigma
be containing
-1
all primes dividing
2e_{1}e_{2}(e_{1}-e_{2})
.
Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of square-free integers,
and
q\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
be the
\Sigma
-part of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
By Theorem
2.9
,
for
m=qn\in{\mathfrak{X}}
the
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(m)})

and
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(m)})
depend only on

\omega=\omega(n)\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
,
by abuse of notation, denote them by
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(q\omega)})

and
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(q\omega)})
.
Suppose
e_{1}(e_{1}-e_{2})
is not a perfect square.
Then we can apply Proposition
3.12

to the Rédei matrices describing

S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(m)})

and
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(m)})
,
which are
B_{1}
and
B_{1}^{\prime}
in Theorem
2.9

with a column removed.
The conclusion is, there exists
a constant
C>0
depending only on
\#\Sigma
, such that for any
k\geq 1
,

(3.15)

{\mathbb{P}}\big(S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(q\omega)})\neq 0\mid\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\big)\leq C\cdot k\cdot(3/4)^{k},

the same result goes for
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(q\omega)})
.
For other
\pi
there are similar results.

3.4.
Change of
\Sigma
and invariance of the parameter

In this section we prove that,
if
{\mathfrak{X}}
is a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of quadratic twists of elliptic curves
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with full rational
2
-torsion points,

\Sigma\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathfrak{X}}
is a
\Sigma^{\prime}
-equivalence class,
then the class
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
has the same parameter
\mathbf{t}

as that of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
This is reduced to the corresponding result on
“high-rank alternating Rédei matrix of level
2
”
in Definition
3.1
.
The Markov analysis is not needed in the proof; we only need to use
the basic properties of low-rank Rédei matrices.
By induction, we may add new primes to
\Sigma^{\prime}
one by one,
so we only need to consider
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\sqcup\{q\}
case.

Let
B=(B_{ij})\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+t)\times(2(k-1)+t)}

be in Definition
3.1
,
associated to the
\Sigma
-equivalence class
S^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma)
of
S(\Sigma)

given by
\mathbf{s}=(s_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Sigma}
.
There are
4
different
\Sigma^{\prime}
-equivalence classes

{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
contained in
S^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma)
:

•

For
s_{q}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, the two classes

{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}=\left\{n~\middle|~n\in S(\Sigma^{\prime})\text{ such that }\left[\frac{p}{n}\right]=s_{p}\text{ for all }p\in\Sigma^{\prime}\right\}.

•

For
s_{q}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, the two classes

{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}=\left\{qn~\middle|~n\in S(\Sigma^{\prime})\text{ such that }\left[\frac{p}{n}\right]=s_{p}+\left[\frac{p}{q}\right]\text{ for all }p\in\Sigma\text{ and }\left[\frac{q}{n}\right]=s_{q}\right\}.

For each of the above
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
, there is an associated

\widetilde{B}=(\widetilde{B}_{ij})
for the
\Sigma^{\prime}
-equivalence
class
\{n\}
,
such that

•

for the first two classes,

\widetilde{B}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+t)\times(2(k-1)+t)}

and
\widetilde{B}(n)=B(n)
;

•

for the last two classes,

\widetilde{B}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+(t+2))\times(2(k-1)+(t+2))}

and
\widetilde{B}(n)\sim B(qn)
,
here we need to fix the order of prime factors of
qn

according to that of
n
:
if
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{k}
is the prime factors of
n
,
then we fix the order of prime factors of
qn

to be
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{k-1},q,\ell_{k}
.

For example, we may take the above
B
and
\widetilde{B}

to be the
\widetilde{B}
in Corollary
2.3

associated to
{\mathfrak{X}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
, respectively,
namely, for
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=\corank(B(n))

where
n
is the product of bad primes of
E

outside
\Sigma
, and for
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
,
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E)=\corank(\widetilde{B}(n))

where
n
is the product of bad primes of
E

outside
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

For each of the above
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}
,
the
\widetilde{B}
is also a
“high-rank alternating Rédei matrix of level
2
”,
and has the same parameter
\mathbf{t}
as that of
B
.

Proof.

For the first two classes, we have
\widetilde{B}=B

and there is nothing to prove.
For the last two classes, it’s easy to see that,
according to our choice of order of prime factors,
the
\widetilde{B}
can be defined block-wise by

\widetilde{A}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}+D_{q}^{\prime}&z_{q}^{\prime}\\
(z_{q^{*}}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{T}}&\left[\frac{q^{*}}{n}\right]\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{z}_{p}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}z_{p}^{\prime}\\
\left[\frac{p}{q}\right]\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{for }p\in\Sigma.

Here
q^{*}=(-1)^{(q-1)/2}q
.
We reorder the rows and columns to make

\widetilde{B}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+(t+2))\times(2(k-1)+(t+2))}
.

It’s easy to see that
\widetilde{B}
is still alternating,
and
\widetilde{B}_{ii}=B_{ii}
for
i=1,2
.
Consider
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\widetilde{B}_{13}\\
\widetilde{B}_{23}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{2(k-1)\times(t+2)}
.
Its first
t
columns are that of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{13}\\
B_{23}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
and its last two columns comes from the additional column associated to

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\widetilde{B}_{1j}\\
\widetilde{B}_{2j}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
j=1,2
.
Considering the occurrence of
z_{q}^{\prime}
, it’s easy to see that

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\widetilde{B}_{13}\\
\widetilde{B}_{23}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is also of generically full rank.

Now we compare the maximal possible rank
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}

of
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\widetilde{B}_{jj}&\widetilde{B}_{j3}\\
\widetilde{B}_{3j}&\widetilde{B}_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

to the maximal possible rank
\rho_{j}

of
B_{j}^{\prime\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{jj}&B_{j3}\\
B_{3j}&B_{33}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
1\leq j\leq s
.
Note that the
t+2
columns of
\widetilde{B}_{j3}
come
from the
t
columns of
B_{j3}
and the additional columns associated
to
\widetilde{B}_{j1}
and
\widetilde{B}_{j2}
.
If we put one of that additional column back to
\widetilde{B}_{jj}
,
then it’s easy to see that
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime}(n)

is equal to
B_{j}^{\prime\prime}(qn)
with an additional row and column containing
z_{q}^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\widetilde{\rho}_{j}\leq\rho_{j}+2
.
On the other hand, by Proposition
3.11

it’s easy to see that “
=
” can be obtained.
Hence the parameter
\widetilde{t}_{j}
of
\widetilde{B}

is
\widetilde{t}_{j}=(t+2)-\widetilde{\rho}_{j}=t-\rho_{j}=t_{j}
, the same as that of
B
.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

Each of the above
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\prime}

gives maps
\psi_{r}:\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}/S_{r}\to\Omega_{r+t}^{\Sigma,*}/S_{r+t}

for
r\geq 0
, where
t=0
for the first two classes,

t=1
for the last two classes, such that

\psi_{r}(\omega(n))=\omega(n)
for the first two classes,

\psi_{r}(\omega(n))=\omega(qn)
for the last two classes,
see §
4.1.2
.
Lemma
4.8
tells us that,
if for each
r
, a subset
B_{r}
of
\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}
is given
(i.e. an event),
which is
S_{r}
-invariant, let
B_{r}^{\prime}
be the preimage of
B_{r+t}

in
\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}
under the map
\psi_{r}
,
then
\lim_{r\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r}^{\prime})=\lim_{r\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})

if they exist.
From this and the Markov analysis
(which gives the limit distribution of the corank of
B
)
one may also deduce that the parameter is invariant
under refinement of equivalence classes
(since different parameter gives different limit distribution).

3.5.
Limit of moments

For
\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
we consider the limit of the
\xi
-th moment of the order of
\ker(B(\omega))
,

\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
, as
k\to\infty
:

\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}(2^{\xi\cdot X_{k}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m).

The (
3.4
) in Theorem
3.2

implies that

(3.16)

\left|{\mathbb{E}}(2^{\xi\cdot X_{k}})-\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)\right|\leq C\cdot\alpha^{k},\text{ in particular }\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}(2^{\xi\cdot X_{k}})=\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m),

which is the
\xi
-th moment of the corresponding matrix model
M_{2k+t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.

On the other hand, Heath-Brown’s method
[
HB94
]
allows us to compute the
limit of average order, we called “
\omega
-average order”
(and hence the limit of
\xi
-th moment),
by a linear algebra way without Markov chain analysis.
The disadvantage is that it does not produce easily a simple formula
(in
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]

the type (A) is considered, although with a lengthy computation).
Instead, it’s better to pass to
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
,
then apply the same method to the matrix model
(which produces
3+\sum_{i}2^{t_{i}}
easily, see appendix
A.3
):

\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}(2^{X_{k}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{m}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)=\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{m}\cdot P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)=3+\sum_{i}2^{t_{i}}.

The advantage of Heath-Brown’s method is
that the same method also applies to the natural average order,
and allows us to compare them.

Let
B=(B_{ij})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq a+1\\
1\leq j\leq c+1\end{subarray}}\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}

be a restricted Rédei matrix.
Consider the high-rank part
B_{ij,H}
of
B_{ij}

for
1\leq i\leq a
,
1\leq j\leq c
.
If
(B_{ij,H})\sim(\diag(A,\cdots,A);0)
, then such
B

is called a “high-rank Rédei matrix”.
This coincides with the previous definition when
B
is of size
k\times k
.
Clearly, matrix in Theorem
2.2

(or Theorem
2.9
,
more generally, in Remark
2.10
)
is of high-rank.
Heath-Brown’s method implies that,
for such matrix, the
\omega
-average order of kernel exists.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}

be a high-rank restricted Rédei matrix.
For each
k\geq 1
let
X_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}

be the random variable
\omega\mapsto\corank(B(\omega))

and
E^{(k)}:={\mathbb{E}}(2^{X_{k}})
.
Then the
\omega
-average order
E^{(\infty)}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}E^{(k)}

exists, has an explicit formula (
3.19
),
satisfies an upper bound

1\leq E^{(\infty)}<3\cdot 2^{c(c+3)/2+d},

and we have the convergence rate estimation:
let
k_{0}\geq 1
be any fixed integer, denote
x_{0}:=(1-2^{-a})^{k_{0}}
,
then for any
k\geq k_{0}
,

\displaystyle\left|E^{(k)}-E^{(\infty)}\right|

\displaystyle<3\cdot 2^{c(c+3)/2+d}\cdot\left(\frac{\left(1+x_{0}\right)^{2^{a}}-1-x_{0}^{2^{a}}}{x_{0}}+2^{a}\right)\cdot(1-2^{-a})^{k}

\displaystyle<3\cdot 2^{c(c+3)/2+d}\cdot(2^{2^{a}}+2^{a}-2)\cdot(1-2^{-a})^{k}.

Proof.

Write
B=(P,Q;R^{\mathrm{T}},S)\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}
.
By elementary row and column operations,
we may keep
a,b,c,d
unchanged and assume
that
P_{H}=(\diag(A,\cdots,A);0)
.

Let
V={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{ck+d}
and
W={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{ak+b}
.
For
B\in M_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
and
v\in V
we have

\frac{1}{\#W}\sum_{w\in W}(-1)^{w^{\mathrm{T}}Bv}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }v\notin\ker(B),\\
1,&\text{if }v\in\ker(B),\end{cases}\quad\text{hence}\quad 2^{\corank(B)}=\frac{1}{\#W}\sum_{v\in V,w\in W}(-1)^{w^{\mathrm{T}}Bv}.

Therefore

(3.17)

{\mathbb{E}}(2^{X_{k}})=\frac{1}{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\cdot\#W}\sum_{v\in V,w\in W}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}(-1)^{w^{\mathrm{T}}B(\omega)v}.

When
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
v\in V={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{ck+d}
and

w\in W={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{ak+b}
are fixed, define the map

\psi=\psi_{B,v,w}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,

\omega\mapsto w^{\mathrm{T}}B(\omega)v
.
To study the map
\psi_{B,v,w}
,
write
v=(v^{(1)};\cdots;v^{(c)};\widetilde{v})

and
w=(w^{(1)};\cdots;w^{(a)};\widetilde{w})
,
where
v^{(1)},\cdots,v^{(c)},w^{(1)},\cdots,w^{(a)}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k}
,

\widetilde{v}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{d}
,
and
\widetilde{w}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{b}

(note that
a\geq c
).
Note that to give
v^{(1)},\cdots,v^{(c)},w^{(1)},\cdots,w^{(a)}
is equivalent to give the map

\iota:[k]:=\{1,\cdots,k\}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a},\quad i\mapsto(v_{i}^{(1)},\cdots,v_{i}^{(c)},w_{i}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{i}^{(a)})^{\mathrm{T}}.

For
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
, define

[k]_{\lambda}:=\iota^{-1}(\{\lambda\})=\{i\in[k]\mid(v_{i}^{(1)},\cdots,v_{i}^{(c)},w_{i}^{(1)},\cdots,w_{i}^{(a)})^{\mathrm{T}}=\lambda\}
.
Similarly, for
1\leq\ell\leq c
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{2}
,
define
[k]_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}:=\{i\in[k]\mid(v_{i}^{(\ell)},w_{i}^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}}=\lambda\}
.

Let
\Lambda:=\Im(\iota)\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
be the image of the map
\iota
.
We claim that once
\Lambda,\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}

are fixed, the
\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)

depends only on
a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}:=\sum_{i\in[k]_{\lambda},j\in[k]_{\lambda^{\prime}}}a_{ij}

for all
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
in
\Lambda
,
and
z_{\lambda}^{(p)}:=\sum_{i\in[k]_{\lambda}}z_{i}^{(p)}

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
p\in\Sigma
.
More precisely, it is a linear combination
of
1
,
a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
,
z_{\lambda}^{(p)}

and
z_{\lambda}^{(p)}z_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(q)}
with coefficients
depending only on
B
.
To prove this, note that the Rédei matrix
A
only
occurred in the diagonal of
P
, so
\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)

is equal to
\sum_{\ell\in[c]}(w^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}}A(\omega)v^{(\ell)}

plus
\widetilde{w}^{\mathrm{T}}S\widetilde{v}

plus some linear combinations (with coefficients
depending only on
B
) of the following:

(i)

(w^{(i)})^{\mathrm{T}}D_{p}v^{(j)}
for some
i\in[a]
,
j\in[c]
,
p\in\Sigma
;

(ii)

(w^{(i)})^{\mathrm{T}}z_{p}z_{q}^{\mathrm{T}}v^{(j)}
for some
i\in[a]
,
j\in[c]
,
p,q\in\Sigma
;

(iii)

(w^{(i)})^{\mathrm{T}}z_{p}\widetilde{v}_{j}
for some
i\in[a]
,
j\in[d]
,
p\in\Sigma
;

(iv)

\widetilde{w}_{i}z_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}v^{(j)}
for some
i\in[b]
,
j\in[c]
,
p\in\Sigma
.

Note that

\displaystyle(w^{(i)})^{\mathrm{T}}D_{p}v^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}w_{\ell}^{(i)}z_{\ell}^{(p)}v_{\ell}^{(j)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\sum_{\ell\in[k]_{\lambda}}w_{\ell}^{(i)}z_{\ell}^{(p)}v_{\ell}^{(j)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\lambda_{c+i}\lambda_{j}z_{\lambda}^{(p)},

\displaystyle(w^{(i)})^{\mathrm{T}}z_{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}w_{\ell}^{(i)}z_{\ell}^{(p)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\sum_{\ell\in[k]_{\lambda}}w_{\ell}^{(i)}z_{\ell}^{(p)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\lambda_{c+i}z_{\lambda}^{(p)},

\displaystyle z_{p}^{\mathrm{T}}v^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}z_{\ell}^{(p)}v_{\ell}^{(j)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\sum_{\ell\in[k]_{\lambda}}z_{\ell}^{(p)}v_{\ell}^{(j)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\lambda_{j}z_{\lambda}^{(p)}.

Therefore the above (i)–(iv) all depend only on

z_{\lambda}^{(p)}
.
For
\sum_{\ell\in[c]}(w^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}}A(\omega)v^{(\ell)}
, we have

(3.18)

(w^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}}A(\omega)v^{(\ell)}=\sum_{i\in[k]_{01}^{(\ell)},j\in[k]_{10}^{(\ell)}}a_{ij}+\sum_{i\in[k]_{11}^{(\ell)},j\in[k]_{00}^{(\ell)}}a_{ij}+\sum_{i\in[k]_{11}^{(\ell)},j\in[k]_{01}^{(\ell)}}z_{i}^{(-1)}z_{j}^{(-1)}.

Note that in each of the three sums in (
3.18
),
the ranges of
i
and
j

are disjoint.
For each
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
in
\Lambda
,

•

the
a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
appeared in (
3.18
) if and only if

(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime},\lambda_{c+\ell},\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime})=(0,1,1,0)
or
(1,0,1,0)
,

•

the
a_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}
appeared in (
3.18
) if and only if

(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime},\lambda_{c+\ell},\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime})=(1,0,0,1)
or
(0,1,0,1)
,

•

the
z_{\lambda}^{(-1)}z_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(-1)}
appeared in (
3.18
) if and only if

(\lambda_{\ell},\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime},\lambda_{c+\ell},\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime})=(1,0,1,1)
or
(0,1,1,1)
.

Replace
a_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}
with

a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}+z_{\lambda}^{(-1)}z_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(-1)}
,
sum them over
1\leq\ell\leq c
, we obtain that

\sum_{\ell\in[c]}(w^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}}A(\omega)v^{(\ell)}=\sum_{\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}}\left(a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})(\lambda_{c+\ell}+\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime})+z_{\lambda}^{(-1)}z_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(-1)}\cdot\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime}\right),

here we fix any complete order
<
on
\Lambda
.
This proves our claim.

The occurrence of
a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
or not in the expression of

\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)
is important to the properties of
\psi
.
For example, if
a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
occurred
for some
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
in
\Lambda
,
fix
i\neq j
with
i\in[k]_{\lambda}
and
j\in[k]_{\lambda^{\prime}}
,
utilizing the bijection

\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k(k-1)/2}\times M_{(k-1)\times 1}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})^{\#\Sigma},\qquad\omega=\big((a_{ij}),(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)\mapsto\big((a_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k},(z_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)

we let
a_{ij}
or
a_{ji}
varies while let others unchanged,
then it’s easy to see that

\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}(-1)^{\psi(\omega)}=0
,
so it has no contribution in (
3.17
).
According to
[
HB94
]
,

[
Kane13
]
, two elements
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}

are called
linked
if

\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})(\lambda_{c+\ell}+\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime})=1
,
or equivalently, when
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
,

a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
occurred in the expression of

\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)
.
A subset
\Lambda
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
is called an
unlinked subset

if any elements
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
of
\Lambda
are unlinked.

Lemma 3.16

(
[
HB94
]
, Lemma 7,
[
Kane13
]
, p. 1274)
.

Let
\Lambda
be an unlinked subset of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
.
Then its projection to the first
2c
components
is contained in a translate of a Lagrangian of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{2c}

with respect to the symplectic form

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
In particular,
\#\Lambda\leq 2^{a}
.

Now we know that the expression (
3.17
)
only involves those
\psi=\psi_{B,v,w}
such that the corresponding

\iota:[k]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
,
\widetilde{v}
and
\widetilde{w}

satisfy that
\Lambda:=\Im(\iota)
is an unlinked subset
(which must have cardinality
\leq 2^{a}
).
We know that for such
\psi
, the
a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
does not occurred in the expression
of
\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)
for any
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
,
hence such map
\psi=\psi_{B,v,w}
factors through the surjective map

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}:=\left\{\mathbf{z}=(z_{\lambda}^{(p)})_{\lambda\in\Lambda,p\in\Sigma}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Lambda\cdot\#\Sigma}~\middle|~\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}z_{\lambda}^{(p)}=s_{p}\text{ for all }p\in\Sigma\right\},

\displaystyle\omega=\big((a_{ij}),(z_{i}^{(p)})\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\sum_{i\in[k]_{\lambda}}z_{i}^{(p)}\right)_{\lambda\in\Lambda,p\in\Sigma}.

For each
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
,
its preimage in
\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

has size
2^{k(k-1)/2+(k-\#\Lambda)\cdot\#\Sigma}
.
When
\Lambda
is fixed with
\#\Lambda=m
, the number of maps
\iota:[k]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}

with
\Im(\iota)=\Lambda
is equal to
the number of surjections
\#\{[k]\twoheadrightarrow[m]\}

from
[k]
to
[m]
, which clearly satisfies

m^{k}-m(m-1)^{k}\leq\#\{[k]\twoheadrightarrow[m]\}\leq m^{k}
.
(In fact
\#\{[k]\twoheadrightarrow[m]\}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{m-i}\binom{m}{i}i^{k}
.)
Therefore (
3.17
) implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(2^{X_{k}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\#\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\cdot 2^{ak+b}}\sum_{\Lambda\text{ unlinked}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota:[k]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}\\
\Im(\iota)=\Lambda\end{subarray}}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}(-1)^{\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{ak+b}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{a}}2^{(1-m)\cdot\#\Sigma}\cdot\#\{[k]\twoheadrightarrow[m]\}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda\text{ unlinked}\\
\#\Lambda=m\end{subarray}}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}(-1)^{\psi_{B,v,w}(\mathbf{z})}.

Its main term
E^{(\infty)}

comes from the
m=2^{a}
term in the above sum
and by replacing
\#\{[k]\twoheadrightarrow[m]\}

with
m^{k}
. Namely, the explicit formula of
E^{(\infty)}
is

(3.19)

E^{(\infty)}=2^{(1-2^{a})\cdot\#\Sigma-b}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda\text{ unlinked}\\
\#\Lambda=2^{a}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}(-1)^{\psi_{B,v,w}(\mathbf{z})}.

The number of different Lagrangian subspaces of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{2c}

is
\#\big({\mathrm{Sp}}_{2c}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})/({\mathrm{Sp}}_{2c}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\cap(\begin{smallmatrix}*&*\\
0&*\end{smallmatrix}))\big)=\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)
.
Therefore an upper bound of
E^{(\infty)}
is

E^{(\infty)}\leq 2^{(1-2^{a})\cdot\#\Sigma-b}\cdot 2^{c}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)\cdot 2^{b+d}\cdot 2^{(2^{a}-1)\cdot\#\Sigma}=2^{c(c+3)/2+d}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(1+2^{-i})<3\cdot 2^{c(c+3)/2+d}.

Let
k_{0}\geq 1
be any fixed integer such that
k\geq k_{0}
.
For simplicity of notation, denote

x:=(1-2^{-a})^{k}
and
x_{0}:=(1-2^{-a})^{k_{0}}
, so that
x\leq x_{0}<1
.
Note that for any
1\leq m\leq 2^{a}-1
,
(1-2^{-a}m)^{k}\leq(1-2^{-a})^{mk}=x^{m}
.
So we may get an estimate of error term

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{E}}(2^{X_{k}})-E^{(\infty)}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2^{ak+b}}\sum_{m=1}^{2^{a}-1}2^{(1-m)\cdot\#\Sigma}\cdot m^{k}\cdot\binom{2^{a}}{m}\cdot 2^{c}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)\cdot 2^{b+d}\cdot 2^{(m-1)\cdot\#\Sigma}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\frac{1}{2^{ak+b}}\cdot 2^{(1-2^{a})\cdot\#\Sigma}\cdot 2^{a}(2^{a}-1)^{k}\cdot 2^{c}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)\cdot 2^{b+d}\cdot 2^{(2^{a}-1)\cdot\#\Sigma}

\displaystyle=2^{c(c+3)/2+d}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(1+2^{-i})\cdot\left(\sum_{m=1}^{2^{a}-1}(1-2^{-a}m)^{k}\cdot\binom{2^{a}}{m}+2^{a}x\right)

\displaystyle<3\cdot 2^{c(c+3)/2+d}\cdot\left(\left(1+x\right)^{2^{a}}-1-x^{2^{a}}+2^{a}x\right)\leq 3\cdot 2^{c(c+3)/2+d}\cdot\left(\frac{\left(1+x_{0}\right)^{2^{a}}-1-x_{0}^{2^{a}}}{x_{0}}+2^{a}\right)x.

This completes the proof.
∎

4.
Natural density and average order

Recall that for
r\geq 1
,
S_{r}(\Sigma)
is
the set of positive square-free integers
prime to
\Sigma
and has exactly
r
prime factors,
and
\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}
is the corresponding
probability space of Legendre symbols
defined in (
2.2
).
For all
n\in S_{r}(\Sigma)

fix an order of prime factors of
n
.
Then there is a natural map
\omega:S_{r}(\Sigma)\to\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}
.
If
N
is a positive real number, define

S_{r}(N,\Sigma):=\{n\in S_{r}(\Sigma)\mid n<N\}
,
similarly define
S_{\geq 1}(N,\Sigma)
.

Let
I
be a countable set. For each
r\geq 1
,
let
B_{r}:\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}/S_{r}\to I
be a random variable.
For
n\in S_{r}(\Sigma)
,
we can define
B_{r}(n):=B_{r}(\omega(n))
.
If there is no risk of confusion, the
B_{r}(\omega)
and
B_{r}(n)
are simply denoted by

B(\omega)
and
B(n)
.
Namely, the
B=(B_{r})_{r\geq 1}
also gives
\bigsqcup_{r=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}/S_{r}\to I

and
S(\infty,\Sigma)\to I
.
For each
i\in I
,
there are three densities associated to
B
.

•

For each
r\geq 1
,
the (algebraic)
\omega
-density

P^{(r)}(i):={\mathbb{P}}(B_{r}(\omega)=i\mid\omega\in\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r})\qquad\text{and}\qquad P^{(\infty)}(i):=\lim_{r\to\infty}P^{(r)}(i).

•

For each
r\geq 1
,
the natural
\omega
-density

P_{\mathrm{nat}}^{(r)}(i):=\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\big(B_{r}(n)=i\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_{\mathrm{nat}}^{(\infty)}(i):=\lim_{r\to\infty}P_{\mathrm{nat}}^{(r)}(i).

•

The natural density

P_{\mathrm{nat}}(i):=\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\big(B(n)=i\mid n\in S_{\geq 1}(N,\Sigma)\big).

By the work of Gerth
[
Ger84
]
,
the algebraic
\omega
-density
P^{(r)}(i)

is equal to the natural
\omega
-density
P_{\mathrm{nat}}^{(r)}(i)
.
The main result of this section gives a uniform behavior
of the limit of algebraic
\omega
-density
P^{(\infty)}(i)

(if exists) related to the natural density
P_{\mathrm{nat}}(i)
,
which is a key ingredient of the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 4.1

(
\omega
-density implies natural density)
.

Let
I
and
B=(B_{r})_{r\geq 1}
be as above.
For each
r\geq 1
, let
I_{r}\subset I
be a finite subset.
Suppose that for each
i\in I
, the limit
P^{(\infty)}(i)
exists.
Then there are absolute constants
C_{1}>0
and
c>0
,
such that for any fixed

\epsilon>0
, the following inequality
holds for sufficiently large
N
(depending on
\epsilon
):

\sum_{i\in I}\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(B(n)=i\mid n\in S_{\geq 1}(N,\Sigma)\big)-P^{(\infty)}(i)\right|\\
\leq I_{\epsilon}(\log\log N)\cdot C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})+R_{\epsilon}(\log\log N),

where

I_{\epsilon}(x):=1+\max_{(1-\epsilon)x\leq r\leq(1+\epsilon)x}\#I_{r}

and

R_{\epsilon}(x):=\max_{(1-\epsilon)x\leq r\leq(1+\epsilon)x}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{r}}\left|P^{(r)}(i)-P^{(\infty)}(i)\right|+\sum_{i\notin I_{r}}\left(P^{(r)}(i)+P^{(\infty)}(i)\right)\right).

In particular, if
\lim_{x\to\infty}I_{\epsilon}(x)\exp(-c(\log x)^{1/2})=0

and
\lim_{x\to\infty}R_{\epsilon}(x)=0
,
then
P_{\mathrm{nat}}(i)=P^{(\infty)}(i)
for all
i\in I
.

This result is due to an earlier work of Smith
[
Smi17
]
.
The following is the main ingredients of the proof.

•

We may enlarge
\Sigma
such that it contains
-1

and all primes
<D
for some fixed real number
D>3
,
which is the case considered in
[
Smi17
]
.
This is reviewed in §
4.1.2
.

•

By the result of Hardy and Ramanujan, we only need to consider integers
n

such that the number
r
of prime factors of
n
is close to
\log\log N
;
these numbers are of density
1-o(1)
as
N\to\infty
.
This argument is also used in Kane’s method
[
Kane13
]
.
This is reviewed in §
4.1.3
.

•

Smith’s box method
[
Smi17
]
improves Kane’s method
[
Kane13
]

by introducing several additional conditions to integers
n

besides the number of prime factors close to
\log\log N
,
such that their density is still
1-o(1)
,
and the problem is reduced to study

{\mathbb{P}}\big(B_{r}(n)=i\mid n\in X\big)

for subsets
X
of
S_{r}(\Sigma)
satisfying some good condition
(called “box”) and which contains at least one
such “good” integers.
This is reviewed in §
4.1.1
.

The error term in Theorem
4.1
is not sufficient
to deduce the result on the average order (Theorem
1.6
)
from the distribution of ranks (Theorem
1.2
).
For this, we go back to the method of Heath-Brown
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]

and Kane
[
Kane13
]
.
Recall that
r\sim\log\log N
means
|r-\log\log N|\leq(\log\log N)^{2/3}
.

Theorem 4.2

(
\omega
-average order implies natural and “essential” average order)
.

Let
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}

be a high-rank restricted Rédei matrix.
For each
k\geq 1
let
X_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,

\omega\mapsto\corank(B(\omega))
,
and let
E^{(\infty)}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}(2^{X_{k}})

be the
\omega
-average order
as in Proposition
3.15
.
Then there exits a constant
C>0
depending only on
B
,
such that for any
N>30
, the natural average order satisfies

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\big(2^{\corank(B(n))}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\big)-E^{(\infty)}\right|<C\cdot\frac{(\log\log N)^{2^{c+1}}}{(\log N)^{1/2^{a}}},

and the “essential” average order satisfies

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\big(2^{\corank(B(n))}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma),r(n)\sim\log\log N\big)-E^{(\infty)}\right|<C\cdot\frac{\log\log\log N}{(\log\log N)^{1/3}}.

4.1.
Natural density and Smith’s box method

In this section we prove Theorem
4.1
.
We change the meaning of
r\sim\log\log N
in this section,
from
|r-\log\log N|\leq(\log\log N)^{2/3}
to

|r-\log\log N|\leq\frac{1}{4}(\log\log N)^{2/3}
.
(Here the
\frac{1}{4}
is arbitrary; any positive constant
<1
should also work.)

4.1.1.
Box method

We review box method of Smith
[
Smi17
]
.
The main result is Proposition
4.7
.

Let
N>30
,
D_{1}>D>3
,
C_{0}>1
be real numbers such that

\log N>(\log D)^{4}
.
Define
\Sigma_{D}
to be the set
consists of
-1
and all primes
<D
.
We introduce the notation
r\sim_{D}\log\log N

which means that
|r-\log(\frac{\log N}{\log D})|\leq(\log(\frac{\log N}{\log D}))^{2/3}
.
As in
[
Smi17
]
, Definition 5.3,
we only consider
r
fixed such that
r\sim_{D}\log\log N
.
For an integer
n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma_{D})
,
let
\ell_{1}<\cdots<\ell_{r}
be its prime factors ordered by size.
The following additional conditions for
n
is introduced
(see
[
Smi17
]
, Definition 5.3 and Proposition 6.10):

•

n
is called
comfortably spaced above

D_{1}

if
\ell_{i}>D_{1}
(
1\leq i<r
) implies

\ell_{i}>2D_{1}
and
\ell_{i+1}>2\ell_{i}
.

•

n
is called
C_{0}
-regular
if the range of
\ell_{i}

is specified in certain way for all
i\leq r/3
.

•

n
is called
extravagantly spaced
if

\ell_{i}
is larger than
\ell_{i-1}
in certain way
for some
\sqrt{r}/2<i<r/2
.

•

n
is called
Siegel-free above
D_{1}
if it is not contained
in the set
V
of
[
Smi17
]
, Proposition 6.10.

The
n
is called “good” if it satisfies all of the above conditions.
Take
D_{1}=D^{(\log\log N)^{c_{13}}}

and
C_{0}=c_{14}\cdot\log\log\log N

for some positive absolute constants
c_{13},c_{14}
in
[
Smi17
]
.
The first important result is that, the “good” integers has density
1-o(1)

as
N\to\infty
.

Proposition 4.3

(
[
Smi17
]
, Theorem 5.4 and Proposition 6.10)
.

Let
W
be the set of
“good” integers
n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma_{D})
.
Then

{\mathbb{P}}\big(n\in W\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma_{D})\big)>1-C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})

for some absolute constants
C_{1}>0,c>0
.

Smith considered certain subsets
X
of
S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})
, called “box”.

Definition 4.4

(
[
Smi17
]
, Definition 6.8)
.

A “box”
X
is a subset of
S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})
given by the following data:

•

an integer
0\leq k\leq r
,

•

primes
D<\ell_{1}<\cdots<\ell_{k}<D_{1}
,

•

real numbers
D_{1}<t_{k+1}<t_{k+1}^{\prime}<\cdots<t_{r}<t_{r}^{\prime}
,
where
t_{i}^{\prime}:=(1+\frac{1}{\exp(i-k)\log D_{1}})t_{i}
for
i\geq k+1
,

such that

X:=\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{r}\ell_{i}~\middle|~t_{i}<\ell_{i}<t_{i}^{\prime}\text{ prime for }i\geq k+1\right\}.

The second important result is that,
if a “box” contains at lease one “good” integers,
then the equidistribution of Legendre symbols holds on it.

Proposition 4.5

(
[
Smi17
]
, Theorem 6.4 and Corollary 6.11)
.

Let
X\subset S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})
be a box, such that
it contains an element
<N
which is “good”.
Then there are positive absolute constants
c_{8},c_{12}
,
such that for any
S_{r}
-stable subset
B_{r}
of
\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma_{D},*}
,

\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\omega(n)\in B_{r}\mid n\in X\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})\right|\leq r^{-c_{12}}+D_{1}^{-c_{8}}.

The third important result is that,
to study the density of a subset of
S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})
,
one only needs to study its density restricted to each good boxes.

Proposition 4.6

(
[
Smi17
]
, Proposition 6.9)
.

Let
\delta\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\epsilon>0
.
Let
W\subset S_{r}(N,\Sigma_{D})
be a subset, whose elements
are all comfortably spaced above
D_{1}
. Suppose

{\mathbb{P}}\big(n\in W\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma_{D})\big)>1-\epsilon.

Let
V
be a subset of
S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})
.
Suppose that for any box
X\subset S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})

satisfying
X\cap W\neq\varnothing
, we have

\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(n\in V\mid n\in X\big)-\delta\right|<\epsilon.

Then

\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(n\in V\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma_{D})\big)-\delta\right|\leq C_{1}\cdot(\epsilon+(\log D_{1})^{-1})

for some absolute constant
C_{1}>0
.

Combine all the above results, we can prove the equidistribution
of Legendre symbols on
S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})
.

Proposition 4.7
.

For any
S_{r}
-stable subset
B_{r}
of
\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma_{D},*}
,

\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\omega(n)\in B_{r}\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma_{D})\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})\right|\leq C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})

for some absolute constants
C_{1}>0,c>0
.

Proof.

In Proposition
4.6
, take
W

to be all “good” integers
<N
,
take
V
to be all
n\in S_{r}(\Sigma_{D})
such that
\omega(n)\in B_{r}
.
By Proposition
4.3
, we may take
\epsilon=C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})
and
\delta={\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})
.
Then the problem is reduced to Proposition
4.5
.
∎

4.1.2.
Change the set of primes

Proposition
4.7
requires that

r\sim_{D}\log\log N
and the set of primes is
\Sigma_{D}
.
For a general
\Sigma
, we can fix a real number
D>3

such that
\Sigma\subset\Sigma_{D}
.
The main result in this section is Proposition
4.9
,
which is similar to Proposition
4.7
