but for the set
\Sigma

and requires that
r\sim\log\log N
.

First we consider the following settings.
Let
\Sigma\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
be finite sets consisting of
-1

and some primes of
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Let
q=q_{1}\cdots q_{t}
be a positive square-free integer whose prime factors
are in
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\Sigma
, with
t\geq 0
be its number of prime factors.
For each
r\geq 1
, let
\psi_{r}:\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}/S_{r}\to\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+t}/S_{r+t}

be the unique map which makes the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{~S_{r}(\Sigma^{\prime})~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{n\mapsto qn}
\textstyle{S_{r+t}(\Sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}/S_{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{r}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+t}/S_{r+t}.}

In fact, the map
\psi_{r}
comes from a map

\widetilde{\psi}_{r}:\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}\to\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+t}

(not unique) and one of the possible choice is

\big((a_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq r},(z_{i}^{(p)})_{1\leq i\leq r,p\in\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)\mapsto\big((b_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq r+t},(w_{i}^{(p)})_{1\leq i\leq r+t,p\in\Sigma}\big),

where

b_{ij}=\begin{cases}a_{ij},&\text{if }1\leq i<j\leq r,\\
z_{i}^{(q_{j-r})},&\text{if }1\leq i\leq r\text{ and }r+1\leq j\leq r+t,\\
\left[\frac{q_{j-r}}{q_{i-r}}\right],&\text{if }r+1\leq i<j\leq r+t,\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad w_{i}^{(p)}=\begin{cases}z_{i}^{(p)},&\text{if }1\leq i\leq r,\\
\left[\frac{p}{q_{i-r}}\right],&\text{if }r+1\leq i\leq r+t.\end{cases}

Let
B=(B_{r})_{r=1}^{\infty}
where

B_{r}
is a
S_{r}
-stable subset of
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r}
.
Define
B^{\prime}=(B_{r}^{\prime})_{r=1}^{\infty}

where
B_{r}^{\prime}\subset\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}

is the preimage of
B_{r+t}
under the map

\psi_{r}:\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}/S_{r}\to\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+t}/S_{r+t}
.
In particular,
B_{r}^{\prime}
is also
S_{r}
-stable, and if
n\in S_{r}(\Sigma^{\prime})
,
then
\omega(n)\in B_{r}^{\prime}
if and only if
\omega(qn)\in B_{r+t}
.

Let
P^{(r)}:={\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})

and
Q^{(r)}:={\mathbb{P}}(B_{r}^{\prime})
.
The following result tells us that the sequence

(Q^{(r)})_{r=1}^{\infty}
is very close to
(P^{(r)})_{r=1}^{\infty}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Under the above settings, for any
r\geq 1
we have

|Q^{(r)}-P^{(r+t)}|\leq 2^{\#\Sigma}\cdot(2^{t}-1)\cdot\frac{\log(r+1)+13/12}{\sqrt{r+1}}.

In particular,
\lim_{r\to\infty}Q^{(r)}=\lim_{r\to\infty}P^{(r)}

if they exist.

Note that the right hand side decreases as
r\geq 1
increases.

Proof.

By induction we only need to prove the case that
\#(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\Sigma)=1
.
There are two cases: either
q=1
or
q
is the unique element of

\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\Sigma
.

If
q=1
, then the map
\widetilde{\psi}_{r}:\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}\to\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r}

is simply

\big((a_{ij}),(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)\mapsto\big((a_{ij}),(z_{p})_{p\in\Sigma}\big)
,
which is surjective, and for any element
\omega
of
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r}
,
the size of its preimage in
\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}
is
2^{r}
.
Hence it’s clear that
Q^{(r)}=P^{(r)}
.

If
q
is the unique element of

\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\Sigma
, then the map

\widetilde{\psi}_{r}:\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}\to\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}

is
\big((a_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq r},(z_{i}^{(p)})_{1\leq i\leq r,p\in\Sigma^{\prime}}\big)\mapsto\big((b_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq r+1},(w_{i}^{(p)})_{1\leq i\leq r+1,p\in\Sigma}\big)
, where

b_{ij}=\begin{cases}a_{ij},&\text{if }1\leq i<j\leq r,\\
z_{i}^{(q)},&\text{if }1\leq i\leq r\text{ and }j=r+1,\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad w_{i}^{(p)}=\begin{cases}z_{i}^{(p)},&\text{if }1\leq i\leq r,\\
\left[\frac{p}{q}\right],&\text{if }i=r+1.\end{cases}

It’s clear that this map is injective, and whose image is the set
of
\big((b_{ij}),(w_{i}^{(p)})\big)
such that

w_{r+1}^{(p)}=\left[\frac{p}{q}\right]
for all
p\in\Sigma
.
By this way we view
\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}
as a subset of
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}
.

For a fixed
r
, we have
P^{(r+1)}=\#B_{r+1}/\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}
as well as

Q^{(r)}=\#(B_{r+1}\cap\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r})/\#\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}
.
Note that
B_{r+1}
is a union of
S_{r+1}
-orbits.
Note that
\#\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}=2^{r(r-1)/2+r\cdot(\#\Sigma+1)}

and
\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}=2^{r(r+1)/2+(r+1)\cdot\#\Sigma}=2^{\#\Sigma}\#\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}
.
In the following we estimate the difference of these two probabilities.

Write
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}=\bigsqcup_{s=0}^{r+1}\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1,s}

where
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1,s}
consists of elements

\big((b_{ij}),(w_{i}^{(p)})\big)
of
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}
such that

\#\{1\leq i\leq r+1\mid w_{i}^{(p)}=\left[\frac{p}{q}\right]\text{ for all }p\in\Sigma\}=s
.
Then
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1,s}
is also a union of
S_{r+1}
-orbits, and

\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1,s}=2^{r(r+1)/2}\binom{r+1}{s}(2^{\#\Sigma}-1)^{r+1-s}.

It’s known that (see for example
[
MU17
]
, Theorem 4.12)
for
N\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
0<p<1
and any
\delta>0

(we will choose
\delta
later), we have

(4.1)

\sum_{|n-Np|\geq N\delta}\binom{N}{n}p^{n}(1-p)^{N-n}\leq 2\exp(-2N\delta^{2}),

hence

R:=\sum_{|s-(r+1)/2^{\#\Sigma}|\geq(r+1)\delta}\frac{\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1,s}}{\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}}\leq 2\exp(-2(r+1)\delta^{2}).

On the other hand,
if
\omega
is an element of
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1,s}
,
consider the
S_{r+1}
-orbit
[\omega]
, it’s easy to see that

\frac{\#([\omega]\cap\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r})}{\#[\omega]}=\frac{s}{r+1}.

Therefore, if we
write
B_{r+1,s}:=B_{r+1}\cap\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1,s}
, then we have

\#(B_{r+1}\cap\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r})=\sum_{s=0}^{r+1}\#(B_{r+1,s}\cap\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r})=\sum_{s=0}^{r+1}\frac{s}{r+1}\#B_{r+1,s}.

Hence

\displaystyle\#(B_{r+1}\cap\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|s-(r+1)/2^{\#\Sigma}|<(r+1)\delta}\frac{s}{r+1}\#B_{r+1,s}+R\cdot\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}

\displaystyle\leq(2^{-\#\Sigma}+\delta)\cdot\#B_{r+1}+2\exp(-2(r+1)\delta^{2})\cdot\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}

as well as

\displaystyle\#(B_{r+1}\cap\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{|s-(r+1)/2^{\#\Sigma}|<(r+1)\delta}\frac{s}{r+1}\#B_{r+1,s}\geq(2^{-\#\Sigma}-\delta)\cdot(\#B_{r+1}-R\cdot\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1})

\displaystyle\geq(2^{-\#\Sigma}-\delta)\cdot\#B_{r+1}-2\exp(-2(r+1)\delta^{2})\cdot\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1},

therefore

\displaystyle|Q^{(r)}-P^{(r+1)}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\#(B_{r+1}\cap\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r})-2^{-\#\Sigma}\cdot\#B_{r+1}}{\#\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}}\right|\leq\frac{\delta\cdot\#B_{r+1}+2\exp(-2(r+1)\delta^{2})\cdot\#\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r+1}}{\#\Omega^{\Sigma^{\prime},*}_{r}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\#\Sigma}\big(\delta+2\exp(-2(r+1)\delta^{2})\big).

Taking
\delta=\log(r+1)/\sqrt{r+1}
it’s easy to obtain

|Q^{(r)}-P^{(r+1)}|\leq 2^{\#\Sigma}(\log(r+1)+13/12)/\sqrt{r+1}
.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
N
be a sufficiently large (depending on
\Sigma
and
c
below) real number,
and let
r
be a positive integer satisfying
r\sim\log\log N
.
Let
B_{r}
be any
S_{r}
-stable subset of
\Omega^{\Sigma,*}_{r}
.
Then

\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\omega(n)\in B_{r}\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})\right|\leq C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})

for some absolute constants
C_{1}>0
and
c>0
.

Proof.

Take
D>3
to be a fixed real number
such that all primes in
\Sigma
are
<D
.
When
r
is sufficiently large, we have

S_{r}(N,\Sigma)=\bigsqcup_{q}q\cdot S_{r-r(q)}(N/q,\Sigma_{D}),

where
q
runs over all positive square-free integers whose prime factors
are in
\Sigma_{D}\setminus\Sigma
.
For each such
q
, define

B_{q}^{\prime}\subset\Omega_{r-r(q)}^{\Sigma_{D},*}

to be the
B^{\prime}
as in the beginning of this section (i.e. preimage of
B_{r}

under certain map),
then for any
n\in S_{r-r(q)}(N/q,\Sigma_{D})
,

\omega(n)\in B_{q}^{\prime}
if and only if
\omega(qn)\in B_{r}
.
Note that
q
and
r(q)
have upper bounds depending only on
D
,
hence it’s easy to see that when
N
is sufficiently large (depending only on
D
),

r\sim\log\log N
implies
r-r(q)\sim_{D}\log\log(N/q)

for all above
q
.
By Lemma
4.8
it’s easy to see that

\left|{\mathbb{P}}(B_{q}^{\prime})-{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})\right|\leq 2^{D}\cdot\frac{\log(r+1)+13/12}{\sqrt{r+1}},

on the other hand, by Proposition
4.7
it’s easy to see that
(here we need to slightly decrease
c
by an absolute constant,
and taking
N
sufficiently large, depending only on
c
and
D
)

\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\omega(n)\in B_{q}^{\prime}\mid n\in S_{r-r(q)}(N/q,\Sigma_{D})\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(B_{q}^{\prime})\right|\leq C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2}),

hence

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\omega(n)\in B_{r}\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{q}\left|{\mathbb{P}}\big(\omega(n)\in B_{q}^{\prime}\mid n\in S_{r-r(q)}(N/q,\Sigma_{D})\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(B_{r})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})+2^{D}\cdot\frac{\log(r+1)+13/12}{\sqrt{r+1}}.

The last term in above inequality is

o(\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2}))
as
N\to\infty
.
Therefore the desired result holds by enlarge
C_{1}
by an absolute constant.
∎

4.1.3.
Change the number of prime divisors

The Proposition
4.9
requires that the number
r

of prime factors of
n
are close to
\log\log N
.
But we have the following result of Hardy and Ramanujan,
which implies that almost all numbers
n
satisfy this property.
From this we can finally prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proposition 4.10

(Hardy and Ramanujan,
[
Kane13
]
, Lemma 6)
.

There are some absolute constants
C_{2}>0,C_{3}>0

such that for any
r\geq 1
and any
N\geq 3
, we have

\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\leq\#S_{r}(N,\varnothing)\leq\frac{C_{3}N}{\log N}\cdot\frac{(\log\log N+C_{2})^{r-1}}{(r-1)!}.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

First let’s give an upper bound of

Q:=\sum_{r\not\sim\log\log N}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}=1-\sum_{r\sim\log\log N}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}.

It’s known that (see for example
[
MU17
]
, Theorem 5.4)
for
\lambda>0
, we have

(4.2)

\sum_{n\geq x}\frac{\lambda^{n}}{n!}\leq\left(\frac{e\lambda}{x}\right)^{x}\text{ for }x>\lambda,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{0\leq n\leq x}\frac{\lambda^{n}}{n!}\leq\left(\frac{e\lambda}{x}\right)^{x}\text{ for }0<x<\lambda.

Consider the functions

f(x)=\log\left((\frac{e\lambda}{x})^{x}\right)=x(\log\lambda+1-\log x)

and
g(x)=\lambda-(x-\lambda)^{2}/4\lambda
.
Then
f(\lambda)=g(\lambda)=\lambda
, and it’s easy to see that

f(x)\leq g(x)
when
0<x\leq 2\lambda
.
In particular, for
\lambda>1
, let
0<\alpha<1
and
0<\beta<1
be fixed, we have

(4.3)

f(\lambda\pm\lambda^{\alpha}\beta)\leq g(\lambda\pm\lambda^{\alpha}\beta)=\lambda-\lambda^{2\alpha-1}\beta^{2}/4.

Take
\lambda=\log\log N+C_{2}
where
C_{2}

is in Proposition
4.10
.
Then for sufficiently large
N
,

\log\log N-\frac{1}{4}(\log\log N)^{2/3}+2<\lambda-\lambda^{2/3}/5<\lambda+\lambda^{2/3}/5<\log\log N+\frac{1}{4}(\log\log N)^{2/3}-2.

On the other hand, there is a constant
C_{4}>0
depending only on
\Sigma
such that

\#S(N,\Sigma)>C_{4}N
for sufficiently large
N
.
Hence by Proposition
4.10

and by taking
\alpha=2/3
and
\beta=1/5
in (
4.3
),

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\not\sim\log\log N}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}\leq\frac{C_{3}}{C_{4}\log N}\sum_{r\not\sim\log\log N}\frac{\lambda^{r-1}}{(r-1)!}\leq\frac{2C_{3}}{C_{4}\log N}\exp(\lambda-\lambda^{1/3}/100)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C_{3}\exp(C_{2})}{C_{4}\exp((\log\log N)^{1/3}/100)}=o(\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2}))\quad\text{as}\quad N\to\infty.

Now we can prove Theorem
4.1
.
Denote the left hand side of Theorem
4.1
by
P
, then

P=\sum_{i\in I}\left|\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}\Big({\mathbb{P}}\big(B(n)=i\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)-P^{(\infty)}(i)\Big)\right|\leq P_{A}+P_{B},

where

P_{A}:=\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{r\sim\log\log N}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\big(B(n)=i\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)-P^{(\infty)}(i)\Big|

and

P_{B}:=\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{r\not\sim\log\log N}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}\Big({\mathbb{P}}\big(B(n)=i\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)+P^{(\infty)}(i)\Big)=2Q.

By applying Proposition
4.9

(on the subset
\{\omega\in\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}\mid B_{r}(\omega)=i\}

for each
i\in I_{r}
, and
the subset
\{\omega\in\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}\mid B_{r}(\omega)\notin I_{r}\}
)
we have

\displaystyle P_{A}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r\sim\log\log N}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}\Bigg(\sum_{i\in I_{r}}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}\big(B(n)=i\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)-P^{(r)}(i)\Big|+\sum_{i\in I_{r}}\left|P^{(r)}(i)-P^{(\infty)}(i)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\sum_{i\notin I_{r}}\Big({\mathbb{P}}\big(B(n)=i\mid n\in S_{r}(N,\Sigma)\big)+P^{(\infty)}(i)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r\sim\log\log N}\frac{\#S_{r}(N,\Sigma)}{\#S(N,\Sigma)}\Bigg((\#I_{r}+1)\cdot C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})+\sum_{i\in I_{r}}\left|P^{(r)}(i)-P^{(\infty)}(i)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\sum_{i\notin I_{r}}\left(P^{(r)}(i)+P^{(\infty)}(i)\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq I_{\epsilon}(\log\log N)\cdot C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})+R_{\epsilon}(\log\log N),

here we note that when
N
is sufficiently large (depending on
\epsilon
),

r\sim\log\log N
implies
(1-\epsilon)\log\log N<r<(1+\epsilon)\log\log N
.
On the other hand, the
Q
computed in the above is
o(\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2}))

as
N\to\infty
.
Thus the desired result holds by enlarge
C_{1}

by an absolute constant.
∎

4.2.
Natural average order and Heath-Brown’s method

The proof of the first part of Theorem
4.2
is
due to Heath-Brown
[
HB93
]
,
[
HB94
]
,
and is based on the proof of Proposition
3.15
.

Write
B=(P,Q;R^{\mathrm{T}},S)\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}
.
We may keep
a,c
unchanged and assume
that
P_{H}=(\diag(A,\cdots,A);0)
,
and that
P_{L}=0
, namely,
B
does not contain
z_{c}z_{d}^{\mathrm{T}}

(similar to the proof of Proposition
3.11
).
For each integer
n
in
S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma):=\{n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma)\mid n<N\}
,
let
r:=r(n)
be the number of prime factors of
n
,
fix an order
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}
of the prime factors of
n
,
let
V={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{cr+d}
and
W={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{ar+b}
(depending on
n
),
then
B(n)
is a well-defined matrix, and we have

{\mathbb{E}}\big(2^{\corank(B(n))}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\big)=\frac{1}{\#S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)}\sum_{n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)}\frac{1}{\#W}\sum_{v\in V,w\in W}(-1)^{w^{\mathrm{T}}B(n)v}.

Recall that we defined the map

\psi=\psi_{B,v,w}:\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,

\omega\mapsto w^{\mathrm{T}}B(\omega)v
.
It induces the map
\psi=\psi_{B,v,w}:S_{r}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,

n\mapsto w^{\mathrm{T}}B(n)v
.
Recall the map
\iota:[r]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
,
and that once
\Im(\iota),\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}

are fixed, the
\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)
depends only on
a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}

for
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
in
\Im(\iota)
,
and
z_{\lambda}^{(p)}
for
\lambda\in\Im(\iota)
and
p\in\Sigma
.
By the same reason,
\psi_{B,v,w}(n)
depends only on

a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}=\sum_{i\in[r]_{\lambda},j\in[r]_{\lambda^{\prime}}}a_{ij}=\sum_{i\in[r]_{\lambda},j\in[r]_{\lambda^{\prime}}}\left[\frac{\ell_{j}}{\ell_{i}}\right]=\left[\frac{n_{\lambda^{\prime}}}{n_{\lambda}}\right]\text{ for }\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}\text{ in }\Im(\iota),

and

z_{\lambda}^{(p)}=\sum_{i\in[r]_{\lambda}}z_{i}^{(p)}=\sum_{i\in[r]_{\lambda}}\left[\frac{p}{\ell_{i}}\right]=\left[\frac{p}{n_{\lambda}}\right]\text{ for }\lambda\in\Im(\iota)\text{ and }p\in\Sigma,

here
n_{\lambda}:=\prod_{i\in[r]_{\lambda}}\ell_{i}

so that
n=\prod_{\lambda\in\Im(\iota)}n_{\lambda}
.
We also know that, if
\Im(\iota)
is an unlinked subset,
then
\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)
depends only on

z_{\lambda}^{(p)}
for
\lambda\in\Im(\iota)
and
p\in\Sigma
,
namely
\psi_{B,v,w}
factors through

\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\twoheadrightarrow\Omega_{\Im(\iota)}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
.
Similarly, in this case
\psi_{B,v,w}(n)

depends only on
\left[\frac{p}{n_{\lambda}}\right]

for
\lambda\in\Im(\iota)
and
p\in\Sigma
.

Divide the sum over all
n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)

into the sum over intervals of each
n_{\lambda}
as in
[
HB94
]
,
we have

{\mathbb{E}}\big(2^{\corank(B(n))}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\big)=\frac{1}{\#S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)}\sum_{\mathbf{A}}S(\mathbf{A}),

where the sum runs over
\mathbf{A}=(A_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}}

with
A_{\lambda}\in 2^{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq-1}}=\{\frac{1}{2},1,2,4,\cdots\}

such that
\prod_{\lambda}A_{\lambda}<N
, and

S(\mathbf{A}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\lambda}<n_{\lambda}\leq 2A_{\lambda}\\
n=\prod_{\lambda}n_{\lambda}\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\#W}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}(-1)^{w^{\mathrm{T}}B(n)v}.

The number of different
\mathbf{A}
’s are

\leq 2^{2^{c+a}}\cdot(\log N)^{2^{c+a}}
.
The terms in
S(\mathbf{A})

satisfy
n_{\lambda}\neq 1
(namely
\lambda
is contained in the image of
\iota
)
if and only if
A_{\lambda}\geq 1
,
namely, the image of
\iota
is determined by
\mathbf{A}
.

These
\mathbf{A}
’s are divided into three types.

(1)

There are linked
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
such that

A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})

and
A_{\lambda^{\prime}}\geq 1
.
Here
\kappa>0
is some constant depending only on
B
to be chosen later.

(2)

\#\{\lambda\mid A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})\}\leq 2^{a}-1
.

(3)

The remaining part.

It turns out that the first two types of
\mathbf{A}
contribute error terms
(
[
HB94
]
, Lemma 6),
and the last type of
\mathbf{A}
contributes main term.
More precisely, for the first type of
\mathbf{A}
, we have the following.

Lemma 4.11
.

There are
\kappa>0
and
C>0
depending only on
B
such that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\text{ such that}\\
\exists\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\text{ linked s.t. }A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2}),A_{\lambda^{\prime}}\geq 1\end{subarray}}|S(\mathbf{A})|\leq C\cdot N\cdot(\log N)^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
be linked
and recall the explicit formula of
\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)
.
We have

1=\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})(\lambda_{c+\ell}+\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})\lambda_{c+\ell}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime}
,
namely, exactly one of

\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})\lambda_{c+\ell}

and
\sum_{\ell=1}^{c}(\lambda_{\ell}+\lambda_{\ell}^{\prime})\lambda_{c+\ell}^{\prime}

is
1
. Therefore, switching
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
if necessary
(switching or not only depends on
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
,
and is independent of
B,v,w
), we may write

\psi_{B,v,w}(\omega)=a_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}+\psi_{B,v,w}^{(1)}(\omega)+\psi_{B,v,w}^{(2)}(\omega)

for some
\psi_{B,v,w}^{(1)}:\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

(resp.
\psi_{B,v,w}^{(2)}:\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
)
which depends only on
a_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}

for all
\mu\neq\mu^{\prime}
in
\Im(\iota)\setminus\{\lambda^{\prime}\}

(resp.
\Im(\iota)\setminus\{\lambda\}
),
and
z_{\mu}^{(p)}
for all

\mu\in\Im(\iota)\setminus\{\lambda^{\prime}\}

(resp.
\Im(\iota)\setminus\{\lambda\}
)
and
p\in\Sigma
.
This implies that

\psi_{B,v,w}(n)=\left[\frac{n_{\lambda^{\prime}}}{n_{\lambda}}\right]+\psi_{B,v,w}^{(1)}(n)+\psi_{B,v,w}^{(2)}(n)

where
\psi_{B,v,w}^{(1)}(n)
is independent of
n_{\lambda^{\prime}}

and
\psi_{B,v,w}^{(2)}(n)
is independent of
n_{\lambda}
.
Utilizing this decomposition,
the
[
HB93
]
, Lemma 7 applies (whose proof uses

[
HB94
]
, Lemma 3 and Lemma 4), namely,

•

For any
\delta>0
,
there are
\kappa>0
and
C>0
depending only on
B
and
\delta
such that
for any linked
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
, and any
\mathbf{A}

such that
A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})
and
A_{\lambda^{\prime}}\geq 1
,
we have
|S(\mathbf{A})|\leq C\cdot N\cdot(\log N)^{-\delta}
.

Now taking
\delta=2^{c+a}+1
the desired result follows.
∎

For the second type of
\mathbf{A}
, we have the following.

Lemma 4.12
.

There are
\kappa>0
and
C>0
depending only on
B
such that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\text{ such that}\\
\#\{\lambda\mid A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})\}\leq 2^{a}-1\end{subarray}}|S(\mathbf{A})|\leq C\cdot N\cdot(\log N)^{-2^{-a}}\cdot(\log\log N)^{2^{c+1}}.

Proof.

We have (
[
HB94
]
, pp. 340–341)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\text{ such that}\\
\#\{\lambda\mid A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})\}\leq 2^{a}-1\end{subarray}}|S(\mathbf{A})|\leq 2^{d}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{\lambda}\text{ such that}\\
\#\{\lambda\mid n_{\lambda}\geq 2\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})\}\leq 2^{a}-1\\
n=\prod_{\lambda}n_{\lambda}\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2^{a\cdot\sum_{\lambda}r(n_{\lambda})}}.

For each
(n_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}}
appeared in the above sum,
let
n^{\prime}
(resp.
n^{\prime\prime}
) be the product of
n_{\lambda}

such that
n_{\lambda}
is
<
(resp.
\geq
)
2\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})
.
Then
n^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}:=(2\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2}))^{2^{c+a}}

and
n^{\prime\prime}\leq N/n^{\prime}
.
Conversely, if
n^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime\prime}
in these ranges are given,
then one can determine

(n_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}}

by determine a map which maps each prime factor of
n^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime\prime}
to
an element of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
.
One may choose a subset of
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
of size
2^{a}-1
,
for each prime factor of
n^{\prime\prime}
it maps into this subset,
and for each prime factor of
n^{\prime}

it maps to anywhere in
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
.
There are repeated and invalid choices, hence the above sum is

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}}\sum_{n^{\prime\prime}\leq N/n^{\prime}}2^{-a(r(n^{\prime})+r(n^{\prime\prime}))}\cdot\binom{2^{c+a}}{2^{a}-1}\cdot(2^{a}-1)^{r(n^{\prime\prime})}(2^{c+a})^{r(n^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\binom{2^{c+a}}{2^{a}-1}\sum_{n^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}}2^{c\cdot r(n^{\prime})}\sum_{n^{\prime\prime}\leq N/n^{\prime}}(1-2^{-a})^{r(n^{\prime\prime})}\leq O(1)\cdot\sum_{n^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}}2^{c\cdot r(n^{\prime})}\cdot N/n^{\prime}\cdot(\log N/n^{\prime})^{-2^{-a}}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\cdot N\cdot(\log N)^{-2^{-a}}\sum_{n^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}}\frac{2^{c\cdot r(n^{\prime})}}{n^{\prime}}\leq O(1)\cdot N\cdot(\log N)^{-2^{-a}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{2^{c}}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\cdot N\cdot(\log N)^{-2^{-a}}\cdot(\log\log N)^{2^{c+1}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Suppose
\mathbf{A}
is of the last type.
Namely,

•

Let
\Lambda:=\{\lambda\mid A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})\}
.
Then
\#\Lambda\geq 2^{a}
.

•

For any linked
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
,
either
A_{\lambda}<\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})

or
A_{\lambda^{\prime}}<1
.

This implies that for any
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
,
they are not linked, hence by Lemma
3.16
,
\#\Lambda=2^{a}
,

\Lambda
is a maximal unlinked subset,
so for any
\lambda^{\prime}\notin\Lambda
it must be linked
with some elements in
\Lambda
, which implies
A_{\lambda^{\prime}}<1
.
In other words, we have
\Lambda=\{\lambda\mid A_{\lambda}\geq 1\}=\Im(\iota)

which is a maximal unlinked subset.
Recall that this implies that once
\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}
are fixed,
the
\psi_{B,v,w}(n)=w^{\mathrm{T}}B(n)v

depends only on

\mathbf{z}=\left(\left[\frac{p}{n_{\lambda}}\right]\right)_{\lambda\in\Lambda,p\in\Sigma}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
.
We have

S(\mathbf{A})=2^{-b}\sum_{\mathbf{z}=(z_{\lambda}^{(p)})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}(-1)^{\psi_{B,v,w}(\mathbf{z})}\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\lambda}<n_{\lambda}\leq 2A_{\lambda},\lambda\in\Lambda\\
n=\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}n_{\lambda}\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\\
\left[\frac{p}{n_{\lambda}}\right]=z_{\lambda}^{(p)},\forall\lambda,p\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2^{a\cdot\sum_{\lambda}r(n_{\lambda})}}}_{(*)}.

The
(*)
is equal to

2^{-2^{a}\cdot\#\Sigma}\sum_{\mathbf{y}=(y_{\lambda}^{(p)})\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,*}}(-1)^{\sum_{p,\lambda}y_{\lambda}^{(p)}z_{\lambda}^{(p)}}\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\lambda}<n_{\lambda}\leq 2A_{\lambda},\lambda\in\Lambda\\
n=\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}n_{\lambda}\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\left(\frac{d_{\lambda}}{n_{\lambda}}\right)\right)\frac{1}{2^{a\cdot\sum_{\lambda}r(n_{\lambda})}}}_{(**)},

where
\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,*}:=\{(y_{\lambda}^{(p)})_{\lambda\in\Lambda,p\in\Sigma}\}\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{\#\Lambda\cdot\#\Sigma}

and
d_{\lambda}:=\prod_{p\in\Sigma}p^{y_{\lambda}^{(p)}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma,2)
.
If there are
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
such that
(y_{\lambda}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}\neq(y_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}
, namely
d_{\lambda}\neq d_{\lambda^{\prime}}
,
then
(**)
is an error term
(
[
HB94
]
, Lemma 12),
and the sum of absolute values of them is
(
[
HB94
]
, p. 348)

\leq C\cdot N\cdot(\log N)^{2^{a}-c^{\prime}\kappa^{1/2}}

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\Sigma
,
a
,
and some constant
C>0
depending only on
\Sigma
,
a
and
\kappa
.
If the
(y_{\lambda}^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}
are equal for all
\lambda
,
say all equal to
(y^{(p)})_{p\in\Sigma}
, then
(**)
is

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\lambda}<n_{\lambda}\leq 2A_{\lambda},\lambda\in\Lambda\\
n=\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}n_{\lambda}\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sum_{p}y^{(p)}s_{p}}\frac{1}{2^{a\cdot\sum_{\lambda}r(n_{\lambda})}}

and the outer
(-1)^{\sum_{p,\lambda}y_{\lambda}^{(p)}z_{\lambda}^{(p)}}

is also
(-1)^{\sum_{p}y^{(p)}s_{p}}
.
Hence the main term of
(*)
is
independent of
\mathbf{z}
,
\widetilde{v}
and
\widetilde{w}
;
sum all of them together we obtain that

S(\mathbf{A})=2^{(1-2^{a})\cdot\#\Sigma-b}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}(-1)^{\psi_{B,v,w}(\mathbf{z})}\right)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\lambda}<n_{\lambda}\leq 2A_{\lambda},\lambda\in\Lambda\\
n=\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}n_{\lambda}\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2^{a\cdot\sum_{\lambda}r(n_{\lambda})}}\right)\\
+O(N\cdot(\log N)^{2^{a}-c^{\prime}\kappa^{1/2}}).

Let
\Lambda
be fixed and let
A_{\lambda}\geq\exp(\kappa(\log\log N)^{2})

varies for
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
There are
\leq 2^{2^{a}}\cdot(\log N)^{2^{a}}
choices.
By
[
HB94
]
, p. 348,
the sum of all such
S(\mathbf{A})
is

2^{(1-2^{a})\cdot\#\Sigma-b}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}(-1)^{\psi_{B,v,w}(\mathbf{z})}\right)\left(\#S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)+O(N\cdot(\log N)^{-2^{-a}}\cdot(\log\log N)^{2})\right)\\
+O(N\cdot(\log N)^{2\cdot 2^{a}-c^{\prime}\kappa^{1/2}}).

Finally, let
\Lambda
varies, combined with the terms of the first
two types of
\mathbf{A}
,
we obtain that

{\mathbb{E}}\big(2^{\corank(B(n))}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\big)=2^{(1-2^{a})\cdot\#\Sigma-b}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda\text{ unlinked}\\
\#\Lambda=2^{a}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{\Lambda}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}\sum_{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}}(-1)^{\psi_{B,v,w}(\mathbf{z})}\\
+O((\log N)^{-2^{-a}}\cdot(\log\log N)^{2})+O((\log N)^{2\cdot 2^{a}-c^{\prime}\kappa^{1/2}})+O((\log N)^{-2^{-a}}\cdot(\log\log N)^{2^{c+1}}).

Taking
\kappa
sufficiently large, only the last error term remains.
The main term is the same as the formula (
3.19
)
of
E^{(\infty)}
in Proposition
3.15
.
Hence the first part of Theorem
4.2
holds.

4.3.
Essential average order and Kane’s method

The proof of the second part of Theorem
4.2
is
due to Kane
[
Kane13
]
,
and is also based on the proof of Proposition
3.15
.

Again let
B=(P,Q;R^{\mathrm{T}},S)\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(ak+b)\times(ck+d)}

be such that
P_{H}=(\diag(A,\cdots,A);0)

and
P_{L}=0
.
Study the property of the map

\psi=\psi_{B,v,w}:\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.
Recall that
\psi
is determined by
\iota:[r]\to{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{c+a}
,

\widetilde{v}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{d}
and
\widetilde{w}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{b}
.
Recall that
\Lambda:=\Im(\iota)
.
Define

\displaystyle m(\psi)

\displaystyle:=\#\{i\mid\text{there exists }j\neq i\text{ such that }a_{ij}\text{ or }a_{ji}\text{ occurred in the expression of }\psi(\omega)\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda\\
\text{s.t. }\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\text{ linked for some }\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\end{subarray}}\#[r]_{\lambda}=r-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda\\
\text{s.t. }\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\text{ unlinked for all }\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\end{subarray}}\#[r]_{\lambda}.

Recall that if
m(\psi)\neq 0
, then

\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}(-1)^{\psi(\omega)}=0
.
Correspondingly,
the result of Kane assert that, if
m(\psi)\neq 0
,
then
\psi
produces error term in the essential average order.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
N>30
be a real number and
r\geq 1
be an integer such that

\frac{1}{2}<\frac{r}{\log\log N}<2
.

(i) (
[
Kane13
]
, Proposition 9)

Let
0<\epsilon<1
. There exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\epsilon
and
\Sigma
,
such that for any above
\psi
with
m(\psi)>0
, we have

\left|\sum_{n\in S_{r}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)}(-1)^{\psi(n)}\right|<C\cdot N\cdot\epsilon^{m(\psi)}.

(ii) (
[
Kane13
]
, Proposition 10)

There exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\Sigma
,
such that for any above
\psi

with
m(\psi)=0
, we have

\left|\sum_{n\in S_{r}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)}(-1)^{\psi(n)}-\frac{\#S_{r}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)}{\#\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}}(-1)^{\psi(\omega)}\right|<C\cdot\frac{N\log\log\log N}{\log\log N}.

For each
0\leq m\leq r
, we estimate the number of
\psi

such that
m(\psi)=m
.

•

For
m=0
case, it means that
\Lambda
is an unlinked subset,
contained in a maximal unlinked subset,
which has
2^{c}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)
of them.
Hence the number of choices of
\psi
is

\leq 2^{c}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)\cdot 2^{b+d}\cdot(2^{a})^{r}
.

•

For
1\leq m\leq r
case, let
\Lambda_{0}:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda\mid\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\text{ unlinked for all }\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\}\subsetneqq\Lambda
.
It’s clear that for any
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{0}
,
the
\Lambda_{0}\sqcup\{\lambda^{\prime}\}
is also an unlinked subset,
hence
\#\Lambda_{0}\leq 2^{a}-1
.
There are at most
2^{a}\cdot 2^{c}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)

unlinked subsets of size
2^{a}-1
.
The
m(\psi)=m
means that there are exactly
r-m
elements
i\in[r]

such that
\iota(i)\in\Lambda_{0}
.
Therefore the number of choices of
\psi
is

\leq 2^{a}\cdot 2^{c}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{c}(2^{i}+1)\cdot 2^{b+d}\cdot\binom{r}{m}(2^{a}-1)^{r-m}(2^{c+a})^{m}
.

Sum of these
\psi
together,
applying Proposition
4.13
,
for
\frac{1}{2}<\frac{r}{\log\log N}<2
, we have

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\big(2^{\corank(B(n))}\mid n\in S_{r}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\big)-{\mathbb{E}}(2^{X_{r}})\right|\\
<\frac{C\cdot N}{\#S_{r}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)}\cdot\left(\frac{\log\log\log N}{\log\log N}+(1-2^{-a}+2^{c}\epsilon)^{r}\right),

where
C>0
is a constant depending only on
\epsilon
,
\Sigma
and
B
.
Take
\epsilon=2^{-c-a-1}
, summing over
r\sim\log\log N
,
it’s easy to obtain
the second part of Theorem
4.2
.

4.4.
Proof of main results on
2
-Selmer groups

The results of Theorem
1.2
, Theorem
1.9

and Theorem
1.6
are stated using
\Sigma
-equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}

of elliptic curves, and the condition is written as
E\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
N(E)<N
.
On the other hand,
we may represent a
\Sigma
-equivalence class
{\mathfrak{X}}

of elliptic curves with full rational
2
-torsion points
as
{\mathfrak{X}}=\{E^{(n)}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma)\}
,
where
E:y^{2}=x(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})
is a fixed elliptic curve with
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}

such that the prime divisors of
2e_{1}e_{2}(e_{1}-e_{2})
are all contained in
\Sigma
,
and
S^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma)
represents
a
\Sigma
-equivalence class of square-free integers,
defined in (
3.1
).
Then there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\Sigma

such that for any
n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(\Sigma)
the conductor
N(E^{(n)})
of
E^{(n)}

satisfies
1/C<N(E^{(n)})/n^{2}<C
.
Therefore by changing the constants in these theorems
by absolute constants, we may replace
the condition
E\in{\mathfrak{X}},N(E)<N
in these theorems
by
n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.9
.

They are consequences of
Theorem
2.2
(which relates the
2
-Selmer group to certain Rédei matrix),
Theorem
3.2
(which gives the limit distribution of such Rédei matrix),
and Theorem
4.1
(which relates limit distribution to natural distribution).
The invariance of parameter
\mathbf{t}

comes from Proposition
3.13
.
In the following we give the details.

Let
r=r(n)
be the number of prime factors of
n
;
we only consider
r\geq 1
case.
Theorem
2.2
,

2.8
,
2.9
and
Corollary
2.3

tell us that the
2
-Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
,

\pi
-strict Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
,
modified
\pi
-strict Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2,\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})
,
as well as their essential versions
S(E^{(n)})
,

S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)})
,

S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n)})
, depend only on

\omega=\omega(n)\in\Omega_{r}^{\Sigma,*}
.
More precisely, we take
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+t_{\mathfrak{X}})\times(2(k-1)+t_{\mathfrak{X}})}

be the matrix
\widetilde{B}
in Corollary
2.3

(see also Theorem
2.2
,

2.8
),
which is a “high-rank alternating Rédei matrix of level
2
”
in Definition
3.1
,
with associated submatrices
B_{j}^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+t_{\mathfrak{X}})\times((k-1)+t_{\mathfrak{X}})}

for
1\leq j\leq s
defined in §
3

(which are
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\prime}
in Corollary
2.3
),
such that
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E^{(n)})=\corank(B(\omega))

and
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{j}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(n)})=\corank(B_{j}^{\prime}(\omega))
.
On the other hand, for
1\leq j\leq s

we can take
B_{j}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+(a+2))\times((k-1)+\#\Sigma)}
,
which is the unrestricted variation of the
B_{j}
in Theorem
2.9
,
such that
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{j}{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)})=\corank(B_{j}(\omega))
.

As in Theorem
3.2
, write
I:={\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{s+1}
,
define the random variables

\displaystyle Y_{k}^{\prime}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle\to I,

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\corank(B(\omega)),(\corank(B_{j}^{\prime}(\omega)))_{1\leq j\leq s}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E^{(\omega)}),(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{j}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(\omega)}))_{1\leq j\leq s}\big),

\displaystyle Y_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle\to I,

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\corank(B(\omega)),(\corank(B_{j}(\omega)))_{1\leq j\leq s}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E^{(\omega)}),(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{j}{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(\omega)}))_{1\leq j\leq s}\big),

\displaystyle X_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\corank(B(\omega))=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E^{(\omega)}).

Let
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
be any fixed real number.
By applying (
3.4
)
of Theorem
3.2
to the Rédei matrix
B
,
we may find constants
C>0
and
0<\alpha<1
such that for all
k
,

(4.4)

\sum_{\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})\in I}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}^{\prime}=\mathbf{m})-P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(\mathbf{m})\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k}.

To compare
Y_{k}^{\prime}
with
Y_{k}
, we consider the event
F

defined in (
3.9
).
We may require the above
C
such that

{\mathbb{P}}(\text{not }F)\leq C\cdot 2^{-k}
for all
k
.
The Corollary
2.11
tells us that

{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}^{\prime}=\mathbf{m}\text{ and }F)={\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\text{ and }F)
.
Also,
S_{\pi_{j}{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(\omega)})\subset S_{\pi_{j}{\text{\rm-str}}}^{\prime}(E^{(\omega)})\subset S(E^{(\omega)})
, hence

{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}^{\prime}=\mathbf{m})={\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})=0
unless

\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})
satisfies

0\leq m_{j}^{\prime}\leq m

for
1\leq j\leq s
.
By Corollary
3.5
,
we may find a constant
\alpha>0
and require the above
C
such that

{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)<C\cdot 2^{-\alpha m^{2}}

for all
k\geq 1
,
m\geq 0
.
Therefore

(4.5)

\sum_{\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})\in I}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}^{\prime}=\mathbf{m})-{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})\right|\leq\sum_{m\geq 0}(m+1)^{s}\cdot 2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m\text{ and not }F)\\
\leq\sum_{m=0}^{M}(m+1)^{s}\cdot 2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot C\cdot 2^{-k}+\sum_{m=M+1}^{\infty}(m+1)^{s}\cdot 2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot C\cdot 2^{-\alpha m^{2}},

here
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
is to be chosen later.
In fact, we choose
M=\lfloor\frac{k}{2(1+\xi)}\rfloor
,
and only consider sufficiently large
k
, such that
(m+1)^{s+1}\leq 2^{m}

for all
m\geq M
. Then we have

(4.6)

\sum_{m=0}^{M}(m+1)^{s}\cdot 2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot C\cdot 2^{-k}\leq(M+1)^{s+1}\cdot 2^{\xi\cdot M}\cdot C\cdot 2^{-k}\leq 2^{(\xi+1)M}\cdot C\cdot 2^{-k}\leq C\cdot 2^{-k/2}.

Also, for all
m
we have

(\xi+1)m-\alpha m^{2}\leq-(\xi+1)(m-\frac{\xi+1}{\alpha})

(taking “
=
” when
m=\frac{\xi+1}{\alpha}
), so

(4.7)

\sum_{m=M+1}^{\infty}(m+1)^{s}\cdot 2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot C\cdot 2^{-\alpha m^{2}}\leq\sum_{m=M+1}^{\infty}2^{(\xi+1)m}\cdot C\cdot 2^{-\alpha m^{2}}\leq C\cdot\sum_{m=M+1}^{\infty}2^{-(\xi+1)(m-\frac{\xi+1}{\alpha})}\\
=C\cdot 2^{-(\xi+1)(M+1-\frac{\xi+1}{\alpha})}/(1-2^{-(\xi+1)})\leq C\cdot 2^{-k/2}\cdot 2^{(\xi+1)^{2}/\alpha}/(1-2^{-(\xi+1)}).

By enlarging
C
, the “sufficiently large
k
” condition in the above can be removed.
Combine (
4.4
),
(
4.5
), (
4.6
),
(
4.7
), we know that
there are constants
C>0
and
0<\alpha<1
such that for all
k
,

(4.8)

\sum_{\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})\in I}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})-P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(\mathbf{m})\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k}.

In particular,
\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})=P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(\mathbf{m})
.
Hence we may apply Theorem
4.1

(taking any
0<\epsilon<1
)
to the random variables
Y_{k}

(need to assign a junk value on

\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,*}\setminus\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}
)
and which tells us that

(4.9)

\sum_{d,d_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,d_{s}^{\prime}\geq 0}\left|{\mathbb{P}}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E^{(n)})=d\text{ and}\\
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi_{i}{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)})=d_{i}^{\prime}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq s\end{array}\middle|~n\in S_{\geq 1}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\right)-P_{r,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(d,d_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,d_{s}^{\prime})\right|\\
\leq I_{\epsilon}(\log\log N)\cdot C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})+R_{\epsilon}(\log\log N),

here for each
k
, the finite subset
I_{k}\subset I
still need to be chosen.
We choose
I_{k}:=\{(m,m_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,m_{s}^{\prime})\in I\mid 0\leq m_{j}^{\prime}\leq m\leq\log k\text{ for }1\leq j\leq s\}
.
Then
\#I_{k}\leq(1+\log k)^{3}

and
I_{\epsilon}(x)\leq 1+(1+\log(1+\epsilon)+\log x)^{3}\leq 2(\log x)^{3}
when
x
is sufficiently large.
As for
R_{\epsilon}(x)
, similar to the above argument,
we only consider sufficiently large
x
, such that
for any
k\geq(1-\epsilon)x
, any
m>\log k
, we have
(m+1)^{s}\leq 2^{m}
,
then by (
4.8
) and Corollary
3.5
,
it is easy to see that

\displaystyle R_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{(1-\epsilon)x\leq k\leq(1+\epsilon)x}\left(C\cdot\alpha^{k}+2C\cdot 2^{1/\alpha-\log k}\right)\leq C\cdot\alpha^{(1-\epsilon)x}+2C\cdot 2^{1/\alpha-\log(1-\epsilon)-\log x}.

From (
4.9
) and the upper bound of
I_{\epsilon}(x)
and
R_{\epsilon}(x)

it is easy to obtain (
1.7
) of Theorem
1.2

(need to change
C_{1}
and
c
by absolute constants).

The (
1.1
) of Theorem
1.2

and the
t_{\pi}\neq-\infty
case
(namely, corresponding to
S_{\pi_{i}{\text{\rm-str}}}
for
1\leq i\leq s
)
of Theorem
1.9

are direct consequences of (
1.7
).
Now we prove Theorem
1.9
for

t_{\pi}=-\infty
case.
In this case, we let
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+(a+2))\times((k-1)+\#\Sigma)}
,
which is the unrestricted variation of the
B_{j}
in Theorem
2.9
,
such that
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)})=\corank(B(\omega))
.
Then
B

satisfies the conditions in Proposition
3.12
,
hence if we define random variables

\displaystyle Z_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\corank(B(\omega))=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(\omega)}),

then there is a constant
C>0
such that for any
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
,

{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{k}\geq m)\leq C\cdot k\cdot 2^{-m}(3/4)^{k}
,
in particular, for any
m\geq 0
,
\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{k}=m)=P_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(m)
.
Again by Theorem
4.1
we have

(4.10)

\sum_{d\geq 0}\left|{\mathbb{P}}\left(\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}(E^{(n)})=d~\middle|~n\in S_{\geq 1}^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\right)-P_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(d)\right|\\
\leq I_{\epsilon}(\log\log N)\cdot C_{1}\cdot\exp(-c(\log\log\log N)^{1/2})+R_{\epsilon}(\log\log N).

In this case, we just take
I_{k}=\{0\}
for all
k
, hence

I_{\epsilon}(x)=2
and

R_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\max_{(1-\epsilon)x\leq k\leq(1+\epsilon)x}\left(2\cdot C\cdot k\cdot 2^{-1}(3/4)^{k}\right)\leq 2\cdot(1-\epsilon)x\cdot(3/4)^{(1-\epsilon)x}

when
x
is sufficiently large.
From (
4.10
) and the upper bound of
I_{\epsilon}(x)
and
R_{\epsilon}(x)

it is easy to obtain
t_{\pi}=-\infty
case of Theorem
1.9
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

It comes from
the (
3.4
) of Theorem
3.2
(which relates the limit of
\xi
-th moment of Rédei matrix to that of the corresponding matrix model),
Appendix
A.3
(which computes the
\xi
-th moment
of the matrix model in
\xi=1
case),
and Theorem
4.2
(which relates the limit of
\xi
-th moment
to the natural and essential
\xi
-th moment).
In the following we give the details.

We take
\widetilde{B}\in{\mathrm{RM}}^{\prime}_{(2(k-1)+t_{\mathfrak{X}})\times(2(k-1)+t_{\mathfrak{X}})}

be the unrestricted Rédei matrix
\widetilde{B}

in Corollary
2.3
,
take
B\in{\mathrm{RM}}_{(2k+t_{\mathfrak{X}})\times(2k+t_{\mathfrak{X}})}

be the restricted Rédei matrix
B

in Theorem
2.2
,
such that
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E^{(n)})+2=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}{\mathrm{Sel}}_{2}(E^{(n)}/{\mathbb{Q}})=\corank(\widetilde{B}(\omega))+2=\corank(B(\omega))
.
Define the random variables

\displaystyle X_{k}:\Omega_{k}^{\Sigma,\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\corank(\widetilde{B}(\omega))=\corank(B(\omega))-2=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}S(E^{(\omega)}).

Then by (
3.4
)
of Theorem
3.2

(or by (
4.4
)
or (
4.8
))
we know that
for any
\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}

there exists
C>0
and
0<\alpha<1
such that for all
k\geq 1
,

(4.11)

\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)-P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k}.

Apply Theorem
4.2
to the restricted
Rédei matrix
B^{\oplus\xi}=\diag(B,\cdots,B)
,
we know that the natural average order satisfies

\left|2^{2\xi}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\big(\#S(E^{(n)})^{\xi}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma)\big)-E^{(\infty)}\right|<C\cdot\frac{(\log\log N)^{2^{2\xi+1}}}{(\log N)^{1/2^{2\xi}}},

and the “essential” average order satisfies

\left|2^{2\xi}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\big(\#S(E^{(n)})^{\xi}\mid n\in S^{\mathbf{s}}(N,\Sigma),r(n)\sim\log\log N\big)-E^{(\infty)}\right|<C\cdot\frac{\log\log\log N}{(\log\log N)^{1/3}}.

Here

E^{(\infty)}=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}(2^{\xi(X_{k}+2)})=2^{2\xi}\cdot\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)=2^{2\xi}\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{{\mathrm{Alt}}}(d)2^{\xi d},

where the last equality is by (
4.11
).
Hence we obtain the two inequalities
(
1.4
) and (
1.5
)
in Theorem
1.6
.
The (
1.2
) in Theorem
1.6
is a consequence of them.
For the
\xi=1
case, by Appendix
A.3

we know that

\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{{\mathrm{Alt}}}(d)2^{\xi d}=3+\sum_{i}2^{t_{i}}
, hence it gives (
1.3
)
in Theorem
1.6
.

For the
S_{\pi{\text{\rm-str}}}
it is similar.
When
t_{\pi}\neq-\infty
, the (
4.8
)
implies that

(4.12)

\sum_{m\geq 0}2^{\xi\cdot m}\cdot\left|{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{k}=m)-P_{t_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(m)\right|<C\cdot\alpha^{k}.

When
t_{\pi}=-\infty
it also holds, similar to the argument
from (
4.4
) to (
4.8
), using
the following upper bounds:
by Corollary
3.5
and Proposition
3.12
,
we may find
C>0
and
\alpha>0
such that

{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{k}=m)<C\cdot 2^{-\alpha m^{2}}
(notice
Z_{k}\leq X_{k}
) for all

k\geq 1
,
m\geq 0
,
and such that
{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{k}\geq m)\leq C\cdot k\cdot 2^{-m}(3/4)^{k}
for all

k\geq 1
,
m\geq 1
.
The desired result comes from applying
Theorem
4.2
to the restricted
Rédei matrix
B_{j}
in Theorem
2.9
,
and repeat the above argument.
∎

Appendix A
Markov chain, model of alternating matrices

A.1.
Basic property of Markov chains

Let
I
be a countable set, usually called the state space.
Let
\Omega
be a probability space.
If
X:\Omega\to I
is a random variable, its
probability mass function
x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}
,

x_{i}={\mathbb{P}}(X=i)
is an element in the
\ell^{1}
-space

\ell^{1}(I):=\left\{x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{I}~\middle|~\|x\|=\|x\|_{1}:=\sum_{i\in I}|x_{i}|<\infty\right\},

such that
x_{i}\geq 0
and
\|x\|=1
.
Namely,
x
is contained in the following closed convex subset of
\ell_{1}(I)
:

\ell_{1}^{1}(I):=\left\{x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in\ell^{1}(I)~\middle|~x_{i}\geq 0\text{ for all }i,\text{ and }\|x\|=1\right\}.

We also define

\ell_{+}^{1}(I):={\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\cdot\ell_{1}^{1}(I)=\left\{x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in\ell^{1}(I)~\middle|~x_{i}\geq 0\text{ for all }i\right\}.

Elements in
\ell^{1}(I)
are viewed as (finite or infinite) column vectors.
If
i\in I
, let
e_{i}\in\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
whose
i
-th component is
1
,
other components are
0
.

A
transition matrix

P
is
a bounded linear operator on
\ell^{1}(I)
preserving
\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
.
When elements of
\ell^{1}(I)
are viewed as column vectors,
the
P
is viewed as a (finite or infinite) matrix
P=(P_{ij})_{i,j\in I}

such that
P_{ij}\geq 0
for all
i,j
,
and
\sum_{i\in I}P_{ij}=1
for all
j
.
The
P_{ij}
is “the transition probability from
j
to
i
”.
An element
x\in\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
is called an
equilibrium distribution
of
P

if
P(x)=x
.

Definition A.1
.

A sequence
\{X_{k}:\Omega\to I\}_{k=0}^{\infty}

of random variables is called a
Markov chain
,
if the following properties are satisfied:

•

(independent of the past)
For any
k\geq 0
and
x_{0},\cdots,x_{k+1}\in I
,

{\mathbb{P}}\big(X_{k+1}=x_{k+1}\mid X_{i}=x_{i}\text{ for all }0\leq i\leq k\big)={\mathbb{P}}\big(X_{k+1}=x_{k+1}\mid X_{k}=x_{k}\big)

if both conditional probabilities are well-defined.

•

(time homogeneous)
There exists a transition matrix

P=(P_{ij})
such that
for any
k\geq 0
and
i,j\in I
,

{\mathbb{P}}\big(X_{k+1}=i\mid X_{k}=j\big)=P_{ij}

if the conditional probability in the left hand side is well-defined.
The
P=(P_{ij})
is called the
transition matrix
of
\{X_{k}\}
.

In our application the
\Omega
is usually
an inverse limit
\varprojlim_{k}\Omega_{k}
of probability spaces,
each
\Omega_{k}
is usually finite,
and each
X_{k}
is factored through
\Omega_{k}
.

If random variables
\{X_{k}\}
form a Markov chain,
then they satisfy
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k+1}=i)=\sum_{j\in I}P_{ij}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=j)
,
namely,
their probability mass functions
\{x_{k}\}
satisfy
x_{k+1}=P(x_{k})

and hence
x_{k}=P^{k}(x_{0})
.
Therefore, to understand the behavior of
X_{k}
as
k\to\infty
,
an important aspect is to study
P^{k}(x)
as
k\to\infty

where
x\in\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
.

Inspired by the the later
results, we may introduce the following definition.

Definition A.2
.

A sequence
\{X_{k}:\Omega\to I\}_{k=0}^{\infty}

of random variables is called an
almost Markov chain
,
if the above “independent of the past” property is satisfied,
and the following property is satisfied:

•

(almost time homogeneous)
There exists a transition matrix

P=(P_{ij})
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i\in I}\left|{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k+1}=i)-\sum_{j\in I}P_{ij}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=j)\right|

converges.
The
P=(P_{ij})
is called the
limit transition matrix
of
\{X_{k}\}
.

Note that if
\{X_{k}\}
is a Markov chain, then the above infinite sum is zero.

Ergodicity and geometric ergodicity

The property of
P^{k}(x)
as
k\to\infty

is affected by the following important properties of
P
:

•

P
is called
irreducible
, if for any
i,j\in I
,
there exists
k\geq 0
such that
(P^{k})_{ij}>0
.

•

P
is called
aperiodic
, if for any
i\in I
,
there exists
N
such that
(P^{k})_{ii}>0
for all
k\geq N
.

If these two properties are satisfied, then we have the ergodicity result
of Markov chains (as well as almost Markov chains):

Theorem A.3

(see
[
Nor97
]
, Theorem 1.8.3 and
[
SD08
]
, §5)
.

Suppose
P
is irreducible and aperiodic,
and has an equilibrium distribution
x_{\infty}\in\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
.

(i)

For any
x\in\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
, we have

\|P^{n}(x)-x_{\infty}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
In particular,
P
has exactly one equilibrium distribution.

(ii)

Suppose
\{x_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
is a sequence in
\ell_{+}^{1}(I)

such that
\|x_{n}\|\to 1
as
n\to\infty
and

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\|P(x_{n})-x_{n+1}\|
converges.
Then
\|x_{n}-x_{\infty}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

The above (i) is a well-known result in the theory of Markov chains.
The (ii) follows from (i) easily.

Under some more stronger conditions,
the convergence rate of Markov chains (as well as almost Markov chains)
to the equilibrium distribution
is geometric, which is called geometric ergodicity.
In other words, it is an effective version of Theorem
A.3
.

Define

\ell_{0}(I):=\left\{x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{I}~\middle|~x_{i}=0\text{ for all but finitely many }i\right\}.

Suppose
\nu:I\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
is a map, such that
for any
C>0
,
\#\{i\in I\mid\nu(i)\leq C\}<\infty
,
equivalently, the image of
\nu
is discrete.
Then it induces a norm

\|\ \|_{\nu}:\ell_{0}(I)\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

(x_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto\sum_{i\in I}|x_{i}|\nu(i)
,
in particular
\|x_{i}\|_{\nu}=\nu(i)
.
Clearly
\|x\|_{\nu}\geq\|x\|
for all
x\in\ell_{0}(I)
.
Define

\ell_{\nu}(I):=\left\{x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{I}~\middle|~\|x\|_{\nu}:=\sum_{i\in I}|x_{i}|\nu(i)<\infty\right\},

which is “the completion of
\ell_{0}(I)
with respect to
\|\ \|_{\nu}
”.
Clearly
\ell_{0}(I)\subset\ell_{\nu}(I)\subset\ell^{1}(I)
.

Definition A.4
.

Let
P
be a transition matrix on
I
.

•

P
is called
finite
, if for any
i\in I
,

\#\{j\in I\mid P_{ij}>0\}<\infty

and
\#\{j\in I\mid P_{ji}>0\}<\infty
.

•

P
is called
extremely aperiodic
, if for any
i\in I
,

P_{ii}>0
.

•

P
is called
\nu
-driftable
, if there exists
\lambda<1
such that
\|P(e_{j})\|_{\nu}\leq\lambda\|e_{j}\|_{\nu}

for all but finitely many
j\in I
.

Theorem A.5

(see
[
KP20
]
, Theorem 2.1, Theorem 3.1, also
[
MT09
]
)
.

Suppose
P
is finite, irreducible, extremely aperiodic,
and
\nu
-driftable.

(i)

P
has a unique equilibrium distribution
x_{\infty}\in\ell_{\nu}(I)\cap\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
.
There exist
C>0
and
0<\alpha<1
depending only on
\nu
and
P
, such that
for any
x\in\ell_{\nu}(I)\cap\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
and any
n\geq 0
,

\|P^{n}(x)-x_{\infty}\|\leq\|P^{n}(x)-x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\leq C\cdot\alpha^{n}\cdot\|x\|_{\nu}.

(ii)

Suppose
\gamma\geq 1
such that

•

\nu(i)\leq\gamma\cdot\nu(j)
for all
i,j\in I
such that
P_{ij}\neq 0
.

Suppose
0<\beta<1
and
\epsilon>0
.
There exist
C_{1}>0
and
0<\alpha_{1}<1
depending only on
\beta,\gamma,\epsilon,\nu

and
P
, such that if
C_{0}>0
and

\{x_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
is a sequence in
\ell_{\nu}(I)\cap\ell_{+}^{1}(I)

satisfying that

•

\big|\|x_{n}\|-1\big|\leq C_{0}\cdot\beta^{n}
for all
n\geq 0
,

•

\|P(x_{n})-x_{n+1}\|\leq C_{0}\cdot\beta^{n}
for all
n\geq 0
,

•

\|x_{n}\|_{\nu}\leq C_{0}
and

\|x_{n}^{\nu>C}\|_{\nu}\leq C_{0}\cdot C^{-\epsilon}
for all
n\geq 0
and
C\geq 1
,
here if
x_{n}=\sum_{i\in I}x_{n,i}e_{i}
,
define
x_{n}^{\nu>C}:=\sum_{i\in I,\nu(i)>C}x_{n,i}e_{i}
,

then for any
n\geq 0
,

\|x_{n}-x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\leq C_{0}\cdot C_{1}\cdot\alpha_{1}^{n}.

Proof.

(i)
By
[
MT09
]
, Theorem 15.0.1,
the
x_{\infty}
exists,
and there exists
R>0
and
r>1
such that
for any
x\in\ell_{\nu}(I)\cap\ell_{1}^{1}(I)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^{n}\|P^{n}(x)-x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\leq R\cdot\|x\|_{\nu}.

Hence
r^{n}\|P^{n}(x)-x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\leq R\cdot\|x\|_{\nu}
for any
n\geq 0
,
which yields the desired result.

(ii)
Denote
y_{n}:=P(x_{n})-x_{n+1}
.
First we claim that there exists
C_{2}>0
and
0<\alpha_{2}<1

depending only on
\beta,\gamma,\epsilon,\nu

and
P
,
such that for any
n\geq 0
,

\|y_{n}\|_{\nu}\leq C_{0}\cdot C_{2}\cdot\alpha_{2}^{n}.

In fact, we may write

y_{n}=P(x_{n})-x_{n+1}=\Big(P(x_{n})^{\nu\leq\gamma C}-x_{n+1}^{\nu\leq\gamma C}\Big)+P(x_{n})^{\nu>\gamma C}-x_{n+1}^{\nu>\gamma C}.

We have

\displaystyle\Big\|P(x_{n})^{\nu\leq\gamma C}-x_{n+1}^{\nu\leq\gamma C}\Big\|_{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma C\cdot\Big\|P(x_{n})^{\nu\leq\gamma C}-x_{n+1}^{\nu\leq\gamma C}\Big\|\leq\gamma C\cdot\|P(x_{n})-x_{n+1}\|\leq\gamma C\cdot C_{0}\cdot\beta^{n},

\displaystyle\|P(x_{n})^{\nu>\gamma C}\|_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\|P(x_{n}^{\nu>C})^{\nu>\gamma C}\|_{\nu}\leq\|P(x_{n}^{\nu>C})\|_{\nu}\leq\|P\|_{\nu}\cdot C_{0}\cdot C^{-\epsilon},

\displaystyle\|x_{n+1}^{\nu>\gamma C}\|_{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\cdot(\gamma C)^{-\epsilon}.

In the second formula we use the fact that

P(x_{n}^{\nu\leq C})^{\nu>\gamma C}=0
.
Therefore if take
C=\beta^{-n/2}
, then

\|y_{n}\|_{\nu}\leq C_{0}\cdot\left(\gamma\cdot\beta^{n/2}+(\|P\|_{\nu}+\gamma^{-\epsilon})\cdot\beta^{n\epsilon/2}\right),

so we can take
C_{2}=\gamma+\|P\|_{\nu}+\gamma^{-\epsilon}

and
\alpha_{2}=\max\{\beta^{1/2},\beta^{\epsilon/2}\}
.

Now let
C
and
\alpha
be constants in (i) depending only on
\nu
and
P
,
fix an arbitrary integer
N\geq 1
,
for any
k\geq 0
write
k=m+n
with
m=\lfloor\frac{k}{N+1}\rfloor
,
so that
n\geq\frac{Nk}{N+1}
and
m\geq\frac{k-N}{N+1}
.
To estimate
\|x_{k}-x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}
we first note that

P^{n}(x_{m})-x_{m+n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}P^{n-1-i}(y_{m+i}).

Denote
s_{n}:=\|y_{n}\|
.
If
s_{m+i}\neq 0
then

y_{m+i}/s_{m+i}\in\ell_{\nu}(I)\cap\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
, hence by (i) it is easy to see that

\|P^{n-1-i}(y_{m+i})-s_{m+i}\cdot x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\leq C\cdot\alpha^{n-1-i}\cdot\|y_{m+i}\|_{\nu}\leq C\cdot C_{0}\cdot C_{2}\cdot\alpha^{n-1-i}\cdot\alpha_{2}^{m+i}.

This is also true for
s_{m+i}=0
. Therefore, enlarge
\alpha_{2}
such that

\alpha<\alpha_{2}<1
, then

\left\|P^{n}(x_{m})-x_{m+n}-x_{\infty}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}s_{m+i}\right\|_{\nu}\leq C\cdot C_{0}\cdot C_{2}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\alpha^{n-1-i}\cdot\alpha_{2}^{m+i}\leq C\cdot C_{0}\cdot C_{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{2}^{m+n}}{\alpha_{2}-\alpha}.

On the other hand, denote
r_{n}:=\|x_{n}\|
,
if
r_{m}\neq 0
then
x_{m}/r_{m}\in\ell_{\nu}(I)\cap\ell_{1}^{1}(I)
,
hence by (i),

\|P^{n}(x_{m})-r_{m}x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\leq C\cdot\alpha^{n}\cdot\|x_{m}\|_{\nu}\leq C\cdot C_{0}\cdot\alpha^{n}.

This is also true for
r_{m}=0
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\|x_{k}-x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot C_{0}\cdot\alpha^{n}+C\cdot C_{0}\cdot C_{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{2}^{m+n}}{\alpha_{2}-\alpha}+\|x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\cdot\left(|r_{m}-1|+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}s_{m+i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\cdot\left(C\cdot\alpha^{\frac{Nk}{N+1}}+C\cdot C_{2}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{2}^{k}}{\alpha_{2}-\alpha}+\|x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\cdot\beta^{\frac{k-N}{N+1}}\cdot\frac{2-\beta}{1-\beta}\right),

so we can take
C_{1}=C+\frac{C\cdot C_{2}}{\alpha_{2}-\alpha}+\|x_{\infty}\|_{\nu}\cdot\frac{2-\beta}{1-\beta}\cdot\beta^{-\frac{N}{N+1}}

and
\alpha_{1}=\max\{\alpha^{\frac{N}{N+1}},\alpha_{2},\beta^{\frac{1}{N+1}}\}
.
∎

A.2.
Models of matrices and corresponding Markov chains

To compute

P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m):=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B)=m\mid B\in M_{2k+t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\big)

in Theorem
1.2
,
a key ingredient is the corresponding Markov chain.

We consider the following slightly generalized model
M_{dk+t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
.
Let
d\geq 2
be an even integer
(it is
d=2
in Theorem
1.2
),
0\leq s\leq d
be an integer,

t
be an integer (the model only depends
on the residue class of
t
modulo
d
),

\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{s})
be an unordered tuple of
s
integers, such that

t_{1}\equiv\cdots\equiv t_{s}\equiv-t\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,d\right)
.
In the case that
s=d
we further require that

\sum_{i=1}^{s}t_{i}\leq 0
.
Denote by
\Omega_{k}=M_{dk+t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
the set of all
B\in M_{dk+t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
of form

B=\begin{pmatrix}0&B_{12}&\cdots&B_{1,s+1}\\
B_{21}&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&0&B_{s,s+1}\\
B_{s+1,1}&\cdots&B_{s+1,s}&B_{s+1,s+1}\end{pmatrix}

such that for
1\leq i\leq s
,
1\leq j\leq s
,
the
B_{ij}
is of size
(k+\frac{t_{i}+t}{d})\times(k+\frac{t_{j}+t}{d})
.
Endow
\Omega_{k}
with normalized counting measure.
Define the projection map
\Omega_{k+1}\twoheadrightarrow\Omega_{k}
,

B_{\mathrm{new}}=(B_{{\mathrm{new}},ij})\mapsto B=(B_{ij})

where each
B_{ij}
is the top-left corner of
B_{{\mathrm{new}},ij}
.
This allows us to define the probability space
\Omega=\varprojlim_{k}\Omega_{k}
.
Consider the random variables on
\Omega
.
For example, for
X_{k}:\Omega_{k}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
B\mapsto\corank(B)
,
then
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m)
.
When
s\neq 0
the
\{X_{k}\}
do not form a Markov chain.
To make them Markovian, the state space should be refined.

Theorem A.6
.

For an element
B=(B_{ij})
of
\Omega_{k}
,
denote
B_{j}^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1j}\\
\vdots\\
B_{s+1,j}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for
1\leq j\leq s
.
Then random variables

Y_{k}:\Omega_{k}\to I:={\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{s+1},\qquad B\mapsto\big(\corank(B),\corank(B_{1}^{\prime}),\cdots,\corank(B_{s}^{\prime})\big)

form an almost Markov chain.
For
d=2
or
s=0
, they actually form a Markov chain,
whose transition probabilities are listed in
§
A.2.1
,
§
A.2.2

and §
A.2.3
.

Proof.

Consider
B_{\mathrm{new}}=(B_{{\mathrm{new}},ij})\in\Omega_{k+1}
.
It is a matrix
B=(B_{ij})\in\Omega_{k}
with newly added
d
rows and columns.
To show
\{Y_{k}\}
form a Markov chain,
we only need to show that for any elements
\mathbf{m}

and
\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}
of
I
,
for any fixed
k
and fixed
B\in\Omega_{k}

such that
Y_{k}(B)=\mathbf{m}
, the conditional probability

(A.1)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbb{P}}\big(Y_{k+1}(B_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\mid B_{\mathrm{new}}\mapsto B\big)\text{ only depends on $\mathbf{m}$, $\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}$,}\\
\text{and is independent of $B$ and $k$.}\end{array}

Here
B_{\mathrm{new}}\mapsto B
means that
under the natural projection map
\Omega_{k+1}\twoheadrightarrow\Omega_{k}
,
the image of
B_{\mathrm{new}}
is
B
.
This implies that

{\mathbb{P}}\big(Y_{k+1}=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)
is also equal to the above conditional probability.

We may divide the process
B\to B_{\mathrm{new}}
into
d
steps

B=B^{(0)}\to B^{(1)}\to\cdots\to B^{(d)}=B_{\mathrm{new}},

each
B^{(\ell)}\in M_{dk+t+\ell}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
is alternating,
and is
B^{(\ell-1)}
with a new row and column added.
To describe this more precisely,
write
B^{(\ell)}=(B_{ij}^{(\ell)})
,
then
B^{(\ell)}\to B^{(\ell+1)}

is adding a row to
B_{rj}^{(\ell)}
for all
j
,
and adding a column to
B_{ir}^{(\ell)}
for all
i
,
and leaving other submatrices unchanged,
here
r:=\min\{\ell+1,s+1\}
.
In other words, we have

\Omega_{k}=\Omega_{k}^{(0)}\twoheadleftarrow\Omega_{k}^{(1)}\twoheadleftarrow\cdots\twoheadleftarrow\Omega_{k}^{(d)}=\Omega_{k+1},

each
B^{(\ell)}
is an element of
\Omega_{k}^{(\ell)}
,
and
\Omega_{k}^{(\ell+1)}=\Omega_{k}^{(\ell)}\times\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}
,
the space
\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}
consists of all possible
added rows and columns in the step
B^{(\ell)}\to B^{(\ell+1)}
.
Moreover,
consider the natural decomposition

\Omega_{k}=\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq s+1}\Omega_{k,ij}

where
\Omega_{k,ii}:=0\subset M_{k_{i}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
for
1\leq i\leq s
,

\Omega_{k,s+1,s+1}:=M_{k_{s+1}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
,
and
\Omega_{k,ij}:=M_{k_{i}\times k_{j}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
for
1\leq i<j\leq s+1
,
which makes
B_{ij}=B_{ji}^{\mathrm{T}}\in\Omega_{k,ij}

for
1\leq i\leq j\leq s+1
,
then we have

\Omega_{k,ij}=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(0)}\twoheadleftarrow\Omega_{k,ij}^{(1)}\twoheadleftarrow\cdots\twoheadleftarrow\Omega_{k,ij}^{(d)}=\Omega_{k+1,ij},

each
B_{ij}^{(\ell)}
is an element of
\Omega_{k,ij}^{(\ell)}
,
and
\Omega_{k,ij}^{(\ell+1)}=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(\ell)}\times\Omega_{k,ij,\Delta}^{(\ell)}
,
the space
\Omega_{k,ij,\Delta}^{(\ell)}
consists of all possible
added rows and columns in the step
B_{ij}^{(\ell)}\to B_{ij}^{(\ell+1)}
.
The precise definition of
\Omega_{k,ij}^{(\ell)}

as well as the decomposition is as follows:
for
1\leq i\leq s
,

\begin{cases}\Omega_{k,ii}^{(0)}=\Omega_{k,ii}^{(1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ii}^{(i-1)}=0\subset M_{k_{i}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}),\\
\Omega_{k,ii}^{(i)}=\Omega_{k,ii}^{(i+1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ii}^{(d)}=0\subset M_{k_{i}+1}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}),\end{cases}

for
i=s+1
,

\begin{cases}\Omega_{k,ii}^{(0)}=\Omega_{k,ii}^{(1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ii}^{(s)}=M_{k_{i}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}),\\
\Omega_{k,ii}^{(\ell+1)}=M_{k_{i}+\ell+1-s}^{\mathrm{Alt}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})=\Omega_{k,ii}^{(\ell)}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k_{i}+\ell-s},&\text{if }s\leq\ell\leq d-1,\end{cases}

for
1\leq i<j\leq s
,

\begin{cases}\Omega_{k,ij}^{(0)}=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i-1)}=M_{k_{i}\times k_{j}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}),\\
\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i)}=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i+1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(j-1)}=M_{(k_{i}+1)\times k_{j}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i-1)}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k_{j}},\\
\Omega_{k,ij}^{(j)}=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(j+1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(d)}=M_{(k_{i}+1)\times(k_{j}+1)}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(j-1)}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k_{i}+1},\end{cases}

and for
1\leq i<j=s+1
,

\begin{cases}\Omega_{k,ij}^{(0)}=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i-1)}=M_{k_{i}\times k_{j}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2}),\\
\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i)}=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i+1)}=\cdots=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(s)}=M_{(k_{i}+1)\times k_{j}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(i-1)}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k_{j}},\\
\Omega_{k,ij}^{(\ell+1)}=M_{(k_{i}+1)\times(k_{j}+\ell+1-s)}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})=\Omega_{k,ij}^{(\ell)}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k_{i}+1},&\text{if }s\leq\ell\leq d-1.\end{cases}

Similar to
B_{j}^{\prime}
, we need to consider

B_{j}^{(\ell)}:=(B_{ij}^{(\ell)})_{1\leq i\leq s+1}

for
1\leq j\leq s
.
Then
B_{j}^{(\ell)}\in M_{(dk+t+\ell)\times k_{j}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
for
0\leq\ell\leq j-1
,
and
B_{j}^{(\ell)}\in M_{(dk+t+\ell)\times(k_{j}+1)}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})
for
j\leq\ell\leq d
.
For simplicity of notation, write

Y_{k}^{(\ell)}:\Omega_{k}^{(\ell)}\to I:={\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{s+1},\quad B^{(\ell)}\mapsto\big(\corank(B^{(\ell)}),\corank(B_{1}^{(\ell)}),\cdots,\corank(B_{s}^{(\ell)})\big).

To show (
A.1
), we only need to show that
each step
B^{(\ell)}\to B^{(\ell+1)}
is independent of previous steps,
and is independent of
k
. More precisely,
we only need to show that for any elements
\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)}

and
\mathbf{m}^{(\ell+1)}
of
I
,
for any fixed
k
and fixed
B^{(\ell)}\in\Omega_{k}^{(\ell)}

such that
Y_{k}^{(\ell)}(B^{(\ell)})=\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)}
, the conditional probability

(A.2)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbb{P}}\big(Y_{k}^{(\ell+1)}(B^{(\ell+1)})=\mathbf{m}^{(\ell+1)}\mid B^{(\ell+1)}\mapsto B^{(\ell)}\big)\text{ only depends on $\ell$, $\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)}$, $\mathbf{m}^{(\ell+1)}$,}\\
\text{and is independent of $B^{(\ell)}$ and $k$.}\end{array}

In the following the condition in (
A.2
)
will be abbreviated as
\cdots
.
Write
\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)}=(m^{(\ell)},m_{1}^{(\ell)},\cdots,m_{s}^{(\ell)})
.
Then
\rank(B^{(\ell+1)})-\rank(B^{(\ell)})\in\{0,2\}

and
\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell+1)})-\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell)})\in\{0,1\}

(note that
B_{jj}^{(\ell)}=0
for all
\ell
), so

m^{(\ell+1)}-m^{(\ell)}\in\{\pm 1\}
,

m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}-m_{j}^{(\ell)}\in\{0,-1\}
for
\ell\neq j-1
,
and
m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}-m_{j}^{(\ell)}\in\{0,1\}
for
\ell=j-1
.
This means that each
m^{(\ell)}\to m^{(\ell+1)}

and
m_{j}^{(\ell)}\to m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}
has
2
possibilities,
and
\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)}\to\mathbf{m}^{(\ell+1)}
has
2^{s+1}

possibilities.
Therefore to compute (
A.2
)
is equivalent to compute

(A.3)

P_{S}^{(\ell)}:={\mathbb{P}}\big(m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}=m_{j}^{(\ell)}+\delta_{j,\ell+1}\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)

and

(A.4)

P_{\{0\}\cup S}^{(\ell)}:={\mathbb{P}}\big(m^{(\ell+1)}=m^{(\ell)}+1\text{ and }m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}=m_{j}^{(\ell)}+\delta_{j,\ell+1}\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)

for every subsets
S
of
\{1,2,\cdots,s\}
.
Here
\delta_{j,\ell+1}
is the Kronecker delta for
j
and
\ell+1
.

Let
v\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{dk+t+\ell}
.
If
0\leq\ell\leq s-1
,
write
v=(v_{1};\cdots;v_{s+1})

with
v_{i}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k_{i}+1}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell

and
v_{i}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{k_{i}}
for
\ell+1\leq i\leq s+1
,
such that
v_{\ell+1}=0
and
v_{i}
varies arbitrarily for
i\neq\ell+1
.
If
s\leq\ell\leq d-1
, let
v
varies arbitrarily.
Then the space
\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}
is just all the possible
v
,
and
B^{(\ell+1)}
is
B^{(\ell)}
with newly added column
v

and newly added row
v^{\mathrm{T}}
.

Let’s compute (
A.3
).
We have

P_{S}^{(\ell)}={\mathbb{P}}\big(m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}=m_{j}^{(\ell)}+\delta_{j,\ell+1}\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)={\mathbb{P}}\big(\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell+1)})=\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell)})\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big).

If
\ell+1\notin S
, then

\displaystyle P_{S}^{(\ell)}={\mathbb{P}}\big(m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}=m_{j}^{(\ell)}\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell+1)})=\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell)})\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(v_{j}\in\operatorname{span}((B_{j}^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}})\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)=2^{-\sum_{j\in S}m_{j}^{(\ell)}}.

If
\ell+1\in S
, write
S=\{\ell+1\}\sqcup S^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle P_{S}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell+1)}=m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)}+1\text{ and }m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}=m_{j}^{(\ell)}\text{ for all }j\in S^{\prime}\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell+1)})=\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell)})\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(v\in\operatorname{span}(B_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)})\text{ and }v_{j}\in\operatorname{span}((B_{j}^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}})\text{ for all }j\in S^{\prime}\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}{\mathbb{P}}\big(v\in\operatorname{span}(B_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)})\mid\cdots\big)=P_{\{\ell+1\}}^{(\ell)}=2^{2k_{\ell+1}-dk-t-\ell-m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)}}=2^{(2-d)k+2(t_{\ell+1}+t)/d-t-\ell-m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)}}.

Here
(*)
holds since if

v\in\operatorname{span}(B_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)})
,
then for any
j\in S^{\prime}
we have

v_{j}\in\operatorname{span}(B_{j,\ell+1}^{(\ell)})=\operatorname{span}((B_{\ell+1,j}^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}})\subset\operatorname{span}((B_{j}^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}})
.
In particular, we know that the (
A.3
)
only depends on
\ell
,
m_{j}^{(\ell)}
and
m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}

for
j\in S
,
and is independent of
m^{(\ell)}
,
m^{(\ell+1)}
,

m_{j}^{(\ell)}
and
m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}

for
j\notin S
,
B^{(\ell)}
and
k

(at least for
d=2
).

Let’s compute (
A.4
).
First consider
S=\varnothing
case.
We have
m^{(\ell+1)}-m^{(\ell)}\in\{\pm 1\}
and

P_{\{0\}}^{(\ell)}={\mathbb{P}}\big(m^{(\ell+1)}=m^{(\ell)}+1\mid\cdots\big)={\mathbb{P}}\big(\rank(B^{(\ell+1)})=\rank(B^{(\ell)})\mid\cdots\big)={\mathbb{P}}\big(v\in\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})\mid\cdots\big).

Hence it’s clear that if
s\leq\ell\leq d-1
, then

P_{\{0\}}^{(\ell)}={\mathbb{P}}\big(m^{(\ell+1)}=m^{(\ell)}+1\mid\cdots\big)=2^{-m^{(\ell)}}.

If
0\leq\ell\leq s-1
, then

P_{\{0\}}^{(\ell)}={\mathbb{P}}\big(m^{(\ell+1)}=m^{(\ell)}+1\mid\cdots\big)=\frac{\#\big(\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}\cap\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})\big)}{\#\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}}=\frac{\#\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})}{\#\big(\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})\big)},

and

\displaystyle\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}+\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc|ccccccc}0&B_{12}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&B_{1,s+1}^{(\ell)}&I\\
B_{21}^{(\ell)}&\ddots&\ddots&&&&\vdots&&\ddots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&&\vdots&&&I\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&\vdots&&&&0\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&&&&&I\\
\vdots&&&&\ddots&0&B_{s,s+1}^{(\ell)}&&&&&&\ddots\\
B_{s+1,1}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&B_{s+1,s}^{(\ell)}&B_{s+1,s+1}^{(\ell)}&&&&&&&I\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc|ccccccc}0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&I\\
\vdots&&&&&&\vdots&&\ddots\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&&&I\\
B_{\ell+1,1}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&B_{\ell+1,\ell}^{(\ell)}&0&B_{\ell+1,\ell+2}^{(\ell)}&\cdots&B_{\ell+1,s+1}^{(\ell)}&&&&0\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&&&&&I\\
\vdots&&&&&&\vdots&&&&&&\ddots\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&&&&&&&I\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{span}(B_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)})\oplus\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)},

hence

P_{\{0\}}^{(\ell)}={\mathbb{P}}\big(m^{(\ell+1)}=m^{(\ell)}+1\mid\cdots\big)=\frac{\#\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})}{\#\operatorname{span}(B_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)})\cdot\#\Omega_{k,\Delta}^{(\ell)}}=2^{m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)}-m^{(\ell)}}.

Now let
S
be any subset of
\{1,2,\cdots,s\}

and we want to compute

\displaystyle P_{\{0\}\cup S}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(m^{(\ell+1)}=m^{(\ell)}+1\text{ and }m_{j}^{(\ell+1)}=m_{j}^{(\ell)}+\delta_{j,\ell+1}\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(\rank(B^{(\ell+1)})=\rank(B^{(\ell)})\text{ and }\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell+1)})=\rank(B_{j}^{(\ell)})\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big).

Similar to the computation of (
A.3
), if
\ell+1\in S
then we have

P_{\{0\}\cup S}^{(\ell)}={\mathbb{P}}\big(v\in\operatorname{span}(B_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)})\mid\cdots\big)=P_{\{\ell+1\}}^{(\ell)}=2^{2k_{\ell+1}-dk-t-\ell-m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)}}=2^{(2-d)k+2(t_{\ell+1}+t)/d-t-\ell-m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)}}.

If
\ell+1\notin S
, then

\displaystyle P_{\{0\}\cup S}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(v\in\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})\text{ and }v_{j}\in\operatorname{span}((B_{j}^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}})\text{ for all }j\in S\mid\cdots\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}{\mathbb{P}}\big(v\in\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})\mid\cdots\big)=P_{\{0\}}^{(\ell)}=\begin{cases}2^{-m^{(\ell)}},&\text{if }s\leq\ell\leq d-1,\\
2^{m_{\ell+1}^{(\ell)}-m^{(\ell)}},&\text{if }0\leq\ell\leq s-1.\end{cases}

Here
(*)
holds since if

v\in\operatorname{span}(B^{(\ell)})
,
then for any
j\in S
we have

v_{j}\in\operatorname{span}(B_{j1}^{(\ell)},\cdots,B_{j,s+1}^{(\ell)})=\operatorname{span}((B_{j}^{(\ell)})^{\mathrm{T}})
.
∎

Remark A.7
.

When
d\geq 4
it turns out that
there exists
C>0
such that

{\mathbb{P}}\big(\corank(B_{j}^{\prime})=0\big)\geq 1-C\cdot 2^{-k}

for all
1\leq j\leq s
and all
k\geq 0
,
and not only
\{Y_{k}\}
, but also
\{X_{k}\}
form an almost Markov chain.

Later we only consider
d=2
cases of Theorem
A.6

which are models used in
Theorem
1.2
.
There are three models: type (A) model which is
s=0
,
type (B) model which is
s=1
,
and type (C) model which is
s=2
.
We will write down the transition probability and limit distribution
of them explicitly.

Denote
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(\mathbf{m}):=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{k}=\mathbf{m})
.
Once we know that
\{Y_{k}\}
form a Markov chain,
when
t
and
\mathbf{t}
are fixed,
denote the transition probability
{\mathbb{P}}\big(Y_{k+1}=\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\mid Y_{k}=\mathbf{m}\big)
by
P_{\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}

which is independent of
k
.
It’s easy to see that such transition probality is
0
unless

\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}-\mathbf{m}\in\{0,\pm 2\}\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}\{0,\pm 1\}
,
hence when
s=0
,
1
and
2
there are
3
,
9
and
27

transition probabilities, respectively.

A.2.1.
Type (A)

The property of type (A) model was studied in, for example,

[
HB94
]
,
[
SD08
]
,
[
Kane13
]
,
[
KMR13
]
,
[
Smi16
]
.
The transition probability

P_{m\to m_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
is
listed in the following table,
here for the simplicity of notation, denote
x:=2^{-m}
.

m_{\mathrm{new}}

P_{m\to m_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}

m+2

x^{2}/2

m

x(3-5x/2)

m-2

(1-x)(1-2x)

Note that we must have
m\equiv t\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)
,
and when we only consider such
m
, it’s easy to see that the
transition matrix
P_{m\to m_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}

is irreducible and aperiodic,
hence by Theorem
A.3
we know that
the
P_{t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
must be the unique
equilibrium state of
P_{m\to m_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
.

Proposition A.8

(see
[
HB94
]
,
[
SD08
]
,
[
Kane13
]
,
[
KMR13
]
,
[
Smi16
]
)
.

We have

P_{t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{2}{2^{i}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1+2^{-i})^{-1},&\text{if }m\equiv t\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right),\\
0,&\text{if }m\not\equiv t\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right).\end{cases}

Corollary A.9

(see
[
Kane13
]
, §5 and
[
HB94
]
, Theorem 1)
.

Define the formal power series

F_{0}(X):=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+2^{-j}X)}{\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+2^{-j})}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[X]]

and
F(X):=F_{0}(X)+(-1)^{t}F_{0}(-X)\in{\mathbb{R}}[[X]]
.
Then
F(X)
is the generating function of
P_{t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
:

\sum_{m}P_{t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)X^{m}=F(X).

It converges on the whole complex plane.
In particular, for
\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
the
\xi
-th moment of the size of
\ker(B)
as
B\in\Omega_{k}
,
k\to\infty
is

\sum_{m}P_{t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)2^{\xi\cdot m}=F(2^{\xi})=F_{0}(2^{\xi})=\prod_{j=1}^{\xi}(1+2^{j})=\prod_{j=1}^{\xi}\frac{2^{2j}-1}{2^{j}-1},

which is independent of
t
,
and taking
\xi=1
, the average size of
\ker(B)
as
B\in\Omega_{k}
,
k\to\infty
is
3
.

A.2.2.
Type (B)

In this case
the transition probability

P_{\mathbf{m}\to\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}

is as follows.
It is zero unless listed in the table,
here for the simplicity of notation, denote

\tau:=2^{t_{1}}
,

x:=2^{m_{1}^{\prime}-m}
,
y:=2^{-m_{1}^{\prime}}
,
so that
xy=2^{-m}
.

m^{\prime}_{1,{\mathrm{new}}}

m_{\mathrm{new}}

m-2

m

m+2

*

m_{1}^{\prime}+1

0

\tau(1-x)y^{2}/2

\tau xy^{2}/2

\tau y^{2}/2

m_{1}^{\prime}

y(1-x)(1-2x)

y(\tau-3\tau y/2+3x-5x^{2}/2+\tau xy/2)

xy(x-\tau y)/2

y(1+\tau-3\tau y/2)

m_{1}^{\prime}-1

(1-x)(1-y)

(x-\tau y)(1-y)

0

(1-y)(1-\tau y)

*

(1-x)(1-2xy)

x(2y+1-5xy/2)

x^{2}y/2

1

Let
I
be the set of
\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime})
satisfying
0\leq m_{1}^{\prime}\leq m\leq 2m_{1}^{\prime}-t_{1}

and
m_{1}^{\prime}\geq\max\{t_{1},0\}

and
m\equiv t\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)
.
It’s easy to check that if
\mathbf{m}\in I
, then all the entries in the above table
are non-negative, moreover, the entry is non-zero
if and only if
\mathbf{m}_{\mathrm{new}}\in I

and is not equal to
(m+2,m_{1}^{\prime}-1)
and
(m-2,m_{1}^{\prime}+1)
.
Hence when we only consider such
\mathbf{m}
, it’s easy to see that the obtaining
Markov chain is irreducible and aperiodic,
by Theorem
A.3

we know that the
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}

must be the unique equilibrium distribution.

Note that the
P_{(m,m_{1}^{\prime})\to(*,m_{1,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime})}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
only depends on
m_{1}^{\prime}
,
and in fact it’s clear that

P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(*,m_{1}^{\prime})=P_{t_{1}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(m_{1}^{\prime}):=\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\corank(B)=m_{1}^{\prime}\mid B\in M_{(k-t_{1})\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})\right).

From
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(*,m_{1}^{\prime})
we construct

(P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m,m_{1}^{\prime}))_{\mathbf{m}=(m,m_{1}^{\prime})\in I}
as follows.
It’s straightforward to check it is indeed an equilibrium distribution,
which is automatically unique.

Proposition A.10
.

For
\ell\geq 0
even and
m_{1}^{\prime}\geq\max\{t_{1}+\ell,0\}
, the following formula

P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(2m_{1}^{\prime}-t_{1}-\ell,m_{1}^{\prime})=P_{t_{1}}^{\mathrm{Mat}}(m_{1}^{\prime})\cdot 2^{-(m_{1}^{\prime}-t_{1})(m_{1}^{\prime}-t_{1}-1)/2}\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(2^{m_{1}^{\prime}-t_{1}-i}-1)\prod_{i=1}^{\ell/2}\frac{4^{i-1}}{4^{i}-1}

gives an equilibrium distribution.

For
m\geq\max\{t_{1},0\}
such that
m\equiv t\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)
,
the
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)
is the sum of

P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m,m_{1}^{\prime})
as
m_{1}^{\prime}
varies:

P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)=\sum_{m_{1}^{\prime}=\max\{(m+t_{1})/2,0\}}^{m}P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m,m_{1}^{\prime}).

For other
m
, we have
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)=0
, in fact,
in this case there is no
B\in\Omega_{k}
such that
\corank(B)=m
.
As a special case, if
t_{1}\leq 0
is even, then
the probability of
\corank(B)
being minimal is

P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(0)=P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(0,0)=\prod_{i=1}^{-t_{1}/2}(1-4^{-i})^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-2^{-i}).

Clearly it is different from the type (A) in Proposition
A.8
,
for example, the average size of
\ker(B)
for these two types are different.

Corollary A.11
.

Define the formal power series

F_{0}(X):=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+2^{-j}X)}{\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+2^{-j})}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}2^{it_{1}}\prod_{j=1}^{i}\frac{2^{1-j}X-1}{2^{2j}-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}[[X]]

and
F(X):=F_{0}(X)+(-1)^{t}F_{0}(-X)\in{\mathbb{R}}[[X]]
,
Then
F(X)
is the generating function of
P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}
:

\sum_{m}P_{t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)X^{m}=F(X).

It converges on the whole complex plane.
In particular, for
\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
the
\xi
-th moment of the size of
\ker(B)
as
B\in\Omega_{k}
,
k\to\infty
is

\sum_{m}P_{\infty;t,\mathbf{t}}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)2^{\xi\cdot m}=F(2^{\xi})=F_{0}(2^{\xi})=\sum_{i=0}^{\xi}2^{it_{1}}\prod_{j=1}^{\xi-i}\frac{2^{2(i+j)}-1}{2^{j}-1}.

Taking
\xi=1
, the average size of
\ker(B)
as
B\in\Omega_{k}
,
k\to\infty
is
3+2^{t_{1}}
.

But this result suggests that type (A) is the limit of type (B)
as
t_{1}\to-\infty
. In fact we have the following result.

Corollary A.12
.

For any fixed
m\geq 0
, let
t_{1}:=t-\rho
varies, then we have the limit

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1}\to-\infty\\
t_{1}\equiv m\,\left(\mathrm{mod}\,2\right)\end{subarray}}P_{t,(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{2}{2^{i}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1+2^{-i})^{-1},

which equals the type (A)
P_{t}^{\mathrm{Alt}}(m)

in Proposition
A.8
.

A.2.3.
Type (C)

Denote
\tau_{i}:=2^{t_{i}}
,

y_{i}:=2^{-m_{i}^{\prime}}
.
Then for
P_{(*,m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})\to(*,m_{1,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime},m_{2,{\mathrm{new}}}^{\prime})}^{\mathrm{Alt}}

we have the following table.

0

\tau_{2}y_{2}(y_{2}-\tau_{1}y_{1})/2

\tau_{1}\tau_{2}y_{1}y_{2}/2

(1-y_{1})(y_{2}-\tau_{1}y_{1})

\begin{array}[]{c}\tau_{1}y_{1}+\tau_{2}y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2}+\tau_{1}\tau_{2}y_{1}y_{2}/2\\

Since
m_{1}^{\prime}-m_{2}^{\prime}\geq t_{1}
and
m_{2}^{\prime}-m_{1}^{\prime}\geq t_{2}
,
we have
y_{2}-\tau_{1}y_{1}\geq 0
and
